# Baldr API

Generated by `./build.sh api` from the doc comments in `baldr/`; edit the comments, not this file. The first part is what apps call, in reading order. The internals at the end are public only because Odin has no finer privacy — from RC1 the first part does not break.

## app.odin

The run loop: platform events → dispatch → update → view → layout → paint → render, waking only when something happens. Frames are paced by the platform (compositor frame callback / vsync), never by a timer loop.

### `App`

```odin
App :: struct($State, $Msg: typeid) {
	title:  string,
	app_id: string,   // reverse-DNS-ish id for the desktop ("" → "baldr.app")
	// icon is every window's icon on Windows and X11, read once at start (the
	// app may free it after `run` returns). Wayland takes the icon from the
	// `.desktop` file named like `app_id`, and macOS from the app bundle. On
	// Windows an icon compiled into the program (`odin build -resource:app.rc`,
	// resource id 1) is used when this is nil.
	icon:   ^Image,
	size:   [2]f32,   // logical; zero → 800×600
	// maximized asks the desktop to open the window maximised — the other
	// half of coming back the way it was left, with `size`. Like
	// `fullscreen` it is a request: `Window_State.maximized` (or
	// `ui.surface.maximized`) is what actually happened. This is
	// `Surface_Desc.maximized` for an app that declares no `surfaces`.
	maximized: bool,
	// How small and how large the desktop may let the window be dragged,
	// in logical pixels; zero on an axis is no limit. `Surface_Desc` has
	// the same pair and the same meaning — these are for an app that
	// declares no `surfaces`, which is most of them.
	min_size: [2]f32,
	max_size: [2]f32,
	clear:  Color,    // window background; zero → theme bg
	theme:  Theme,    // zero (name == "") → follow the system light/dark setting; named → every surface starts pinned to it, as set_theme pins, and follow_system_theme still reaches the desktop's
	// accent is the app's own colour to generate the theme from, for an app
	// with a brand that still belongs on the desktop it runs on: the theme
	// is theme_from_seed(accent, …) built again for the desktop's light or
	// dark and its contrast setting, at start and whenever they change.
	// Zero is Baldr's own blue. `theme` set names a theme outright and wins:
	// that app follows nothing.
	accent: Color,
	// adjust_theme is the app's last word on the theme it runs with, called
	// on every one the runtime hands it — the generated `accent` theme, the
	// high-contrast one, a `theme` named outright, and one a tree is given
	// with `set_theme` — and so again after each rebuild, which keeps an
	// app's own type scale or radii through a light/dark switch. Change the
	// Theme in place; whatever it leaves is what draws, so an app that
	// overwrites colours owns their contrast.
	adjust_theme: proc(th: ^Theme),
	// a11y is the accessibility preferences to run with — text size, reduce
	// motion, reduce transparency — for a desktop that owns them and would
	// rather say than be asked. Nothing (the default) follows the desktop's,
	// and `set_a11y_prefs` changes them while running.
	a11y:   Maybe(A11y_Prefs),
	// titlebar is the choices for a title bar the app draws itself
	// (window_chrome): the buttons and their side, what a double click and
	// the wheel do — for a desktop that owns them and would rather say than
	// be asked. Nothing (the default) follows the desktop's (GNOME's
	// settings, through the Settings portal), and `set_titlebar_prefs`
	// changes them while running.
	titlebar: Maybe(Titlebar_Prefs),
	// gpu_reset runs when the card was reset and the device built again.
	// Every canvas the app made is gone — canvases live in VRAM and have no
	// copy on the CPU — so an editor rebuilds its document from whatever it
	// does keep or says what was lost. An app with no canvases leaves it nil.
	// Anything the toolkit uploaded (glyphs, pictures, images) comes back on
	// its own. `BALDR_SIMULATE_GPU_RESET=2000` (or `2000,1500` to keep doing
	// it) rehearses one.
	gpu_reset: proc(s: ^State) -> Cmd(Msg),
	// init builds the app's first state. **Anything it keeps must be the
	// app's own memory**: it runs inside a frame whose temp allocator is
	// freed under it, so a string borrowed from `os.get_env(…,
	// context.temp_allocator)`, or a slice of a literal built on the
	// stack, is a dangling pointer by the first `update`. Clone what you
	// keep.
	init:   proc() -> State,
	// start runs once after init, for the command an app begins with
	// (read its file, start a job); nil → none.
	start:  proc(s: ^State) -> Cmd(Msg),
	update: proc(s: ^State, m: Msg) -> Cmd(Msg),
	view:   proc(s: ^State, ui: ^Ui(Msg)),
	// surfaces declares which surfaces exist, by tag, for this state (see
	// surface.odin). nil → one window from title / size. Called at start
	// and after every update; declaring none ends the app unless
	// keep_running holds it.
	surfaces: proc(s: ^State, out: ^[dynamic]Surface_Desc(Msg)),
	// on_close is what the app hears when the person closes its window —
	// the title bar's button, the desktop's Close, Alt+F4 — instead of the
	// run ending there. Set it and **the window does not close**: the
	// message reaches `update`, and it is the app that decides, by asking
	// first and then returning `quit`, or by saving and then returning it,
	// or by doing neither. nil ends the run, which is right for a window
	// holding nothing worth keeping.
	//
	// This is `Surface_Desc.on_close` for an app that declares no
	// `surfaces`. An app with a `surfaces` proc sets it per surface instead,
	// and this one is ignored.
	on_close: Maybe(Msg),
	// on_state is what the app hears about its window: its size, whether it
	// has the keyboard, whether the desktop maximised it. It arrives once
	// the window is first configured and then only on a change, so an app
	// that reopens the way it was left keeps the latest, and one that acts
	// on focus reads it in `update`, where that decision is made. See
	// `Window_State`.
	//
	// This is `Surface_Desc.on_state` for an app that declares no
	// `surfaces`, which is most of them; an app with a `surfaces` proc sets
	// it per surface instead and this one is ignored.
	on_state: Handler(Msg, Window_State),
	// single_instance makes this app one copy: a second launch hands its
	// arguments to the first and exits, and the first hears them through
	// `on_relaunch`. Opening an app that is already running (in the tray,
	// behind a window) should bring it forward, not start a second copy.
	//
	// The first copy's `run` carries on as usual; the second's returns
	// `.Already_Running`, which is **not a failure**: it did its job and
	// should exit 0.
	single_instance: bool,
	// on_relaunch hears a later launch, with its arguments (`Relaunch`).
	// The strings live until the update that was given them has run: copy
	// what you keep. Usually the app shows and raises its window, and opens
	// whatever that launch named.
	on_relaunch: Handler(Msg, Relaunch),
	// tray declares what this app shows in the desktop's tray — a status
	// item with an icon, a tooltip and a menu (tray.odin).
	// Called at start and after every update, and diffed, so a menu
	// rebuilt here is a menu updated in place. A declared tray **holds the
	// run open**, as keep_running does: an app in the tray with no window
	// has not finished.
	tray: proc(s: ^State, out: ^[dynamic]Tray_Desc(Msg)),
	// keep_running holds the run while the app is busy with no surface
	// declared — a copy finishing after its window closed. Messages, jobs,
	// subscriptions and delays carry on and no view is built; declaring a
	// surface opens it again. The run ends when nothing is declared and this
	// says false (or is nil), or on `quit`. Called at start and after every
	// update.
	//
	// **This is also how an app lives in the tray**, with no window at all:
	// a `surfaces` proc that appends nothing, and this returning true. Note
	// that *leaving `surfaces` out* is not the same thing — an app without
	// one gets the default window.
	keep_running: proc(s: ^State) -> bool,
	// subscriptions declares what the app listens to for this state:
	// `every` timers and `watch`es (sched.odin). Called at start and after
	// every update; a line that stays keeps running, a gone one stops.
	subscriptions: proc(s: ^State, out: ^[dynamic]Sub(Msg)),
}
```

App is what an application hands to run(). `init` may be nil (zero State).

### `Cmd_Kind`

```odin
Cmd_Kind :: enum u8 { None, Quit, Open_Files, Save_File, Job, Cancel, Batch, Delay, Read_File, Write_File, Copy_Text, Copy_Files, Copy_Html, Paste, Copy_Image, Paste_Image, Gpu_Open, Canvas_Read, Toplevel, Workspace, Activate, Poster, Lock, Unlock, Open_Url, Open_Path, Reveal_Path, Secret_Get, Secret_Set, Secret_Delete, Notify, Notify_Close, Focus, Reveal, Hold, Forget_Image }
```

Cmd is what update returns: an effect for the runtime to perform. `quit` ends the app; `open_files` / `save_file` show the desktop's own dialog and later send a message built from the outcome (a `Files`); `job` runs work on a thread and `cancel` stops it (job.odin); `delay` sends a message later (sched.odin); `read_file` / `write_file` do file IO off the main thread (file_io.odin); `batch` runs several.

### `Cmd`

struct

Cmd is a command: what `update` returns to ask the runtime for an effect. Build one with the constructors rather than filling the fields — `job`, `delay`, `read_file`, `write_file`, `copy_text`, `open_files`, `open_url`, `notify`, `quit`, `batch` and the rest — and return `{}` for "do nothing", which is the common case. `Cmd_Kind` above lists everything the runtime can be asked for. A command never touches the app's state itself: it runs, and the runtime delivers a message back to `update`.

### `Relaunch`

```odin
Relaunch :: struct {
	args: []string,
}
```

Relaunch is a later launch of a single-instance app: the arguments it was started with, which are the frame's — copy what you keep. `args[0]` is that launch's own program path, as os.args has it.

### `Files`

```odin
Files :: struct {
	paths: []string,
	ok:    bool,
	why:   Files_Why,
	uris:  []string,
}
```

Files is a file dialog's outcome: `ok` with the chosen paths, or not, and `why` either way — `.Cancelled` is the only one a person chose; `.Not_Shown` means no dialog appeared, which an app should say rather than leave the person wondering; `.Unusable` means what was picked is not a file on this computer, and `uris` says what it was. The paths and the URIs live only until the message is handled — clone what you keep.

### `Files_Why`

```odin
Files_Why :: platform.Files_Why
```

Files_Why is how a file dialog ended: `.Chosen`, `.Cancelled`, `.Not_Shown` or `.Unusable` (see `Files`).

### `File_Filter`

```odin
File_Filter :: platform.File_Filter
```

File_Filter is one choice in a file dialog's type list: a label a person reads and the patterns it matches. Pass a slice of them to `open_files` or `save_file`; the desktop's own dialog shows them.

### `Clip_Item`

```odin
Clip_Item :: platform.Clip_Item
```

Clip_Item is one form of what is on the clipboard: a mime type and its bytes.

### `Drag_Icon`

```odin
Drag_Icon :: platform.Drag_Icon
```

Drag_Icon is the picture a drag carries once the desktop has it, drawn by the toolkit as the drag leaves the window.

### `quit`

```odin
quit :: proc($Msg: typeid) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

### `open_files`

```odin
open_files :: proc($Msg: typeid, on_done: Handler(Msg, Files), title: string = "", multiple := false, directory := false,
	filters: []File_Filter = {}, folder: string = "", accept_label: string = "") -> Cmd(Msg)
```

open_files asks for the desktop's open dialog; the handler receives Files with the chosen path(s), or ok = false when cancelled.  case Pick: return baldr.open_files(Msg, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Files)) -> Msg { return Picked{e.value} }, title = "Choose an image", filters = {{name = "Images", patterns = {"*.png", "*.jpg"}}}) The strings and filters are copied for the rest of the loop iteration: literals written inside update (which live on its stack) are fine.

### `save_file`

```odin
save_file :: proc($Msg: typeid, on_done: Handler(Msg, Files), title: string = "", name: string = "",
	filters: []File_Filter = {}, folder: string = "", accept_label: string = "") -> Cmd(Msg)
```

save_file asks for the desktop's save dialog; the handler receives one path to write, or ok = false and `why`. Pass `folder` where you can; on Linux a dialog given none starts in the home folder, because the desktop's dialog service would otherwise start in whatever folder it was itself started from — which can be `/`.

### `files_deliver`

```odin
files_deliver :: proc(h: Handler($Msg, Files), files: Files, token: u64, out: ^[dynamic]Msg) -> bool
```

files_deliver turns a dialog outcome into the app's message through the command's handler (a plain message, or a proc given Ev{value, tag = token}); nil delivers nothing.

### `Frame_Ctx`

struct

Frame_Ctx is the non-generic slice of the run loop so a platform modal loop (Win32 size/move) can drive a frame through Backend.redraw_callback.

### `Output`

```odin
Output :: platform.Output
```

Output is one screen: connector name, description, logical position and size, scale and transform.

### `Output_Transform`

```odin
Output_Transform :: platform.Output_Transform
```

Output_Transform is how a screen is rotated or flipped, as the compositor reports it on an `Output`.

### `outputs`

```odin
outputs :: proc() -> []Output
```

outputs is the screens as the compositor last reported them, for `surfaces` to declare a bar on each. Valid until they change (an `outputs_changed` subscription says when); empty where the backend does not report screens.  The order is the order the screens arrived, which is no order at all to a person: use `Output.primary` for "the first screen" — where a desktop puts an icon nobody placed, where a shell puts the thing there should only be one of, and where something whose own screen was unplugged goes.

### `output_rect`

```odin
output_rect :: proc(o: Output) -> (pos: [2]i32, size: [2]i32)
```

output_rect is a screen's rectangle in the desktop's own coordinates, so that `Output.pos` and a window's position can be compared without the caller knowing which plane this platform uses.  It exists because `Output.pos` is logical on Wayland and X11 but **device pixels on Windows**, which has no logical desktop plane — so a screen's extent is `size` on the first two and `mode_px` on the third, and an app working out "is this point on a screen" would otherwise need that branch itself.

### `message_surface`

```odin
message_surface :: proc() -> (tag: u64, ok: bool)
```

message_surface is the tag of the surface whose widget, shortcut or dismiss produced the message `update` is handling, so one process with several windows applies it to the right one. ok is false for messages the app routes itself (job posts, delays, timers, watches, file dialogs, pastes) and outside update.

### `run`

```odin
run :: proc(app: App($State, $Msg)) -> Error
```

run opens the app's surfaces and runs it until the last one closes (or update returns quit). Returns the error that stopped it; .None on a normal close.  Act on what it returns: `.Disconnected` means the desktop went away or dropped the connection — the app did not ask to stop and has no window left to say so in — and an app that ignores it exits 0, which looks exactly like a clean close. The reason is already on stderr, so the app only carries it through:  if baldr.run_failed(baldr.run(app)) do os.exit(1)  `run_failed` rather than `err != .None`, because a single-instance app's second launch ends with `.Already_Running`, which is it doing its job.

## build.odin

Scoped builders. A view proc calls builders in order; containers open a scope that `end` closes. Each call reconciles against the current parent's child cursor — reuse the node in place if kind (and key) match, move a keyed node into position, or create — so a view re-run touches only what changed and never allocates a description tree.

### `Ev`

```odin
Ev :: struct($T: typeid) {
	value: T,
	tag:   u64,
	node:  Node,
}
```

Ev is what a payload handler receives: the new value, the emitting node, and the app-supplied tag (how a row says which item it was).

### `Handler`

```odin
Handler :: union($Msg, $T: typeid) {
	Msg,
	proc(Ev(T)) -> Msg,
}
```

Handler is either a plain message or a proc that builds one from an Ev. One builder per widget; both forms go through the same parameter.  `on_x = msg if cond else nil` means no handler. Odin brings both arms of a ternary to one type, so that `nil` is a nil procedure inside the union, not an empty union, and `h == nil` is false. Ask `handler_live(h)` instead of comparing to nil: it treats both as "nothing was given".

### `Space_Token`

```odin
Space_Token :: enum u8 { none, xxs, xs, sm, md, lg, xl, xxl, xxxl }
```

Space_Token is the theme's spacing scale, from `none` to `xxxl`: the sizes gaps, padding and margins come in, resolved through `Theme.space` so one theme sets the rhythm of every widget.

### `Space`

```odin
Space :: union { Space_Token, f32 }
```

Space is a gap, padding or margin: either a token from the theme's spacing scale or a raw number of logical pixels. Prefer the token — `gap = .md` stays right when the theme or its density changes, `gap = f32(7)` does not.

### `space_px`

```odin
space_px :: proc "contextless" (th: ^Theme, s: Space) -> f32
```

### `Ui`

struct

Ui is the per-app builder state: the tree plus message plumbing. `Msg` is the app's message type; layout/paint/a11y live on the embedded Tree and are compiled once.

### `ui_init`

```odin
ui_init :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), allocator := context.allocator)
```

### `ui_destroy`

```odin
ui_destroy :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg))
```

### `Locale`

```odin
Locale :: platform.Locale
```

Locale is how the desktop writes dates, times and numbers (see platform.Locale). run reads it from the desktop; set_locale overrides it for an app that carries its own, and re-runs the view.

### `set_locale`

```odin
set_locale :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), l: Locale)
```

### `begin_view`

```odin
begin_view :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), loc := #caller_location)
```

begin_view starts a build: the root becomes the open scope. The run loop calls this before the app's `view`, and end_view after.

### `end_view`

```odin
end_view :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), loc := #caller_location) -> Error
```

end_view finishes a build. Scopes the view forgot to close are reported (with the opener's location) and closed; unvisited children are removed.

### `end`

```odin
end :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), loc := #caller_location)
```

end closes the innermost open container scope (and the helper scopes an overlay builder opened along with it).

### `ui_tick`

```odin
ui_tick :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), now_ms: f64) -> bool
```

ui_tick advances animations, fires timers, and removes nodes whose exit transition finished. Call once per wake-up (the run loop does). Returns true while anything is still animating.

### `ui_after_events`

```odin
ui_after_events :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), now_ms: f64)
```

ui_after_events runs the checks that depend on what the latest input did: tooltip timing and virtual-list ranges. The run loop calls it after dispatching a batch of events (ui_tick calls it too).

### `Click_Pair`

```odin
Click_Pair :: struct {
	ms: f64,
	at: [2]f32,
}
```

Click_Pair is when and where a clickable was last clicked, for pairing a double-click. A pointer area cannot do this for a control: its press handler stops at the hovered node, which for a control is the area itself.

### `transition`

```odin
transition :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, enter: Transition_Kind, exit: Transition_Kind = .None, duration_ms: f32 = 0, easing: Easing = .Ease_Out)
```

transition sets how `n` appears and leaves. Call right after the builder that created it: `c := card(ui); transition(ui, c, .Fade_Up, .Fade)`. A node that leaves keeps its layout slot while fading, then is removed. Under reduce-motion both are instant. Its fill, border, shadow and glass fade with it; an overlay (a popover, menu, select's list or dialog) keeps them whole instead and fades only what is on it, so the controls it opens over never show through it (Transition.whole).

### `input_area`

```odin
input_area :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, on := true)
```

input_area marks `n` as part of where its surface takes the pointer, for a surface that declares `input = .Marked`: the region is the union of the marked nodes' rects (and any popover open on the surface) and follows layout.

### `backdrop_area`

```odin
backdrop_area :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, on := true)
```

backdrop_area marks `n` as part of where its surface is blurred behind, for a surface that declares `backdrop_region = .Marked`: the region is the union of the marked nodes' rounded rects where they are drawn, following layout, slides and glides, and the surface is clear elsewhere. Each marked node gives itself its glass (`card(glass = true)`): separate toast cards on one surface. A node on its way out leaves the region as its fade starts. Its corners round by its radius, square ones included. On a surface that adapts only its marked nodes, the children built inside `n` after this call get the glass theme, solved for the wallpaper, while the rest of the window keeps the app's colours; the scope gives the theme back when it closes. A pane that paints its own ground must read `ui.theme` after this call, or it paints with the unsolved theme under solved text (`card(glass = true)` does this itself).

### `theme_area`

```odin
theme_area :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, theme: Theme)
```

theme_area builds container `n` and everything inside it in a theme of the app's own: a reader's Sepia page, a photo editor's dark canvas, a terminal pane in its own palette. Call it on the container whose scope is open, as with `backdrop_area`; the tree's theme comes back when the scope closes. The subtree is painted with the theme as well as built with it, so hovers, focus rings and washes follow it; `theme_on_ground` derives a whole theme from a ground, an ink and an accent, keeping the desktop's accessibility preferences and the contrast text needs. A popup opened from inside (a select's list, a menu, a tooltip) keeps the window's theme, since it sits over the whole window; give its content a `theme_area` of its own to match the page. Layout, hit-testing and accessibility are untouched.

### `context_area`

```odin
context_area :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, on_context: Maybe(Msg))
```

context_area gives container `n` (a bar, a panel's background) a menu of its own without making it a control: a right-click on it, or on anything inside it without a menu of its own, sends `on_context`, as does the Menu key on a focused control inside it without one. It takes no focus, role, hover or press. Open the menu with `context_menu`. nil removes it.

### `square_corners`

```odin
square_corners :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, corners: Corners)
```

square_corners leaves `corners` of `n`'s `radius` square, the others rounded: a bar's content following the bar's shape, or a tab joined to its panel. It stays until changed; {} rounds all four again. Its fill, border, shadow, glass and focus ring all take the shape.

### `animate_moves`

```odin
animate_moves :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), container: Node, on := true)
```

animate_moves makes a container's children glide to their new positions when a rebuild reorders or reflows them (a toast pushing content down, a sorted list, chips wrapping). Layout is never touched: the glide is a paint-time shift over the theme's base duration, instant under reduce-motion, and skipped for window resizes.

### `handler_live`

```odin
handler_live :: proc(h: Handler($Msg, $T)) -> bool
```

handler_live says whether a handler would send anything: for a widget deciding whether to show a control at all. `h == nil` cannot answer it, because `x if cond else nil` gives a nil procedure inside the union rather than an empty union (see Handler).

### `handler_send`

```odin
handler_send :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), h: Handler(Msg, $T), value: T, node: Node) -> bool
```

handler_send delivers a Handler: a plain message is queued as-is; a proc receives Ev{value, tag, node} and its result is queued. An empty union or a nil proc sends nothing. True when a message was queued.

### `RECONCILE_LOOKAHEAD`

```odin
RECONCILE_LOOKAHEAD :: 32
```

RECONCILE_LOOKAHEAD is how many siblings an unkeyed call searches for its own node before making a new one.

### `column`

```odin
column :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), gap: Space = Space_Token.none, padding: Space = Space_Token.none,
	align: Align = .Stretch, justify: Justify = .Start, width: f32 = 0, height: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0,
	max_width: f32 = 0, max_height: f32 = 0,
	bg: Color = {}, radius: f32 = 0, border: Color = {}, border_width: f32 = 1, material: Material = {}, offset: [2]f32 = {}, animate := false, tag: u64 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

column opens a vertical container. Children stack top to bottom. `offset` moves it inside a `stack` parent and is ignored anywhere else. Close with end. `border` outlines it: a line of that colour, `border_width` px wide (1 unless said), just inside its edge and round its `radius`, taking no room; a zero colour is none.

### `stack`

```odin
stack :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), padding: Space = Space_Token.none, align: Align = .Start,
	width: f32 = 0, height: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0, max_width: f32 = 0, max_height: f32 = 0,
	bg: Color = {}, radius: f32 = 0, border: Color = {}, border_width: f32 = 1, tag: u64 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

stack opens a container whose children overlap, each placed at the container's padding origin plus the child's `offset`. The building block for overlays, floating panels, and glass over content. Close with end. `border` outlines it: a line of that colour, `border_width` px wide (1 unless said), just inside its edge and round its `radius`, taking no room; a zero colour is none.

### `row`

```odin
row :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), gap: Space = Space_Token.none, padding: Space = Space_Token.none,
	align: Align = .Start, justify: Justify = .Start, width: f32 = 0, height: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0,
	max_width: f32 = 0, max_height: f32 = 0,
	bg: Color = {}, radius: f32 = 0, border: Color = {}, border_width: f32 = 1, material: Material = {}, offset: [2]f32 = {}, animate := false,
	vertical := false, tag: u64 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

row opens a horizontal container. Children run left to right. Close with end. `vertical = true` stacks them top to bottom instead, stretched across: the one switch a header, a form row or a card's action row needs at phone width, `baldr.row(ui, gap = .md, vertical = baldr.size_class(ui) == .Compact)`. `offset` moves it inside a `stack` parent and is ignored anywhere else. `border` outlines it: a line of that colour, `border_width` px wide (1 unless said), just inside its edge and round its `radius`, taking no room; a zero colour is none.

### `box`

```odin
box :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), width: f32 = 0, height: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0, bg: Color = {}, radius: f32 = 0,
	border: Color = {}, border_width: f32 = 1, tag: u64 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

box is a leaf rectangle: spacing, dividers, colour swatches, a frame. `border` outlines it: a line of that colour, `border_width` px wide (1 unless said), just inside its edge and round its `radius`, taking no room; a zero colour is none.

### `text`

```odin
text :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), s: string, role: Text_Role = .Body, size: f32 = 0, color: Color = {},
	muted := false, nowrap := false, ellipsis: Ellipsis = .None, max_lines := 0, align: Text_Align = .Start,
	weight: Maybe(Font_Weight) = nil, mono := false, family: Family_Id = 0, tabular := false,
	sensitive := false,
	width: f32 = 0, min_width: f32 = 0, max_width: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0,
	key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

text is a run of text. `role` picks the size and weight from the theme's type scale (`size` and `weight`, Regular, Medium or Semibold, override them); zero `color` means the theme's on_surface. Its accessible name is its content. It is sized as any leaf is: a fixed `width`, `min_width` / `max_width`, or `flex` for a share of a row's free space; it wraps at the width it ends up with, and `align` places its lines in it. A label column and the values beside it are two texts in a row:  baldr.row(ui, gap = .md); { defer baldr.end(ui) baldr.text(ui, "Licence", muted = true, width = 170) baldr.text(ui, licence, flex = 1) }  `mono` sets it in the code face the editors use, for a run of text that is machinery rather than prose — a tool step under a reply, a hash, a path — so the words around it lead. `family` draws it in a family of its own instead: one this machine has (`text_family`) or one the app carries (`text_family_bytes`), such as a wordmark's face.

### `Button_Variant`

```odin
Button_Variant :: enum u8 {
	Primary,   // filled with the primary colour — the main action
	Secondary, // tonal: primary container
	Outline,   // transparent with an outline
	Ghost,     // transparent, text only
	Danger,    // filled with the danger colour
}
```

Button_Variant picks the colour roles a button uses.

### `button`

```odin
button :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), label: string, on_click: Msg, variant: Button_Variant = .Primary, disabled := false,
	icon: Icon = .None, icons: []Icon = nil, image: ^Image = nil, theme_icon := "", icon_only := false, size: Button_Size = .Regular, default := false, pressed: Maybe(bool) = nil, on_middle_click: Maybe(Msg) = nil,
	on_context: Maybe(Msg) = nil, on_scroll: Handler(Msg, Scroll) = nil, focus_on_click := true,
	tag: u64 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

button is a clickable control. `on_click` is queued when it is activated (pointer, Enter, Space, or an assistive-technology action). `default` marks the button Enter activates in a dialog: it takes the dialog's initial focus (unless the dialog starts with a text field) and is announced as the default. `pressed` makes it a toggle (show hidden files, the preview pane): a Toggle_Button, Pressed while true, drawn off in the variant's quiet form and on with its emphasis (the tonal fill for Ghost, Outline and Secondary). `on_context` is what a right-click, or the Menu key while it is focused, sends; `Ui.context_pos` is recorded first, so a `context_menu` opens there (under the button from the keyboard). `on_scroll` gets a wheel or touchpad scroll over it (a tray icon's Scroll, a volume applet) before any scroll view around it, and doesn't take focus. `icons` shows several icons side by side after `icon` (a bar button's Wi-Fi, sound and battery), decorative: say what they show in the button's description (`set_describe`). `theme_icon` is an icon from the desktop's icon theme by name (an app's Icon=), drawn where `icon` goes, as `theme_icon` draws it, in the label's colour. `size = .Small` makes it compact: by default 26 × 26 when `icon_only` (a tile's arrow), a 12 px label otherwise (Theme.button restyles both). Rarer settings (a width that cuts the label with "…", the icon's size and colour) are `button_options`.

### `Button_Size`

```odin
Button_Size :: enum u8 { Regular, Small }
```

Button_Size is how big a button is: Regular, or Small, compact.

### `button_options`

```odin
button_options :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, max_width: Maybe(f32) = nil, icon_size: Maybe(f32) = nil, icon_color: Maybe(Color) = nil,
	mark: Maybe(Button_Mark) = nil, badge: Maybe(Button_Badge) = nil, progress: Maybe(f32) = nil, quiet: Maybe(bool) = nil,
	focus_on_click: Maybe(bool) = nil, // focus_on_click is on `button` too: a tools column sets it on every one
	progress_style: Maybe(Progress_Style) = nil, indeterminate: Maybe(bool) = nil)
```

button_options sets a button's rarer settings, each kept until changed. `max_width` caps its width (0: none): its label then ends in "…" where it is cut, and a tight row shortens it further; the whole label stays its accessible name and shows on hover (a window's title on a taskbar). `icon_size` sizes its icon, theme icon or picture (0: their own size). `icon_color` colours its icon or theme icon ({}: the label's colour); a full-colour theme icon keeps its colours and fades with the alpha (a pinned app that isn't running). `mark` is drawn inside the button's edge and takes no room (a taskbar's running-app dot or bar, Button_Mark); `badge` sits on its icon's top end corner, taking no room either (a count, or a bell's dot: Button_Badge). `progress` draws a thin bar across the bottom of its icon, 0…1, taking no room and clear of the badge (a download on a taskbar entry): 0 is an empty track, a negative value takes the bar away, and the value eases as it changes. `quiet` takes the hover and press wash off, for a control that answers the pointer another way (a dock's icons grow, so the wash would say it twice): it still hovers, presses, takes the keyboard and shows its focus ring, mark, badge and tooltip, and reads the same to a screen reader — only the wash goes. An option left out keeps what the button had, so a mark, a badge or a bar goes only when you pass its empty value: a mark with `length = 0`, an empty `Button_Badge{}`, a negative `progress`. Anything but a button is left alone.

### `Button_Mark`

```odin
Button_Mark :: struct {
	side:      Edge,
	length:    f32,
	thickness: f32,
	color:     Color,
	count:     int, // 0 or 1: one mark; more: that many, side by side along the edge
}
```

Button_Mark is a mark a button draws inside its edge, taking no room: a taskbar's or dock's running-app dot, or a longer bar for the app in front. It sits on `side` (the edge facing the screen's edge), centred along it, `length` long (0: none) and `thickness` thick (0: the theme's), rounded, in `color` ({}: the button's content colour). A change of length or colour is animated (instant under reduce motion). `count` above 1 splits it into that many short marks with a gap between, in the same place and about the same length — two windows of one app, where a single mark could only say "running" (the caller caps it: three is plenty). It is drawn, not announced: say what it means in the button's description (`set_describe`), count included.

### `Button_Badge`

```odin
Button_Badge :: struct {
	text:  string,
	dot:   bool,
	color: Color,
}
```

Button_Badge is a small badge at the top end corner of a button's icon, over it and taking no room: a count or a word in a pill ("3", "99+"), or, with no text and `dot` set, a dot (a bell with notifications waiting). It is in `color` ({}: the accent), its text in a colour that reads on it; neither text nor dot is none. Coming and going it grows and shrinks over motion.fast (at once under reduce motion); on a button's first paint it shows as it is. It is drawn, not announced: say what it means in the button's description (`set_describe`).

### `Progress_Style`

```odin
Progress_Style :: enum u8 {
	Bar,  // a thin bar across the bottom of the icon: for an app's picture in a taskbar or a dock
	Ring, // a ring just outside the icon, filling clockwise from twelve o'clock: for a line glyph, whose bottom stroke a bar would land on
}
```

Progress_Style is how a button shows its progress (`button_options`).

### `component`

```odin
component :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), key: string, rev: u64, loc := #caller_location) -> bool
```

component wraps a subtree that only needs rebuilding when `rev` changes. Returns false — and leaves the retained subtree untouched — when the revision matches the last build. Close with end when it returns true.

### `click`

```odin
click :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node) -> bool
```

click activates a control (used by event dispatch and by tests): a button queues its message; a checkbox / toggle sends its flipped value.

### `Elevation_Level`

```odin
Elevation_Level :: enum u8 { None, Low, Mid, High }
```

Elevation_Level picks a shadow preset from the theme.

### `wrap`

```odin
wrap :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), gap: Space = Space_Token.none, padding: Space = Space_Token.none,
	align: Align = .Start, justify: Justify = .Start, width: f32 = 0, height: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0,
	max_width: f32 = 0, max_height: f32 = 0,
	vertical := false, bg: Color = {}, radius: f32 = 0, border: Color = {}, border_width: f32 = 1, animate := false, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

wrap is a row that breaks into new lines when its children do not fit the width (chips, tags, a toolbar, a card grid). Children with `flex` grow to fill their line. `align` positions children within a line; `justify` spreads a line's leftover. `vertical` runs it down instead, starting a new column to the right when the next child would not fit the height (a top bar's rows of applets): the wrap is as wide as its columns, `align` places children across a column's width and `justify` spreads a column's leftover height. `gap` is between children and between lines or columns. Close with end. `border` outlines it: a line of that colour, `border_width` px wide (1 unless said), just inside its edge and round its `radius`, taking no room; a zero colour is none.

### `Size_Class`

```odin
Size_Class :: enum u8 { Compact, Medium, Expanded }
```

Size_Class buckets the window width so a view can choose a layout without inventing breakpoints (Material's window size classes): Compact is a phone or a narrow pane, Medium a tablet or split view, Expanded a desktop window. The run loop re-runs the view when the class changes; a view reads it through size_class and never a raw pixel width.

### `size_class_of`

```odin
size_class_of :: proc "contextless" (width: f32) -> Size_Class
```

### `size_class`

```odin
size_class :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg)) -> Size_Class
```

size_class is the window's current size class (see Size_Class).

### `viewport`

```odin
viewport :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg)) -> [2]f32
```

viewport is the window's logical size (zero before the first configure). A view that asks for it is built again whenever it changes, so what it worked out from it (a viewer's visible part of a picture) never goes stale; a view that doesn't is only laid out again, as any resize is.

### `device_scale`

```odin
device_scale :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg)) -> f32
```

device_scale is how many of the screen's pixels one logical pixel of this surface is (2 on a 2× screen, 1.25 at 125 %), from the same configure as `viewport`; 1 before the first. What a picture at 100 % needs: one of its pixels to one of the screen's is 1 / device_scale logical px. A move to a screen of another scale builds the view again.

### `spacer`

```odin
spacer :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), size: f32 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

spacer takes up room along the parent's main axis: `size` logical px, or all the remaining space when `size` is 0 (push siblings apart, right-align a row's tail). Invisible and not in the accessibility tree.

### `card`

```odin
card :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), gap: Space = nil, padding: Space = nil,
	align: Align = .Stretch, width: f32 = 0, height: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0, max_width: f32 = 0, max_height: f32 = 0,
	elevation: Maybe(Elevation_Level) = nil, glass := false, offset: [2]f32 = {}, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

card opens a raised surface: background, rounded corners, hairline border, and a drop shadow. Children stack vertically. `gap`, `padding` and `elevation` left out are the theme's (Theme.card). `offset` moves it inside a `stack` parent and is ignored anywhere else. Close with end.

### `scroll`

```odin
scroll :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), axis: Axis = .Column, gap: Space = Space_Token.none, padding: Space = Space_Token.none,
	align: Align = .Stretch, width: f32 = 0, height: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0, bg: Color = {},
	max_width: f32 = 0, max_height: f32 = 0, follow_end := false, gutter := false, content_width: f32 = 0,
	key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

scroll opens a scroll container: children stack along `axis` and may overflow it; the wheel, trackpad, and the overlay scrollbar move them. Give it a size — `height` (`width` along a row), or `flex = 1` inside a column — or let it take its content's size up to `max_height` (`max_width`) and scroll past that: a popover's list, which has no height to flex into. A scroll region that could grow forever would never need to scroll. Keyboard focus scrolls a control into view either way. Close with end.  `content_width` makes a column scroll sideways as well: its children are laid out that wide, it pans with the wheel's x, Shift+wheel, a touchpad, Shift+Page / Home / End and `scroll_by` / `scroll_to`'s x, and it draws a bar along its bottom. Give the width the app knows (a document's widest page at the current zoom) rather than letting the widest child decide: in a virtualised view only built children are measured, so the extent would change as it scrolls. A child naturally wider keeps its width and the others are widened to match. Zero scrolls only down. It is read every build.

## surface.odin

Surfaces: what an app opens on the desktop. A plain app has one window and never touches this file (run opens it from App.title / App.size). A shell — the compositor's panel, dock, launcher, wallpaper, lock screen — declares several surfaces by tag in App.surfaces and switches on ui.surface.tag in its view; the runtime opens what is new and closes what is gone after every update, so a surface's lifetime is a fact about the state, not a command to remember. Each surface has its own tree, focus and layers; the state, update, view, theme and text engine are shared.

```odin
surfaces :: proc(s: ^State, out: ^[dynamic]baldr.Surface_Desc(Msg)) {
	append(out, baldr.Surface_Desc(Msg){tag = PANEL, kind = .Layer, size = {0, 36},
		layer = {level = .Top, anchor = {.Top, .Left, .Right}, exclusive_zone = 36, keyboard = .On_Demand},
		transparent = true, backdrop = .Blur, on_close = Msg.Quit})
	if s.launcher do append(out, baldr.Surface_Desc(Msg){tag = LAUNCHER, kind = .Layer, …})
}
```

On Wayland a .Layer surface is a wlr-layer-shell surface; without layer shell (other platforms, or a compositor lacking it) it becomes an undecorated window. A Blur backdrop needs the compositor (ext-background-effect-v1); without it, or with the OS "reduce transparency" setting on, the surface is cleared with the same material colour made opaque — text on it stays readable either way.

### `Keyboard_Interactivity`

```odin
Keyboard_Interactivity :: platform.Keyboard_Interactivity
```

Keyboard_Interactivity is how much of the keyboard a layer surface asks for — none, on demand, or exclusive. A bar wants none, a launcher with a search field wants it outright.

### `FIT`

```odin
FIT :: f32(-1)
```

FIT as a .Layer's `size` along an axis makes the surface as long as its content there: a dock or a floating bar, anchored to one edge to sit in its middle, or to an edge and a side to sit at that end. Baldr measures the view's natural length at the surface's thickness, capped at the screen's length less its margins, and asks for it when it changes; the frame at the old length is skipped. Content longer than the screen shortens what gives, as a tight row does.

### `FIT_START_LEN`

```odin
FIT_START_LEN :: f32(64)
```

FIT_START_LEN is the length a FIT surface opens at, before its content is measured.

### `Surface_Desc`

```odin
Surface_Desc :: struct($Msg: typeid) {
	tag:         u64,
	kind:        Surface_Kind,
	title:       string,
	size:        [2]f32,
	layer:       Layer_Desc, // .Layer only: level, anchors, exclusive zone, margins, keyboard, output
	applet:      Applet_Desc, // .Applet only: the slot's token ("" = RIKKITI_APPLET_TOKEN, read when it opens) and whether a click gives it the keyboard (live)
	output:      string,     // .Lock only: the screen it covers, by its name in outputs(); it is that screen's size (lock.odin)
	transparent: bool,       // alpha composites with the desktop (rounded corners, margins, glass)
	// What the desktop does behind this surface. `.Blur` is glass (needs
	// `transparent`). `.Measure` blurs nothing and reports the brightness
	// behind, read with `backdrop_range`: for text drawn straight on the
	// wallpaper, which picks its colour with `legible_ink`.
	backdrop:    Backdrop,
	backdrop_region: Backdrop_Region, // …behind the whole surface, or only its backdrop_area nodes (clear elsewhere); live
	undecorated: bool,       // .Window: no server-side frame
	fixed_size:  bool,       // .Window: not resizable
	// .Window: how small and how large the desktop may let it be dragged,
	// in logical px; zero on an axis is no limit (`{480, 0}` floors the width
	// only). Limits, not a size: `size` is clamped into them before the
	// desktop is asked, so a restored size below a newer minimum still lays
	// out. `fixed_size` wins over both, and is how a window that cannot be
	// resized says so (not equal limits). A request: the compositor may
	// ignore it, and what happened arrives in `on_state`.
	min_size:    [2]f32,
	max_size:    [2]f32,
	// .Window: ask the desktop for the whole screen (a reading or focus
	// mode, a slideshow, a video). It is a request — the compositor decides
	// and may say no — so build from `ui.surface.fullscreen`, which is what
	// it actually did, never from what was asked. Live.
	fullscreen:  bool,
	// .Window: ask the desktop to open it maximised, for a window coming
	// back the way it was left. A request, honoured at creation only: what
	// happened is `ui.surface.maximized`, which `on_state` also reports and
	// which is what to save.
	maximized:   bool,
	// .Window: ask the desktop to open it at this position, in the desktop's
	// own coordinates (`Output.pos`). A request, honoured at creation only;
	// nil asks for nothing, and Wayland ignores it. A position whose screen
	// has gone is dropped so the window is never placed out of reach (size
	// and maximised still apply), so pass back whatever was saved. Save
	// `Window_State.position` from `on_state`, not while maximised: that
	// position is the maximised one.
	position:    Maybe([2]i32),
	// .Window: the tag of the window this one belongs to — a preferences or a
	// details window — which the desktop keeps it above and minimises with.
	// Declare it after that window, which must be open. Wayland, X11 and
	// Windows honour it; on macOS windows stay independent, as Mac apps' do.
	parent:      Maybe(u64),
	input:       Surface_Input, // where it takes the pointer: everywhere, the input_area nodes, or nowhere; live
	hide:        Layer_Hide,    // .Layer on one edge: slides off it while hidden (autohide, intellihide); live
	radius:      f32,           // a transparent glass .Layer's corners (a floating bar, a dock): its tint and highlight round, it is clear outside, and with input = .All the corners take no pointer; live
	square:      Corners,       // …the corners it keeps square (a bar attached to an edge: those at the edge)
	clear:       Maybe(Color),  // what it is cleared to: nil, the theme's (a glass layer's panel tint); a colour keeps its blur without the tint, so the view draws its own scrim (a launcher's dimmed desktop; {} fully clear)
	on_close:    Maybe(Msg),
	// on_state is what the app hears when the desktop changes this
	// surface's size, keyboard focus, or maximised / full-screen / tiled
	// state. See `Window_State`.
	on_state:    Handler(Msg, Window_State),
}
```

Surface_Desc describes one surface the app wants open. `tag` identifies it across updates (0 is fine for a single surface); `on_close` is sent when the user or compositor closes it — leave it nil and a close quits the app. `size` is logical px; a .Layer stretched between two opposite anchors leaves that dimension 0 and takes the output's, and one as long as its content along an axis gives that dimension FIT.

### `Window_State`

struct

Window_State is what the desktop has done with a surface (not what was asked for), delivered to `update` through `Surface_Desc.on_state` or `App.on_state`, for decisions made outside `view`: whether the window has the keyboard, and the size and maximised state to save and pass back as `Surface_Desc.size` / `.maximized`. It arrives once the surface is first configured and then only on a change, so keeping the latest is enough.

### `Surface_Info`

```odin
Surface_Info :: struct {
	tag:   u64,
	kind:  Surface_Kind,
	glass: bool,
	edge:  Maybe(Edge),
	// what the desktop has actually done with the window, from its latest
	// configure — not what the app asked for. A view that hides its chrome
	// full screen, or its own title bar when maximised, reads these.
	fullscreen: bool,
	maximized:  bool,
	tiled:      bool, // against a neighbour or a screen edge: sized by the desktop, as when maximised
	rolled:     bool, // rolled up to its title bar (window_chrome): only the bar shows, laid out at its full size still
	keyboard:   bool, // it (or a popup of it) has the keyboard: a lock screen's password card goes on this one
	scale:      f32, // device pixels per logical pixel, from the same configure (0 before it: device_scale says 1)
}
```

Surface_Info is what a view learns about the surface it is building: ui.surface.tag picks the layout, ui.surface.glass says whether translucent materials will show blurred desktop behind them (else paint opaque). `kind` is what the surface opened as (an applet with no shell to embed it is a window; a layer surface without layer shell too). `edge` is the screen edge a bar or applet is on: a layer surface's anchored edge, an applet's bar's (the shell can move it: the view is built again); none for a window. A side bar's content runs down it.

### `surface_resolve`

```odin
surface_resolve :: proc(desc: Surface_Desc($Msg), caps: platform.Caps, app_id: string) -> platform.Surface_Desc
```

surface_resolve maps a description onto what the backend can do: .Layer degrades to an undecorated window without layer shell, transparency and blur are dropped when unsupported. Sizes a stretched layer would take from the output get a window default instead.  An applet binds to its bar with its token (the declared one, else RIKKITI_APPLET_TOKEN) where the backend can embed applets, transparent; without a token or the capability it becomes a small window.

### `layer_edge`

```odin
layer_edge :: proc "contextless" (l: Layer_Desc) -> Maybe(Edge)
```

layer_edge is the screen edge a layer surface sits along: an anchored edge whose opposite isn't (a bar stretched between Left and Right is on Top or Bottom); for a corner, its exclusive edge. None for a surface on no single edge (a launcher in a corner, a centred dialog).

### `surface_glass`

```odin
surface_glass :: proc(desc: Surface_Desc($Msg), caps: platform.Caps, level: Glass_Level) -> bool
```

surface_glass says whether blurred desktop will actually show through the surface: the theme's glass isn't Solid (OS "reduce transparency" makes it so), the app asked for a blur backdrop, and the compositor can do it.

### `surface_watches_backdrop`

```odin
surface_watches_backdrop :: proc(desc: Surface_Desc($Msg), caps: platform.Caps) -> bool
```

surface_watches_backdrop: whether this surface asked to be told how bright what is behind it is without being glass (`Backdrop.Measure`), the desktop shows through it, and the compositor measures. Glass asks on its own account; this is the surface that draws straight on the desktop and reads the numbers in its view (`backdrop_range`).

### `surface_popups_glass`

```odin
surface_popups_glass :: proc(desc: Surface_Desc($Msg), caps: platform.Caps, level: Glass_Level) -> bool
```

surface_popups_glass: whether the popups a surface opens are glass over the desktop. Not the same as whether the surface is: a desktop asks for no blur because it is the wallpaper, yet its menus open over that wallpaper and must be glass (and measured for contrast) on it.

### `Backdrop_Region`

```odin
Backdrop_Region :: enum u8 { All, Marked }
```

Backdrop_Region is where a surface's blur backdrop reaches: the whole surface, or only the nodes marked with backdrop_area, following layout (separate glass toast cards on one surface), the surface clear elsewhere.

### `Surface_Input`

```odin
Surface_Input :: enum u8 {
	All,    // the whole surface
	Marked, // the nodes marked with input_area, following layout (a floating bar's shape, a hidden dock's reveal strip)
	None,   // nowhere: drawn only (a toast fading out)
}
```

Surface_Input is how much of a surface answers the pointer. It is live, so a dock can take clicks only on its reveal strip while hidden and everywhere once shown.

### `Surface_Change`

```odin
Surface_Change :: enum u8 { Title, Backdrop, Kind, Transparent, Decoration, Resizable, Layer, Size, Output, Input, Applet, Shape, Hide, Fullscreen }
```

Surface_Change names a difference between how an open surface was declared and how it is declared now.

### `Surface_Changes`

```odin
Surface_Changes :: bit_set[Surface_Change; u16]
```

### `SURFACE_LIVE_CHANGES`

```odin
SURFACE_LIVE_CHANGES :: Surface_Changes{.Title, .Backdrop, .Layer, .Size, .Output, .Input, .Applet, .Shape, .Hide, .Fullscreen}
```

SURFACE_LIVE_CHANGES apply to an open surface: a layer surface's placement and size in place, and its screen by a new platform surface under the same tree; an applet's keyboard setting. The rest need a new tag.

### `Hide_Mode`

```odin
Hide_Mode :: enum u8 { Never, Always, Overlap }
```

Hide_Mode is when a layer surface hides at its edge: Never; Always (autohide: hidden until the pointer reaches its edge); Overlap (intellihide: hidden while Layer_Hide.covered says a window covers it).

### `Layer_Hide`

```odin
Layer_Hide :: struct {
	mode:     Hide_Mode,
	covered:  bool, // Overlap: a window covers where it shows
	delay_ms: f32,  // after the pointer leaves (0: HIDE_DELAY_MS)
	strip:    f32,  // logical px along its edge that reveal it (0: HIDE_STRIP)
}
```

Layer_Hide makes a layer surface anchored to one screen edge hide at it. Hidden, its drawing and blur slide off towards the edge and `strip` px along the edge take the pointer and reveal it; it takes no exclusive zone while hiding is on. It shows while the pointer is on it or its popups, a popover or menu is open on it, or it has the keyboard, and hides `delay_ms` after the pointer has left them all. `covered` is the app's answer for Overlap (surface_rect gives where it shows). Give it a transparent surface. Its margin on that edge holds while it shows and is dropped while hidden, so the strip sits at the screen's own edge.

### `layer_placed`

```odin
layer_placed :: proc "contextless" (d: Surface_Desc($Msg), at_edge := false) -> Layer_Desc
```

layer_placed is the placement a surface asks the compositor for: as declared, without an exclusive zone while it hides (windows go under it), and — while it is hidden and fully slid out (`at_edge`) — without the margin on the edge it hides at, so its reveal strip lies along the screen's own edge and the pointer shoved into the corner finds it. The margin is given back as it slides out again.

### `surface_changes`

```odin
surface_changes :: proc(old, new: Surface_Desc($Msg)) -> (ch: Surface_Changes)
```

surface_changes compares two declarations of the same surface (the close message is always taken as it is now; `size` follows the declaration for a window and a layer surface alike, as a layer surface's placement and screen do).

### `surface_clear`

```odin
surface_clear :: proc(th: ^Theme, desc: Surface_Desc($Msg), glass: bool) -> Color
```

surface_clear is the colour the runtime clears a surface with before the view paints: the theme background for an opaque surface; the panel / window material tint over blurred desktop when glass is live (its alpha raised as far as the text needs over what is behind, glass.odin); Solid glass's own near-opaque tint; that tint made opaque when the compositor can't blur (readable either way); and nothing at all for a transparent surface without a backdrop — the view draws its own shapes there and consults ui.surface.glass.

### `surface_shape`

```odin
surface_shape :: proc(th: ^Theme, desc: Surface_Desc($Msg), glass: bool) -> Color
```

surface_shape is the tint a rounded glass layer surface, or one that hides at its edge, draws as its shape (Surface_Desc.radius and square) so that it can slide, leaving the rest of it clear: the panel material's tint, raised as surface_clear raises it, opaque where the compositor can't blur. Zero for any other surface (its clear is its tint).

### `surface_highlight`

```odin
surface_highlight :: proc(th: ^Theme, desc: Surface_Desc($Msg), glass: bool) -> Color
```

surface_highlight is the bright line along a glass layer surface's top edge: none on a window, nor where that edge is the screen's (a bar attached to the top).

### `surface_output_present`

```odin
surface_output_present :: proc(desc: Surface_Desc($Msg), outs: []Output) -> bool
```

surface_output_present: a .Layer surface that names a screen, and a .Lock surface, open only while their screen exists; anything else can always open.

### `surface_diff`

```odin
surface_diff :: proc(open: []u64, wanted: []u64, allocator := context.temp_allocator) -> (to_open, to_close: []int)
```

surface_diff compares the tags of the open surfaces with the wanted ones: `to_open` indexes `wanted` (in declaration order), `to_close` indexes `open` (ascending; close in reverse to keep indices valid). Both slices are in temp memory by default.

## theme.odin

Theme is plain data: semantic colour roles, state-layer strengths, radii, a spacing scale, a type scale, and motion tokens. Widgets read tokens; they never hold literal colours. Dark and light ship in-tree; a custom theme is a struct literal or a copy of one of these with fields changed. Colours are authored in sRGB via rgb()/rgba() and stored linear.

### `Theme`

```odin
Theme :: struct {
	name:    string,
	dark:    bool,          // Dim and Dark modes
	mode:    Theme_Mode,
	contrast: Theme_Contrast,
	seed:    Color,         // the accent it was made from (theme_from_seed); Clear glass takes its text families from it (zero: the theme's own colours only)
	// The families its text is set in, named as this machine has them
	// (`installed_fonts`): "" is the face embedded in the binary — Inter,
	// and JetBrains Mono for the code one. Everything the toolkit draws
	// follows them; a family this machine has not got draws as the embedded
	// face, with no error. The strings are the app's,
	// as `name` is: valid when the theme is installed, and copied then.
	font_family: string,
	mono_family: string,
	color:   Color_Roles,
	state:   State_Layers,
	radius:  Radii,
	space:   [Space_Token]f32, // logical px per token
	type:    Type_Scale,
	motion:  Motion,
	elev:    Elevation,
	glass:   Glass_Level,   // how much desktop shows through glass; reduce transparency and high contrast make it Solid and opaque
	material: [Glass_Level]Materials, // every level's materials, tuned for the mode; material[glass] is the one in use
	density: f32, // control heights: 0 comfortable, -1 compact, +1 spacious (DENSITY_STEP a unit; fractions too)
	// The smallest an interactive control is laid out, across and down, in
	// logical px, whatever the pointer: buttons, checkboxes, switches,
	// sliders, fields, selects, tabs, menu items, list and tree rows. 0 is
	// the toolkit's own sizes (a button about 32 px tall, a checkbox 26);
	// 44 is what the accessibility guidelines ask for. A control grows to it
	// with its content centred, and one given a fixed `width` or `height`
	// keeps it.
	min_target: f32,
	// Component styles (theme_style.odin): every field nil derives from the
	// tokens above, so set only what you change — `th.button.radius = 4`.
	button:      Button_Style,
	icon_toggle: Icon_Toggle_Style,
	field:       Field_Style,
	check:       Check_Style,
	toggle:      Toggle_Style,
	slider:      Slider_Style,
	segmented:   Segmented_Style,
	list_row:    List_Row_Style,
	tree_row:    Tree_Row_Style,
	table:       Table_Style,
	icon_view:   Icon_View_Style,
	tab:         Tab_Style,
	menu:        Menu_Style,
	menu_bar:    Menu_Bar_Style,
	chip:        Chip_Style,
	menu_chip:   Menu_Chip_Style,
	separator:   Separator_Style,
	card:        Card_Style,
	dialog:      Dialog_Style,
	popover:     Popover_Style,
	tooltip:     Tooltip_Style,
	toast:       Toast_Style,
	scrollbar:   Scrollbar_Style,
	split:       Split_Style,
	focus_ring:  Focus_Ring_Style,
}
```

Theme is everything a widget needs to draw itself consistently: colour roles, state layers, a corner-radius ladder, the spacing scale, a type scale, motion timings, elevation and glass materials. An app usually sets none of it — the default follows the desktop's light/dark, accent and contrast — and reads it when drawing something of its own (`ui.tree.theme`).  Three ways in, in increasing order of "and then it follows nothing": `App.accent` gives a brand colour the theme is generated from, still following the desktop; `App.adjust_theme` is the app's last word on every theme the runtime hands it, which is the right door for a style knob because it survives a light/dark switch and an in-app theme picker; and `App.theme` names one outright. `set_theme` changes a tree's theme while it runs.  The knobs an app most often turns in `adjust_theme`: `font_family` and `mono_family`, the faces all its text and its code are set in; `type.<step>.family`, a face of its own for one step of the type scale — headings on `display` / `headline` / `title`, every control's label on `label` (Text_Style); `radius`, the corner ladder; and `min_target`, the smallest a control is laid out across and down, 44 for targets anyone can hit.

### `Glass_Level`

```odin
Glass_Level :: enum u8 { Clear, Frosted, Solid }
```

Glass_Level: one glass setting for every theme. Clear shows the most desktop, with a soft glow behind text and icons; Frosted (the default) a milky blur; Solid a near-opaque tint and no blur. Each level is tuned per mode, so Clear looks as clear in Light as in Dark, and on a glass surface the tint rises only as far as its text needs to read over what is behind it (glass.odin).

### `Materials`

```odin
Materials :: struct {
	popover, sidebar, sheet, panel, window: Material,
	glow: Glow,
}
```

Materials: frosted-glass presets per surface class. `window` and `panel` also tint whole surfaces over the blurred desktop (behind-window blur). `glow` goes behind text and icons that sit on glass (Clear only).

### `Glow`

```odin
Glow :: struct {
	color:  Color,
	radius: f32,
	passes: int, // how many times it is laid down (0 or 1: once). A blur of a glyph is thin stuff — an outline that has to carry white text over a white wallpaper needs several
}
```

Glow: a soft halo of `color` behind text and icons, blurred over `radius` logical px (at most GLOW_MAX_RADIUS). Zero = none.

### `Elevation`

```odin
Elevation :: struct {
	low, mid, high: Shadow_Style,
}
```

Elevation: drop-shadow presets. Colour comes from color.shadow.

### `Color_Roles`

```odin
Color_Roles :: struct {
	bg:                   Color, // window background
	surface:              Color, // cards, sheets
	surface_variant:      Color, // inputs, wells
	surface_high:         Color, // popovers, elevated
	on_bg:                Color,
	on_surface:           Color,
	on_surface_muted:     Color, // secondary text
	primary:              Color,
	on_primary:           Color,
	primary_container:    Color,
	on_primary_container: Color,
	// accent_text is the accent drawn as ink, where `primary` is the accent
	// drawn as a fill (see above). Always 4.5:1 on `surface`, the bar the
	// text roles meet.
	accent_text:          Color,
	secondary:            Color,
	on_secondary:         Color,
	danger:               Color,
	on_danger:            Color,
	warning:              Color,
	success:              Color,
	fill:                 Color, // a faint fill of the content colour that reads on any surface or glass: tiles, chips
	fill_strong:          Color, // …a stronger one: slider tracks, switched-off toggles, a tile's controls
	outline:              Color, // borders
	outline_variant:      Color, // dividers
	focus_ring:           Color,
	selection:            Color, // a 35 % wash of the accent, for an app drawing its own selection. No widget draws with it: it falls under the 3:1 a change of state wants, so the toolkit's selections are `primary` with the text in `on_primary` (selection_paint.odin)
	scrim:                Color,
	shadow:               Color,
	code:                 Code_Colors, // syntax colours over surface_variant (the editor's well)
}
```

Color_Roles is a theme's palette by *job* rather than by hue: what a surface is, what goes on top of it, and what each state and accent means. A widget asks for the role it needs — `surface_variant` for a well, `on_surface` for text on a card — so one theme change recolours everything coherently and the contrast ratios hold in light, dark and high contrast.  The accent is two roles because it does two jobs. `primary` is the accent as a *fill*: a selection, a switched-on toggle, a Primary button, a progress bar, a focus ring. `accent_text` is the accent as *ink*: a ghost button's label and icon, a breadcrumb's current place, a link, an unread count. Every generated theme sets them to the same colour; they differ only for a style whose accent reads well as a fill and badly as text, as a mid grey does. `accent_text` is always at least 4.5:1 on `surface`.

### `Code_Colors`

```odin
Code_Colors :: struct {
	keyword, type, string, number, comment, operator, punct, constant, attribute: Color,
	current_line, gutter, gutter_current, match: Color,
}
```

Code_Colors: one colour per token kind, each readable (≥ 4.5:1) over surface_variant; derived from the seed hue so a themed app's editor belongs to the app. `current_line` is the caret line's wash, `gutter` the line numbers, `match` the bracket-pair box.

### `State_Layers`

```odin
State_Layers :: struct {
	hover, press, focus, drag: f32,
	disabled_alpha:            f32,
}
```

State_Layers: how strongly interaction states mix `on_surface` over a control's colour (0..1), and the alpha applied to disabled controls.

### `Radii`

```odin
Radii :: struct {
	none, xs, sm, md, lg, xl, full: f32,
}
```

Radii: the corner radii a theme draws with, from `none` to `xl`, and `full` for a pill — a shape whose ends want to be round. Every theme in the toolkit sets `full` to 9999, which is "as round as the shape allows"; a theme with square corners sets it low, and the pills follow. What is round on purpose — a switch's knob, a slider's thumb, a radio, a dot, a swatch, an avatar — does not read `full` at all.

### `Text_Style`

```odin
Text_Style :: struct {
	size, line_height: f32,
	weight:            Font_Weight,
	family:            string,
}
```

Text_Style is one step of the type scale: font size (logical px), line-height multiplier, the role's weight, and the family it is set in.  `family` is for a design whose headings or control labels are set in a face of their own: name it on `display`, `headline` and `title` for the headings, on `label` for what the toolkit sets at that step (button, tab, chip, segmented and menu-bar labels, table headers, breadcrumbs, tooltips). "" is the theme's `font_family`. It is named as `font_family` is — a family this machine has, or one registered with `text_family_bytes` — and one that cannot be found draws as `font_family`, with no error. Body text, fields and editors follow `font_family` itself. The string is the app's, as `font_family`'s is.

### `Type_Scale`

```odin
Type_Scale :: struct {
	display, headline, title, body, label, caption: Text_Style,
}
```

Type_Scale is the theme's six text styles, one per `Text_Role`. `text_style(th, role)` is how a widget asks for one.

### `Text_Role`

```odin
Text_Role :: enum u8 { Body, Display, Headline, Title, Label, Caption }
```

Text_Role is which step of the theme's type scale a text is set at: `Body` for prose, `Display` / `Headline` / `Title` for headings in decreasing size, `Label` for a control's own text, `Caption` for the smallest supporting text. Each carries a size, line height and weight from `Theme.type`, and every one of them follows the desktop's text-size setting — so reach for a role before a `size` in pixels.

### `text_style`

```odin
text_style :: proc "contextless" (th: ^Theme, role: Text_Role) -> Text_Style
```

### `Motion`

```odin
Motion :: struct {
	fast, base, slow: f32,
	reduce:           bool,
}
```

Motion durations in milliseconds; `reduce` mirrors the OS reduce-motion setting.

### `theme_opaque`

```odin
theme_opaque :: proc(th: ^Theme)
```

theme_opaque makes glass Solid with every material fully opaque and no glow: what reduce transparency and high contrast ask for. For an app that reads its own reduce-transparency setting; the desktop's is folded in by the toolkit.

### `Theme_Mode`

```odin
Theme_Mode :: enum u8 { Light, Dim, Dark }
```

Theme_Mode picks the lightness family: Light for daylight rooms, Dim (a soft mid-dark, easier on the eyes than full dark for long sessions), Dark for low light and OLED.

### `Theme_Contrast`

```odin
Theme_Contrast :: enum u8 { Normal, High }
```

Theme_Contrast: Normal meets WCAG AA/AAA for text; High pushes text and edges further apart for low vision and mirrors the OS high-contrast setting (it also turns glass off).

### `BALDR_BLUE`

```odin
BALDR_BLUE :: Color{0.04, 0.22, 0.90, 1} // ≈ #3B82F6 in linear sRGB
```

BALDR_BLUE is the default accent: a calm blue that reads well on both light and dark ground.

### `NEUTRAL_SEED_C`

```odin
NEUTRAL_SEED_C :: f32(0.02)
```

NEUTRAL_SEED_C is the seed chroma from which an accent is taken at full strength. Below it a theme fades toward neutral, reaching grey for a seed with no chroma at all — see theme_from_seed.

### `theme_light`

```odin
theme_light :: proc() -> Theme
```

### `theme_high_contrast`

```odin
theme_high_contrast :: proc(mode: Theme_Mode) -> Theme
```

### `theme_from_seed`

```odin
theme_from_seed :: proc(accent: Color, mode: Theme_Mode, contrast: Theme_Contrast = .Normal, name := "") -> (th: Theme)
```

theme_from_seed builds a complete theme from one accent colour: neutrals are tinted with the accent's hue at very low chroma (no pure black or white anywhere — comfortable on the eyes and consistent with the accent), surfaces step up in perceptual lightness per elevation tier, the accent is toned for the mode (lighter and softer on dark ground, deeper on light), and every text/background pair is pushed to at least AAA (7:1) for body text and AA (4.5:1) for secondary text and controls, so a theme made from any seed is readable by construction. Every value is in OKLCH, so the same recipe gives a matching family across hues.  A seed with (almost) no chroma is a choice of no colour, and gives a neutral theme: the accent, its container, the focus ring and the selection come out grey, and the neutrals take no tint. Danger, warning and success keep their own hues, and so does the code palette, because those colours carry meaning rather than identity.

### `A11y_Prefs`

```odin
A11y_Prefs :: struct {
	reduce_motion:       bool, // transitions off
	reduce_transparency: bool, // glass becomes opaque surfaces
	text_scale:          f32,  // the desktop's text size: 1 normal, 1.5 = "large text" (0 is read as 1)
}
```

A11y_Prefs are the desktop's accessibility preferences, which every theme follows whoever built it.

### `TEXT_SCALE_MIN`

```odin
TEXT_SCALE_MIN :: f32(0.5)
```

TEXT_SCALE_MIN / TEXT_SCALE_MAX bound what a desktop can ask for, so a stray setting cannot make text unreadable or unlayoutable.

### `text_scale_of`

```odin
text_scale_of :: proc "contextless" (p: A11y_Prefs) -> f32
```

text_scale_of is the multiplier to draw text at: the desktop's, clamped.

### `set_theme`

```odin
set_theme :: proc(t: ^Tree, th: Theme)
```

set_theme swaps the tree's theme. The desktop's accessibility preferences are folded in and stay current (a preference turned off gives the theme back as set), and a theme the app sets stays through the desktop's light / dark changes until `follow_system_theme` gives it back. Builders resolve tokens when they run, so the app's view must be re-run afterwards (the run loop does this); paint-only tokens (focus ring, state layers) take effect immediately.  `App.adjust_theme` runs over it, as over every other theme the app runs with, so an app's own type scale or radii survive a theme picked in its settings.

### `refresh_theme`

```odin
refresh_theme :: proc(t: ^Tree)
```

refresh_theme runs the app's `adjust_theme` over the current theme again, from the theme as it was *before* the last adjust — because the app's adjust has new inputs.  Call it when something `adjust_theme` reads of the app's own — a text size in its settings, a density, a chosen font — has changed; it works whether the tree is pinned or following the desktop.  It starts from the unadjusted theme, not the running one: an adjust run over its own output compounds (a size times 1.2 becomes 1.44, then 1.73).  The view must be re-run afterwards, as after `set_theme`; the run loop does this.

### `follow_system_theme`

```odin
follow_system_theme :: proc(t: ^Tree)
```

follow_system_theme puts the tree back on the desktop's theme after `set_theme` pinned one, and lets the desktop's light / dark changes reach it again: the System choice beside Light and Dark in an appearance setting.  The view must be re-run afterwards, as after `set_theme`; the run loop does this. Harmless on a tree that was already following.

### `set_a11y_prefs`

```odin
set_a11y_prefs :: proc(t: ^Tree, p: A11y_Prefs)
```

set_a11y_prefs swaps the accessibility preferences this tree runs with — text size, reduce motion, reduce transparency — for a desktop that owns them and would rather say than be asked. They are folded into the theme the same way the desktop's are, they reach a size the app set itself as well as the theme's roles, and once set the desktop's preferences leave this tree alone (as a theme the app sets does). `App.a11y` says it for every surface at start-up. Builders resolve sizes when they run, so the view must be re-run afterwards; the run loop does this.

### `follow_system_a11y`

```odin
follow_system_a11y :: proc(t: ^Tree)
```

follow_system_a11y puts the tree back on the desktop's accessibility preferences after `set_a11y_prefs` pinned the app's, and lets the desktop's changes — text size, reduce motion, reduce transparency — reach it again. It is `follow_system_theme`'s counterpart; an app that offers "use the desktop's appearance" as a setting needs both.  The desktop's preferences as they are *now* are installed, not as they were when the app pinned its own, so a change made meanwhile is not missed. The view must be re-run afterwards, as after `set_a11y_prefs`; the run loop does this. Harmless on a tree that was already following.

### `a11y_prefs`

```odin
a11y_prefs :: proc(t: ^Tree) -> A11y_Prefs
```

a11y_prefs is the accessibility preferences in force: the desktop's, or the app's where it set them with `set_a11y_prefs`. Read it before changing one of them, so the other two are carried over rather than quietly switched off.  `text_scale` here is the **desktop's** factor. An app's own text-size setting belongs in `set_text_scale`, which multiplies this rather than replacing it.

### `set_text_scale`

```odin
set_text_scale :: proc(t: ^Tree, scale: f32)
```

set_text_scale is the app's own text size — an S / M / L / XL setting in its preferences — **multiplied** with the desktop's, not replacing it. 1 (or 0) is no scaling of its own.  Multiplying keeps the desktop's Large Text an accessibility setting the app cannot take away: its 1.5 and the app's 1.25 compose, as a zoom does. The product is clamped to TEXT_SCALE_MIN…TEXT_SCALE_MAX, so neither setting can make the other unreadable. Use it, not `set_a11y_prefs`, for a text size alone: that takes all three preferences over and stops the desktop's reaching the tree, while here reduce-motion and reduce-transparency keep following the desktop.

### `text_scale`

```odin
text_scale :: proc(t: ^Tree) -> f32
```

text_scale is the factor text is actually drawn at: the desktop's and the app's own together, clamped. What `set_text_scale` asked for is not always what comes back.

### `mix`

```odin
mix :: proc "contextless" (a, b: Color, t: f32) -> Color
```

mix blends a toward b by t (linear space).

### `Theme_Palette`

```odin
Theme_Palette :: struct {
	bg:               Color, // the window
	surface:          Color, // views and cards
	surface_variant:  Color, // sidebars and wells
	surface_high:     Color, // popovers
	on_bg:            Color, // text on the window
	on_surface:       Color, // text on a view
	on_surface_muted: Color, // secondary text
	outline:          Color, // borders
	primary:          Color, // the accent, as a fill
	on_primary:       Color, // text on the accent
	accent_text:      Color, // the accent as ink (a link, a ghost button's label); unset = primary
}
```

Theme_Palette names the neutral roles outright, for a style whose colours are a design in their own right rather than something to generate — a desktop that reproduces a known look. Every field is optional: a colour left zero is the generated theme's own, so a palette can give as little as a window and a view colour. `primary` is the accent as a **fill** (a selection, a primary button) and `on_primary` the text on it; the rest are the neutrals, in the order they stack.  `accent_text` is the accent as **ink** — a ghost button's label, a breadcrumb, a link, an unread count. Left unset it is `primary` lifted until it clears 4.5:1 on the view. Set it for a style whose accent cannot be read as text at any lightness (a grey selection colour is right as a fill and dim as ink); `primary` is kept exactly as given either way.

### `theme_from_palette`

```odin
theme_from_palette :: proc(p: Theme_Palette, mode: Theme_Mode, contrast: Theme_Contrast = .Normal, name := "") -> (th: Theme)
```

theme_from_palette builds a complete theme from colours given outright, for a style whose palette is the design — the exact greys of a desktop theme being reproduced — rather than one generated from an accent (`theme_from_seed`).  What the palette names is taken as asked. What it leaves out is derived from what it gives, as `theme_on_ground` does: the accent's container is mixed toward the window, the secondary ground steps from the surface toward the ink, the subtler outline toward the surface, and the focus ring and selection are the accent. The hover and press washes, syntax palette, shadows, glass materials and fills are computed afresh for these colours. Danger, warning and success are the generated theme's, since they mean the same thing on any ground.  Readability still wins: every pair that carries text goes through `ensure_contrast`, so a palette whose muted text falls short comes back with that one colour moved. **Under High contrast the palette is not used at all** and the generated theme comes back: a mid-grey window cannot carry 10:1 text. With a neutral accent that theme is itself grey.  `mode` is the caller's and is never inferred from the colours: a reproduced dark window may be lighter than Baldr's own Dim one and is still Dark. The ladder is the palette's business too — a style whose views sit *darker* than their window is saying so. For a style with no glass, call `theme_opaque` on what comes back.

### `theme_from_system`

```odin
theme_from_system :: proc(p: platform.System_Palette, high_contrast: bool, accent: Color = {}) -> Theme
```
