Magnified, by default it goes crisp on its own: smooth up to 2 device pixels a picture pixel, hard-edged squares from 4, crossfaded between, so a zoom sharpens as it goes in instead of popping at a threshold each app would pick. `pixelated = true` forces the hard squares (a pixel editor's document), `pixelated = false` the smooth filter; drawn smaller than it is, it is smooth either way. `pixel_grid` draws a 1-pixel line between the picture's pixels, fading in from 8 device pixels each and fully there from 24.

### `image_forget`

```odin
image_forget :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), img: ^Image)
```

image_forget tells the toolkit that `img` is about to be freed, so every node still showing it lets go at once and draws the copy the atlas kept until the next build replaces it.  Otherwise an Image may be freed once the view stops showing it: the build that drops it releases the node, and a message always rebuilds every view before anything paints. Freeing in `update`, before that build, needs this: a `component` whose revision did not change is not rebuilt and keeps pointing at the Image. The Image must also be gone from wherever the view reads it, or the build hands the freed pointer straight back. It costs a walk of the image nodes, not the tree; an Image nothing shows is a no-op.  audio_of_the_cache_entry_went_away(s) baldr.image_forget(ui, img)   // or from `update`, through the Ui you hold baldr.image_destroy(img) free(img)

### `forget_image`

```odin
forget_image :: proc($Msg: typeid, img: ^Image) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

forget_image is `image_forget` as a command, for the usual case: an app that frees pictures in `update`, where there is no `^Ui` to hand and where several surfaces may each be showing the same one.  case Cache_Cleared: cmds := make([dynamic]Cmd(Msg), context.temp_allocator) for img in s.dropped do append(&cmds, baldr.forget_image(Msg, img)) return baldr.batch(Msg, cmds[:])  It lets every live surface go of the picture and then destroys it, so the app does not call `image_destroy` itself. It runs during message dispatch, before any view is built.  The `^Image` box is still the app's to free, but not in the `update` that returns this command: the Cmd holds the pointer until dispatch runs it. Free it on a later turn (keep a list, free it at the top of the next sync), or not at all: the struct is small and `image_destroy` has freed everything it pointed at.

### `image_contain_rect`

```odin
image_contain_rect :: proc(box: Rect, img_w, img_h: int) -> Rect
```

image_contain_rect is the largest rect of the picture's aspect that fits centred in `box` (the Contain fit).

## widget_icon.odin

icon draws one of the vendored Lucide icons (icons_data.odin) at `size` logical px in `color` (the text colour by default). Decorative by default (no accessibility node); give it a `label` to make it an Image with a name. Buttons, menu items and table cells take an `icon` argument and place it themselves.

### `icon`

```odin
icon :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), which: Icon, size: f32 = 0, color: Color = {}, label: string = "",
	key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

## widget_custom.odin

A widget of the app's own. `custom` is a node that sizes and paints itself; it takes the pointer through the modifiers any node takes (clickable, pointer_area, pointer_track, hoverable, draggable, drop_target, tooltip) and, while it has focus, the keys it names. It paints with the theme in force, so it follows light, dark, contrast and a theme_area like the toolkit's own widgets.

```odin
stars :: proc(p: ^baldr.Painter) {
	value := (^int)(p.user)^
	for i in 0 ..< 5 {
		ink := p.theme.color.primary if i < value else p.theme.color.outline_variant
		baldr.draw_icon(p, .Star, {p.rect.x + f32(i) * 24, p.rect.y + 2}, 20, ink)
	}
}
n := baldr.custom(ui, stars, &s.rating, width = 120, height = 24, label = "Rating",
	role = .Slider, focusable = true, keys = {.Left, .Right}, on_key = on_rating_key)
baldr.clickable(ui, n, Msg.Rate)
```

The view is built again after a message, and a custom widget repaints every time it is built, so drawing from the app's state needs nothing more. What it draws is cached with its layer until then.

### `Custom_Paint`

```odin
Custom_Paint :: #type proc(p: ^Painter)
```

Custom_Paint draws a custom widget. Draw only through the painter's procs.

### `Custom_Measure`

```odin
Custom_Measure :: #type proc(user: rawptr, room: [2]f32) -> [2]f32
```

Custom_Measure is a custom widget's size given the room it is offered, logical px; an axis with no limit is UNBOUNDED. A `width` or `height` the widget was built with still wins on its axis, as on any node; without a measure it is that size, or its share of `flex`.

### `Painter`

```odin
Painter :: struct {
	rect:     Rect,
	theme:    ^Theme,
	user:     rawptr,
	hovered:  bool,
	pressed:  bool,
	focused:  bool,
	disabled: bool, // the painter dims what it draws already
	tree:     ^Tree,
	node:     Node,
	layer:    ^Layer,
}
```

Painter is what a Custom_Paint draws with: where (`rect`, absolute logical px), in which theme, and the node's state.

### `custom`

```odin
custom :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), paint: Custom_Paint, user: rawptr = nil, measure: Custom_Measure = nil,
	width: f32 = 0, height: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0, label: string = "", role: Role = .None, focusable := false,
	keys: []Key = nil, on_key: Handler(Msg, Chord) = nil, tag: u64 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

custom builds a widget of the app's own (see the top of this file). `user` is handed to `paint` and `measure` untouched and must outlive the node. `label` and `role` are what assistive technology is told; a focusable widget with no role is announced as a Group, so give it one. `on_key` hears the `keys` it names while the widget has focus; any other key goes on to the window.

### `draw_rect`

```odin
draw_rect :: proc(p: ^Painter, r: Rect, fill: Color, radius: f32 = 0, border: Color = {}, border_width: f32 = 0)
```

draw_rect fills `r`, rounded by `radius`, with an optional border drawn inside its edge.

### `draw_line`

```odin
draw_line :: proc(p: ^Painter, a, b: [2]f32, width: f32, color: Color)
```

draw_line draws a straight line from `a` to `b` with round ends.

### `draw_circle`

```odin
draw_circle :: proc(p: ^Painter, centre: [2]f32, radius: f32, fill: Color, border: Color = {}, border_width: f32 = 0)
```

draw_circle fills a circle of `radius` around `centre`, with an optional ring drawn inside its edge: a dot, a knob, a node's port.

### `draw_polyline`

```odin
draw_polyline :: proc(p: ^Painter, points: [][2]f32, width: f32, color: Color)
```

draw_polyline joins `points` with straight segments of `width`, round at the joins and the ends. An opaque colour reads as one line; a translucent one is darker where two segments meet.

### `draw_arc`

```odin
draw_arc :: proc(p: ^Painter, centre: [2]f32, radius, from, to, width: f32, color: Color)
```

draw_arc strokes the part of the circle of `radius` around `centre` from angle `from` to `to`, in radians clockwise from three o'clock (y grows down): a gauge, a progress ring. Segments of about 3 px read as a curve.

### `draw_bezier`

```odin
draw_bezier :: proc(p: ^Painter, a, c1, c2, b: [2]f32, width: f32, color: Color)
```

draw_bezier strokes the cubic Bézier from `a` to `b` pulled towards `c1` and `c2`: a node graph's wire. Segments of about 3 px read as a curve.

### `draw_icon`

```odin
draw_icon :: proc(p: ^Painter, icon: Icon, pos: [2]f32, size: f32, color: Color)
```

draw_icon draws a Lucide icon `size` px square with its top-left at `pos`.

### `draw_text`

```odin
draw_text :: proc(p: ^Painter, pos: [2]f32, s: string, size: f32 = 0, color: Color = {}, weight := Font_Weight.Regular,
	mono := false, wrap_width: f32 = 0) -> [2]f32
```

draw_text draws `s` with its first line's top-left at `pos` and returns the size it takes. `size` 0 is the theme's body size; `wrap_width` > 0 wraps it. The string is copied, so a temporary one is fine.

### `draw_text_size`

```odin
draw_text_size :: proc(p: ^Painter, s: string, size: f32 = 0, weight := Font_Weight.Regular, mono := false, wrap_width: f32 = 0) -> [2]f32
```

draw_text_size is the size `s` would take, without drawing it.

### `draw_clip`

```odin
draw_clip :: proc(p: ^Painter, r: Rect)
```

draw_clip limits what is drawn after it to `r`, until draw_unclip.

### `draw_unclip`

```odin
draw_unclip :: proc(p: ^Painter)
```

draw_unclip ends the innermost draw_clip.

## canvas.odin

Canvases: textures an app draws into and shows, for an image editor's layers and tiles, a viewer's filters, a sketch pad. Three bit depths (RGBA8, RGBA16, RGBA32F), a colour space on every canvas, and Baldr's own operations on them, each with a CPU reference.

An app gets its Gpu with the `gpu_open` command, once the device is up, and keeps it for the run. canvas_create makes a canvas; canvas_write, canvas_fill and canvas_copy are work on canvases, queued in the order they are asked for and run on the GPU after the update that asked (before any frame shows them: the loop flushes after handling messages); `canvas_read` is a command whose outcome, the texels, arrives as a message. The `canvas` node shows one, as `image` shows a picture, its values decoded from its colour space for the screen (sRGB for now).

Behind the same calls is a reference that runs on the CPU (gpu_reference): what the GPU's work must match, the headless tests' oracle, and the fallback where there is no GPU. Its canvases are drawn as pictures.

Everything here runs on the UI thread (update and view).

### `Canvas_Format`

```odin
Canvas_Format :: gpu.Canvas_Format
```

Canvas_Format is how a canvas stores a texel: 8 or 16 bits a channel (unsigned, 0..1), or 32-bit float (HDR).

### `Color_Space`

```odin
Color_Space :: gpu.Color_Space
```

Color_Space is what a canvas's values mean: sRGB, Display P3 or Adobe RGB (1998) values encoded by the space's own curve, or Linear light with sRGB's primaries.

### `Canvas`

```odin
Canvas :: distinct u32
```

Canvas names a canvas of a Gpu. 0 is none.

### `Gpu`

```odin
Gpu :: struct {
	host: ^Canvas_Host,
}
```

Gpu is an app's way to its canvases: from `gpu_open`, valid for the run. Copy it freely; its fields are Baldr's.

### `Gpu_Limits`

```odin
Gpu_Limits :: struct {
	max_side:      int,
	device_memory: u64,
	formats:       bit_set[Canvas_Format],
	filtered:      bit_set[Canvas_Format],
	storage:       bit_set[Canvas_Format], // a compute shader can write (canvas_shader)
	on_gpu:        bool,
}
```

Gpu_Limits is what canvases this Gpu can have: the largest side, the device memory, the formats it makes canvases in, those it filters (the rest are drawn with the nearest texel) and those a compute shader can write, and whether it is the GPU or the CPU reference.

### `Canvas_Read`

```odin
Canvas_Read :: struct {
	canvas:     Canvas,
	x, y, w, h: int,
	format:     Canvas_Format,
	pixels:     []u8,
	ok:         bool,
}
```

Canvas_Read is a read's outcome: the rectangle's texels in the canvas's format, rows tightly packed (little-endian 16-bit and 32-bit float channels). The bytes live only while the message is handled: copy what you keep. `ok` is false when the canvas was gone or the read failed.

### `Canvas_Host`

struct

Canvas_Host holds a Gpu's canvases; its fields are Baldr's.

### `gpu_open`

```odin
gpu_open :: proc($Msg: typeid, on_done: Handler(Msg, Gpu)) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

gpu_open asks for the app's Gpu: the handler gets it once the device is up (when the first surface has been made), or at once with the CPU reference where there is no GPU. Every ask gets the same one.  start = proc(s: ^State) -> baldr.Cmd(Msg) { return baldr.gpu_open(Msg, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Gpu)) -> Msg { return Gpu_Ready{e.value} }) },

### `gpu_reference`

```odin
gpu_reference :: proc(allocator := context.allocator) -> Gpu
```

gpu_reference is a Gpu that runs on the CPU, for a headless test or a tool with no window. The caller's: gpu_close.

### `gpu_close`

```odin
gpu_close :: proc(g: ^Gpu)
```

gpu_close frees a Gpu from gpu_reference and every canvas it holds.

### `gpu_limits`

```odin
gpu_limits :: proc(g: Gpu) -> Gpu_Limits
```

gpu_limits says what canvases `g` can have.

### `canvas_create`

```odin
canvas_create :: proc(g: Gpu, size: [2]int, format := Canvas_Format.RGBA8, space := Color_Space.sRGB, mips := true) -> (c: Canvas, ok: bool)
```

canvas_create makes a `size` canvas of `format` whose values are in `space`, clear (every texel 0). False when the Gpu cannot make it: a side not positive or past gpu_limits, a format it lacks, no memory.  `.R8` and `.R16` hold one channel of coverage (a selection, a mask, a stroke); they have no colour space and read as a texel of (r, 0, 0, 1). `mips = false` gives one level, for a canvas never shown scaled down (a scratch, an undo copy, a layer under the composite): a mip chain costs a third more memory and a rebuild over the touched rect after every batch.

### `canvas_valid`

```odin
canvas_valid :: proc(g: Gpu, c: Canvas) -> bool
```

canvas_valid says whether `c` is still a canvas of `g`. It stops being one when it is destroyed, and when the GPU is reset — canvases live in VRAM and have no copy, so a reset takes them (App.gpu_reset).

### `gpu_renderer`

```odin
gpu_renderer :: proc(g: Gpu) -> ^gpu.Renderer
```

gpu_renderer is the renderer behind a Gpu, or nil for the CPU reference. For tools that have to speak to the device itself — a recovery probe, a profiler — not for drawing.

### `canvas_destroy`

```odin
canvas_destroy :: proc(g: Gpu, c: Canvas)
```

canvas_destroy frees a canvas; its queued work goes with it. On the GPU it waits for the device to be idle: not for every frame.

### `canvas_info`

```odin
canvas_info :: proc(g: Gpu, c: Canvas) -> (size: [2]int, format: Canvas_Format, space: Color_Space, ok: bool)
```

canvas_info is a canvas's size, format and colour space; false when it is not one of `g`'s.

### `canvas_write`

```odin
canvas_write :: proc(g: Gpu, c: Canvas, x, y, w, h: int, pixels: []u8) -> bool
```

canvas_write puts `pixels` (w × h texels of the canvas's format, rows tightly packed) at x, y. The bytes are copied now. False for a rectangle outside the canvas, too few bytes, or no such canvas.

### `canvas_write_begin`

```odin
canvas_write_begin :: proc(g: Gpu, c: Canvas, x, y, w, h: int) -> ([]u8, bool)
```

canvas_write_begin hands back the bytes to fill for the rectangle at x, y — w × h texels of the canvas's format, rows tightly packed — instead of copying from the app's own buffer, and canvas_write_end finishes the write. Use it where pixels are made a frame at a time (a video frame): it saves the copy through the app's own buffer.  ```odin if px, ok := baldr.canvas_write_begin(g, c, 0, 0, w, h); ok { decode_into(px)                       // or copy from a helper's memory baldr.canvas_write_end(g, c) } ```  The memory is the toolkit's and the slice is valid until canvas_write_end, after which the GPU owns those bytes and a write through it lands in a copy in flight: never free it or keep it across an update. The write is queued where it is asked for, so operations keep their order; one may be open at a time. Nil and false for a rectangle outside the canvas, no such canvas, a write already open, or no memory.

### `canvas_write_end`

```odin
canvas_write_end :: proc(g: Gpu, c: Canvas) -> bool
```

canvas_write_end finishes the write canvas_write_begin opened on `c`, and the slice it handed over is spent. False when no write was open on that canvas.

### `canvas_fill`

```odin
canvas_fill :: proc(g: Gpu, c: Canvas, x, y, w, h: int, value: [4]f32) -> bool
```

canvas_fill sets the rectangle's texels to `value`, as stored: 0..1 for RGBA8 and RGBA16 (clamped, rounded to the nearest step), any float for RGBA32F. False as canvas_write.

### `canvas_copy`

```odin
canvas_copy :: proc(g: Gpu, src: Canvas, x, y, w, h: int, dst: Canvas, to: [2]int) -> bool
```

canvas_copy copies w × h texels at x, y of `src` to `to` in `dst`. The values keep their meaning across formats (an 8-bit 255 is a 16-bit 65535; floats past 0..1 are clamped into an unsigned format); colour spaces are not converted. Within one canvas the rectangles must not overlap. False for a rectangle outside either canvas, overlapping ones, or no canvas.

### `canvas_read`

```odin
canvas_read :: proc($Msg: typeid, g: Gpu, c: Canvas, x, y, w, h: int, on_done: Handler(Msg, Canvas_Read), tag: u64 = 0) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

canvas_read reads a rectangle of a canvas back; the handler gets Canvas_Read (with `tag`) once the GPU has done the work queued before it. For saving, exporting, sampling a colour.

### `canvas_read_now`

```odin
canvas_read_now :: proc(g: Gpu, c: Canvas, x, y, w, h: int, allocator := context.allocator) -> (pixels: []u8, ok: bool)
```

canvas_read_now reads a rectangle straight away, into `allocator`: the CPU reference's canvases only (a headless tool, a test). False on the GPU, whose reads come back as messages (canvas_read), and for a rectangle outside the canvas.

### `color_space_to_linear`

```odin
color_space_to_linear :: proc(rgb: [3]f32, space: Color_Space) -> [3]f32
```

color_space_to_linear is a colour's values in `space` as linear light with sRGB's primaries: the display transform the shader applies to a canvas (ui.frag canvas_decode), here for the reference and the tests. Colours outside sRGB come out below 0 or above 1.

### `canvas`

```odin
canvas :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), g: Gpu, c: Canvas, width: f32 = 0, height: f32 = 0, radius: f32 = 0, label: string = "",
	fit: Image_Fit = .Cover, source: Rect = {}, pixelated: Maybe(bool) = nil, pixel_grid := false, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

canvas shows canvas `c` of `g` scaled into `width` × `height`, as `image` shows a picture: one side given keeps its aspect, `fit` places it, and `source` is the part shown in its texels ({}: all; changing it is a repaint, as a zoom or a pan is). Its values are decoded from its colour space for the screen. Decorative unless `label` names it (role Image): an editor names its document.  Magnified, it goes crisp on its own by default: smooth up to 2 device px a texel, hard-edged from 4, crossfaded between. `pixelated = true` forces hard texels, `false` the smooth filter. `pixel_grid` draws a 1-pixel line between texels, fading in from 8 device px a texel, full from 24. Layout sizes it like any node, so one asked for larger than its parent allows is squeezed and looks blurry; to show part of a large canvas, pass `source` at the node's real size.

## job.odin

Background work. `job` runs a proc on a thread of its own; the proc gets a Job to `post` messages through (progress, results) and asks `cancelled` whether to stop; when it returns, `on_done` becomes a message. Every message reaches `update` on the main thread, so a worker never touches State and the view never waits on one. Posting wakes the run loop, which otherwise sleeps.

### `Job`

```odin
Job :: struct($Msg: typeid) {
	data:     rawptr,
	tag:      u64,
	mailbox:  ^Mailbox(Msg),
	on_done:  Handler(Msg, Job_Done),
	work:     proc(^Job(Msg)),
	thread:   ^thread.Thread,
	stop:     bool, // set by `cancel`, read by `cancelled` (atomically)
	finished: bool, // the work proc returned (atomically)
}
```

Job is a background job's handle, made by the runtime for the `job` command and given to the work proc. `data` is what the command passed; `tag` is echoed in the done message.

### `Job_Done`

```odin
Job_Done :: struct {
	cancelled: bool,
}
```

Job_Done is a job's outcome: whether it was cancelled before the work returned. Results travel in the messages the job posted.

### `Mailbox`

struct

Mailbox carries messages from worker threads to the run loop.

### `job`

```odin
job :: proc($Msg: typeid, work: proc(^Job(Msg)), data: rawptr = nil, on_done: Handler(Msg, Job_Done) = nil, tag: u64 = 0) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

job runs `work` on a thread of its own. The worker posts progress and results as messages and returns when finished, or when `cancelled` says to stop; `on_done` (optional) then becomes a message carrying Job_Done and the job's `tag`. `data` is handed to the worker untouched: give it something the worker owns (and frees), never a pointer into State.  case Start: return baldr.job(Msg, transfer, new_clone(Req{url = v.url}), on_done = Msg(Finished{}), tag = v.id)

### `cancel`

```odin
cancel :: proc($Msg: typeid, tag: u64) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

cancel asks every running job with `tag` to stop: their `cancelled` turns true, and each one's done message reports it once the worker returns. A worker that never asks runs to its end.

### `batch`

```odin
batch :: proc($Msg: typeid, cmds: ..Cmd(Msg)) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

batch runs several commands from one update, in order.  return baldr.batch(Msg, baldr.job(Msg, transfer, a, tag = 1), baldr.job(Msg, transfer, b, tag = 2))

### `post`

```odin
post :: proc(j: ^Job($Msg), m: Msg)
```

post queues `m` for update from the worker thread and wakes the run loop. The message is copied; anything it points to must stay valid until update has handled it (heap-allocate strings and let update own them).

### `Poster`

```odin
Poster :: struct($Msg: typeid) {
	box: ^Poster_Box(Msg),
}
```

Poster is a way into the run loop for code Baldr did not start: a C library calling back on its own thread (libmpv's wakeup, sd-bus, PipeWire). `post_from` queues a message from any thread and wakes the loop, as a job's `post` does — no polling job, no latency. Get one with the `poster` command. It is valid for the life of the process: a callback firing after the run has ended (between `run` returning and the library being shut down) posts nowhere, safely, so its few bytes are never freed.

### `poster`

```odin
poster :: proc($Msg: typeid, on_done: Handler(Msg, Poster(Msg))) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

poster makes a Poster and delivers it through `on_done`: keep it in State and hand it (as user data) to the library that calls back.  case Started: return baldr.poster(Msg, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Poster(Msg))) -> Msg { return Got_Poster{e.value} }) // on mpv's thread:  baldr.post_from(p, Msg(Mpv_Event{}))

### `post_from`

```odin
post_from :: proc(p: Poster($Msg), m: Msg) -> bool
```

post_from queues `m` for update from any thread and wakes the run loop. The message is copied; what it points to must stay valid until update has handled it. False when the run has ended (nothing is delivered then) or the Poster is the zero value.

### `cancelled`

```odin
cancelled :: proc(j: ^Job($Msg)) -> bool
```

cancelled is true once `cancel` was asked for the job or the app is quitting; a worker checks it between steps and returns.

## sched.odin

Time and subscriptions. `delay` is a command: one message after N milliseconds. Subscriptions are declared from state, like surfaces: `every` sends a message on an interval and `watch` reports changes to a path; the runtime starts and stops them as the declared list changes after each update, keeping a timer's phase when its line stays. Due times feed the run loop's wait, so nothing polls.

### `Sub_Kind`

```odin
Sub_Kind :: enum u8 { None, Every, Watch, Outputs, Toplevels, Workspaces, Frames }
```

Sub_Kind: what a subscription is.

### `Sub`

```odin
Sub :: struct($Msg: typeid) {
	kind:      Sub_Kind,
	ms:        i64,                       // Every
	msg:       Msg,                       // Every, Outputs, Toplevels, Workspaces: what is sent
	path:      string,                    // Watch
	on_change: Handler(Msg, File_Change), // Watch
	on_frame:  Handler(Msg, Frame_Time),  // Frames
	tag:       u64,
}
```

Sub is one line of an app's `subscriptions` list: `every(Msg, ms, msg)` or `watch(Msg, path, on_change)`. Two lines with the same kind, timing or path and `tag` are the same subscription across updates.

### `Change_Kind`

```odin
Change_Kind :: platform.Watch_Kind
```

Change_Kind: what happened under a watched path.

### `File_Change`

```odin
File_Change :: struct {
	path: string,
	name: string,
	kind: Change_Kind,
}
```

File_Change is what a watch reports: the watched `path`, the entry `name` inside it when the path is a directory ("" for the path itself), and what happened. Strings live only while the message is handled — clone what you keep.

### `outputs_changed`

```odin
outputs_changed :: proc($Msg: typeid, msg: Msg, tag: u64 = 0) -> Sub(Msg)
```

outputs_changed sends `msg` whenever a screen is added, removed or changes; `outputs()` then has the new set.  append(out, baldr.outputs_changed(Msg, Screens_Changed{}))

### `delay`

```odin
delay :: proc($Msg: typeid, ms: i64, msg: Msg) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

delay sends `msg` once, `ms` milliseconds from now.  case Saved: s.notice = "Saved"; return baldr.delay(Msg, 2000, Hide_Notice{})

### `every`

```odin
every :: proc($Msg: typeid, ms: i64, msg: Msg, tag: u64 = 0) -> Sub(Msg)
```

every sends `msg` each `ms` milliseconds while the line stays declared. A blocked app gets one message when it wakes, not a burst.

### `Frame_Time`

```odin
Frame_Time :: struct {
	now_ms:     f64,
	present_ms: f64,
	refresh_ms: f64,
	estimated:  bool,
}
```

Frame_Time is when a frame will be seen. `present_ms` is the moment the frame the app is about to draw is expected on screen; `now_ms` is the toolkit's clock as it was told; `refresh_ms` is the display's interval.  `estimated` is true when the compositor does not report presentation times and these were worked out from the frame callback and the refresh rate instead. An app tuning motion should know which it has.

### `frames`

```odin
frames :: proc($Msg: typeid, on_frame: Handler(Msg, Frame_Time), tag: u64 = 0) -> Sub(Msg)
```

frames sends a message for every frame the display is about to show, while the line stays declared, with the time that frame is expected on screen. It is for anything that must keep in step with the display (a video player, an animation driven from outside the toolkit), which a timer cannot: nothing ties `every(16)` to the screen. It paints at the screen's rate for as long as it is declared, where Baldr otherwise draws only when something changed, so declare it only while it is needed; a player drops the line when it pauses.  if s.playing do append(out, baldr.frames(Msg, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Frame_Time)) -> Msg { return Tick{e.value.present_ms} }))

### `watch`

```odin
watch :: proc($Msg: typeid, path: string, on_change: Handler(Msg, File_Change), tag: u64 = 0) -> Sub(Msg)
```

watch reports changes to `path` while the line stays declared: a file (also when it is replaced by a save-and-rename), or a directory and its entries. The handler gets File_Change with the sub's `tag`. Linux (inotify) today; a backend without watches reports nothing.  baldr.watch(Msg, s.path, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.File_Change)) -> Msg { return Reload{} })

## shortcut.odin

Window-level keyboard shortcuts. A view declares them like anything else it builds; a key the focused widget does not use is matched against them and the message goes to update. A menu item's `shortcut` text shows the binding; `shortcut(ui, …)` is what makes it work.

### `Shortcut`

```odin
Shortcut :: struct($Msg: typeid) {
	key:  Key,
	mods: Mods,
	msg:  Msg,
}
```

Shortcut is one binding of the latest view.

### `shortcut`

```odin
shortcut :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), keys: string, msg: Msg, loc := #caller_location)
```

shortcut binds `keys` to `msg` for the window while the view declares it: "Ctrl+N", "Ctrl+Shift+N", "F2", "Delete", "Alt+Left", "Ctrl++". Modifiers are Ctrl, Shift, Alt and Super (also Cmd / Meta / Win); the key is a letter, digit, F-key, arrow or a name such as Delete, Enter, Escape, Space, Home, Page_Up, Comma. A key the focused widget uses (Delete in a text field, Ctrl+C in an editor) stays with the widget, and so does a character being typed: a binding on a printable key with no Ctrl, Alt or Super ("/", "R", "Shift+/") waits while the focus is a text field, an editable text area, a time picker taking digits or a view with type-ahead. Nothing fires while a modal dialog is open. A string that does not parse is reported as a build diagnostic and binds nothing.  baldr.shortcut(ui, "F2", Rename{}) baldr.shortcut(ui, "Ctrl+Shift+N", New_Folder{})

### `shortcut_bound`

```odin
shortcut_bound :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), key: Key, mods: Mods) -> bool
```

shortcut_bound says whether the latest view bound `key` + `mods`: a toolkit chord that is also a common command (Ctrl+X picking an item up for a keyboard drag) yields to the app's own binding of it.

### `shortcut_fire`

```odin
shortcut_fire :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), key: Key, pressed: bool, mods: Mods) -> bool
```

shortcut_fire sends the message bound to `key` + `mods`, if any. Called by the run loop for a key press no widget consumed.

### `parse_shortcut`

```odin
parse_shortcut :: proc(s: string) -> (key: Key, mods: Mods, ok: bool)
```

parse_shortcut reads "Ctrl+Shift+F2" into a key and its modifiers.

## clipboard.odin

The clipboard as commands. `copy_text`, `copy_files` and `copy_html` put something on it; `paste` reads it and the outcome is a message. Files travel as text/uri-list, as the form GNOME's file managers paste (which says whether they were cut) and KDE's cut marker, and as the paths in plain text, so a file copied or cut here pastes in a file manager and in a terminal alike. The text fields and editors use the clipboard on their own for Ctrl+C / X / V.

Pictures: `copy_image` offers a picture as image/png, and `paste_image` reads the best picture form on offer. Neither makes the UI wait: the PNG is encoded on a thread from the moment of the copy, and a program that pastes before it is ready is written it when it is; a paste is read and decoded on a thread, and arrives as a message.

### `Pasted`

```odin
Pasted :: struct {
	text:  string,
	files: []string,
	cut:   bool,
}
```

Pasted is what `paste` found: the text form, the local paths when files were on the clipboard, and whether they were cut (the paste should move them). The strings live only while the message is handled — clone what you keep.

### `copy_text`

```odin
copy_text :: proc($Msg: typeid, text: string, primary := false) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

copy_text puts `text` on the clipboard; with `primary`, makes it the primary selection instead — what was last selected, which a middle click pastes (a terminal sets it as a selection is made). A platform without a primary selection does nothing with that.

### `copy_files`

```odin
copy_files :: proc($Msg: typeid, paths: []string, cut := false) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

copy_files puts local `paths` on the clipboard as files, with the paths as plain text beside them. `cut` marks them cut: a file manager pasting them moves them.  case Copy: return baldr.copy_files(Msg, selected_paths(s, context.temp_allocator)) case Cut:  return baldr.copy_files(Msg, selected_paths(s, context.temp_allocator), cut = true)

### `copy_html`

```odin
copy_html :: proc($Msg: typeid, html: string, text: string) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

copy_html puts formatted text on the clipboard: `html` as text/html and `text` as the plain text, both at once. The program pasting takes the richest form it understands — a word processor takes the HTML, a terminal the text — so `text` should be the text as the person wrote it (the Markdown behind the formatting, say) rather than a flattening of the HTML.  The text goes on in every form `copy_text` offers. On Windows the HTML goes on as CF_HTML, the form word processors there paste. A platform whose clipboard Baldr can only put text on offers the text alone.  Both strings are copied: the app may free them straight after. Nothing happens when both are empty.  case Copy_Formatted: return baldr.copy_html(Msg, html_of(s, context.temp_allocator), s.source)

### `paste`

```odin
paste :: proc($Msg: typeid, on_done: Handler(Msg, Pasted), primary := false) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

paste reads the clipboard; the handler gets Pasted with what was there (every field empty when nothing was). With `primary` it reads the primary selection instead (a middle click's paste): its text only.  case Paste: return baldr.paste(Msg, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Pasted)) -> Msg { return Paste_Files{e.value.files, e.value.cut} }) case Middle_Click: return baldr.paste(Msg, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Pasted)) -> Msg { return Type{e.value.text} }, primary = true)

### `copy_image`

```odin
copy_image :: proc($Msg: typeid, img: ^Image) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

copy_image puts a picture on the clipboard, as image/png: pasted into an image editor, a browser or a chat, it is the picture. Its pixels are copied at once, so the app may change or free `img` straight after; the PNG is made on a thread, and a paste that comes before it is ready waits for it without the app waiting. Nothing happens for an empty picture.  case Copy_Merged: return baldr.copy_image(Msg, &s.flattened)

### `Pasted_Image`

```odin
Pasted_Image :: struct {
	image: Image,
	mime:  string,
	data:  []u8,
	ok:    bool,
}
```

Pasted_Image is what `paste_image` found. `image` is the picture as RGBA8 and is yours: image_destroy it. `mime` is the form it came in, and `data` its bytes as they came (a 16-bit PNG, say, for an app that keeps the depth, or a form Baldr cannot decode, for an app that can). Both live only while the message is handled: clone what you keep. `ok` is false when there was no picture, or one that could not be read or decoded; then `image` is empty and `mime` and `data` say what there was, if anything.

### `paste_image`

```odin
paste_image :: proc($Msg: typeid, on_done: Handler(Msg, Pasted_Image)) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

paste_image reads the picture on the clipboard, in its best form: PNG, then the other lossless ones Odin decodes (BMP, QOI, TGA, the netpbm kinds), then JPEG, then SVG (drawn at its own size). The form is read and decoded on a thread, and the handler gets Pasted_Image. Use a handler proc: a plain message would drop the picture (it is freed).  case Paste: return baldr.paste_image(Msg, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Pasted_Image)) -> Msg { return Pasted_Layer{e.value} })

### `Paste_Prefer`

```odin
Paste_Prefer :: enum u8 { Text, Picture }
```

Paste_Prefer is what an editor takes when the clipboard offers both a picture and text. `.Text`, the default, is what a text editor should do. `.Picture` is for a composer that attaches pictures: copying an image in a browser puts the picture and its URL on the clipboard, so `.Text` pastes the address and loses the image. With `.Picture` the picture wins, the app hears `on_paste_other` and nothing is inserted. Text alone still pastes as text, and a picture alone always reaches `on_paste_other`.

### `Paste_Other`

```odin
Paste_Other :: struct {
	picture: bool,
	files:   bool,
	mimes:   []string,
}
```

Paste_Other is what a paste found when the clipboard held no text to insert: the forms it does hold, so the app can decide what to do with them. `picture` says a picture form is there — answer with `paste_image`. `files` says file paths are — answer with `paste` and read its `files`. Both can be true (a file manager offering a thumbnail as well as paths): the app chooses. `mimes` is every form the clipboard offers, in its own order, for an app that reads a form Baldr does not; it and its strings live only while the message is handled, so clone what you keep.  It reaches the app through `on_paste_other` on `editor_options` (an editor) or on `text_input` (a field). A paste that brought text is untouched: the text is inserted and no message is sent.

## file_io.odin

File IO as commands: `read_file` and `write_file` return at once and the outcome arrives as a message. The work runs on one IO thread with its own core:nbio event loop (io_uring / IOCP / kqueue), started the first time it is needed; where nbio cannot run (io_uring locked down) each operation falls back to a thread of its own with blocking calls. Either way the run loop never waits on a disk.

### `File_Read`

```odin
File_Read :: struct {
	path:  string,
	data:  []u8,
	ok:    bool,
	error: string,
}
```

File_Read is a read's outcome. `data` lives only while the message is handled — clone what you keep. `error` names what failed when not ok.

### `File_Written`

```odin
File_Written :: struct {
	path:  string,
	ok:    bool,
	error: string,
}
```

File_Written is a write's outcome.

### `read_file`

```odin
read_file :: proc($Msg: typeid, path: string, on_done: Handler(Msg, File_Read), tag: u64 = 0) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

read_file reads all of `path` off the main thread; the handler gets File_Read (with `tag`) when it is done.  case Open: return baldr.read_file(Msg, v.path, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.File_Read)) -> Msg { return Loaded{e.value} })

### `write_file`

```odin
write_file :: proc($Msg: typeid, path: string, data: []u8, on_done: Handler(Msg, File_Written) = nil, tag: u64 = 0) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

write_file replaces `path` with `data` (created when missing) off the main thread; the handler gets File_Written (with `tag`) when it is done. `data` is copied.

## dnd.odin

Drag and drop inside a window. A node becomes a drag source with `draggable` (what it is, which one, how many when it stands for a selection); a node takes drops with `drop_target` (which kinds, at which positions). Press and move past a small threshold (or long-press and move on touch) to lift a ghost — the item itself, faded, with a count badge when it stands for a selection — that follows the pointer above everything and is transparent to hit testing; the target under the pointer shows a ring (On) or an insertion line (Before / After); release sends on_drop with a Drop; Escape cancels; the pointer near a scroll region's edge scrolls it. Keyboard: with a draggable item focused (or under a tree's or table's cursor), Ctrl+X picks it up, move focus to a target, Ctrl+V drops On it (After when On is not allowed), Escape cancels; a Status node in the overlay announces each step.

```odin
chip := baldr.row(ui, …); …; baldr.end(ui)
baldr.draggable(ui, chip, "chip", tag = u64(i))
baldr.drop_target(ui, chip, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Drop)) -> Msg { return Reorder{from = e.value.tag, to = e.tag, before = e.value.position == .Before} },
	accepts = "chip", positions = {.Before, .After})
```

Across apps: a source with `files` (a proc giving the paths, asked once as the drag is picked up) is handed to the compositor as text/uri-list, so it can be dropped in a file manager or a terminal; another app's drag over the window is a drag of kind "files" that a `drop_target(accepts = "files")` receives with the paths in Drop.files.

### `Drop_Position`

```odin
Drop_Position :: enum u8 { On, Before, After }
```

Drop_Position: where a drop lands relative to the target.

### `Drop_Positions`

```odin
Drop_Positions :: bit_set[Drop_Position; u8]
```

### `Drop`

```odin
Drop :: struct {
	kind:       string,      // live only while the message is handled, as `files` is
	tag:        u64,
	count:      int,
	position:   Drop_Position,
	source:     Node,        // NODE_NONE for a drag from another app
	files:      []string,    // local paths: another app's files, or this source's own `files`; live only while the message is handled
	background: bool,        // landed on a table's or icon view's empty space (the folder it shows), not on an item; Ev.tag is then the view's
	action:     Drop_Action, // Ctrl copies, Shift moves, Alt (or Ctrl+Shift) asks, Default leaves it to the app; from another app, what was negotiated with it
	mods:       Mods,        // modifiers held at release (best effort for a drag from another app)
	// Where it was let go, from the target's own top-left, in the logical
	// pixels `pointer_samples` reports for that node (a keyboard drop gives
	// the target's own drop point). Carried in the message because
	// `Ui.context_pos` is shared and the next event may overwrite it first.
	at:         [2]f32,
}
```

Drop is what on_drop receives: the source's kind / tag / count and where it landed. The handler's Ev.tag is the target's tag.

### `Drag_Over`

```odin
Drag_Over :: struct {
	at:    [2]f32, // from the target's own top-left, as `Drop.at` is
	kind:  string, // live only while the message is handled
	tag:   u64,
	count: int,
	mods:  Mods,
	left:  bool,   // the drag has left the target; `at` is where it last was
}
```

Drag_Over is a drag passing over a target that asked to hear about it: where it is, what it carries, and — with `left` — that it has gone.  It lets a target show the drop before it happens: a timeline ghosting the clip where it would land, an icon view showing the cell, a list showing the line it would go in at.

### `Drag_Rest`

```odin
Drag_Rest :: struct {
	at:    [2]f32, // from the node's own top-left, as `Drop.at` is
	kind:  string, // live only while the message is handled
	tag:   u64,
	count: int,
	mods:  Mods,
}
```

Drag_Rest is a drag that has stopped still on a node for DRAG_REST_MS: spring-loading, the way a folder opens or a taskbar button raises its window when a file is held over it without letting go. It is heard whether or not the node takes the drop, so it is asked for with `drag_rest` rather than as part of `drop_target`.

### `Drop_Action`

```odin
Drop_Action :: platform.Drag_Action
```

Drop_Action is what a drop should do with what it carries.

### `drop_action_of_mods`

```odin
drop_action_of_mods :: proc "contextless" (mods: Mods) -> Drop_Action
```

drop_action_of_mods is the action the modifiers held at a drop ask for, the way file managers read them.

### `Files_Of`

```odin
Files_Of :: proc(user: rawptr, tag: u64, count: int, allocator: runtime.Allocator) -> []string
```

Files_Of gives a drag source's files: the paths for `tag` (and `count` of them when a selection drags whole). It answers a drop inside the app and, unless the source also gives a `Files_Out` (dnd_files.odin), a drag that leaves the window as well.  It is asked once, as the drag is picked up, and the answer is what the drag carries from then on: `tag` is usually a row number, and the view can change under a held drag (spring-loading opens a folder), so asking again at the drop would move the wrong file. Answer for `tag` and `count` as they are now.

### `Dnd_Hook`

```odin
Dnd_Hook :: struct {
	user:    rawptr,
	surface: u32, // the platform surface this tree is
	start:   proc(user: rawptr, surface: u32, items: []Clip_Item, serial: u32, icon: Drag_Icon) -> bool,
	accept:  proc(user: rawptr, accept: bool),
	receive: proc(user: rawptr, mime: string) -> bool, // ask for a drop's data in one form; drag_ext_data brings it
	finish:  proc(user: rawptr, accepted: bool),
}
```

Dnd_Hook is what the platform installs so a drag can cross app boundaries (zero in headless tests: drags stay inside the window).

### `Drag_Source`

```odin
Drag_Source :: struct {
	kind:  string, // owned by the tree allocator
	tag:   u64,
	count: int,
	files: Files_Of, // the paths; nil (and no files_out) = the drag stays inside the window
	files_out: Files_Out, // the paths for a drag that *leaves*, made as it goes
	user:  rawptr,
}
```

Drag_Source is one registered node (per view, like a click handler).

### `Grid_Drag`

```odin
Grid_Drag :: struct {
	kind:    string, // owned; "" = rows are not draggable
	accepts: string, // owned; used when the view has an on_drop
	files:   Files_Of,
	files_out: Files_Out,
	user:    rawptr,
}
```

Grid_Drag is a table's or icon view's drag setup: its rows / tiles are registered from it as they are built (see grid_row_dnd).

### `Drop_Target`

```odin
Drop_Target :: struct {
	positions: Drop_Positions,
	accepts:   string, // owned; "" = any kind
}
```

Drop_Target lives in the tree so paint can read it without the message type.

### `draggable`

```odin
draggable :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, kind: string, tag: u64 = 0, count: int = 1, files: Files_Of = nil, user: rawptr = nil,
	on_drag_out: Handler(Msg, Drop) = nil, files_out: Files_Out = nil)
```

draggable marks `n` (a node just built) as a drag source. `count` > 1 says the drag stands for a selection (the ghost shows a badge). A free-standing source becomes a Tab stop (named by its first text when it has no name) so the keyboard path — Ctrl+X, move focus, Ctrl+V — can reach it; rows inside a tree, table or icon view stay under their view's single Tab stop (those register through drag_register). `on_drag_out` hears when a drag carrying `files` left the window and was dropped in another app: a Drop with the action that app took (a Move means the files are gone from here).

### `drop_target`

```odin
drop_target :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, on_drop: Handler(Msg, Drop), accepts: string = "", positions: Drop_Positions = {.On}, on_over: Handler(Msg, Drag_Over) = nil, tag: u64 = 0)
```

drop_target makes `n` accept drops of `accepts` kind ("" = any) at `positions`. on_drop receives a Drop; its Ev.tag is `n`'s tag (`tag` sets it here for nodes whose builder has none, such as a plain row). `on_over` hears where a drag is over this target as the pointer moves — not only when it arrives — and once more with `left` when it goes, however the drag ended, so a target can show the drop before it happens.

### `Rest_Target`

```odin
Rest_Target :: struct {
	accepts: string, // owned; "" = any kind
	repeat:  bool,   // false: acting once is enough, and nothing more is heard until the drag moves
}
```

Rest_Target lives in the tree so the rest timer can read it without the message type, as Drop_Target does.

### `drag_rest`

```odin
drag_rest :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, on_rest: Handler(Msg, Drag_Rest), accepts: string = "", repeat := true)
```

drag_rest tells `n` when a drag stands still on it for DRAG_REST_MS — spring-loading. A file manager opens the folder rested on, a taskbar button raises its window, a sidebar place navigates to itself.  Unlike `drop_target` this says nothing about taking a drop: a node that accepts nothing still hears a drag rest on it, since resting asks it to get out of the way. `accepts` is the drag kind it hears ("" = any).  It fires once where the drag settles, and again only after the drag moves on or the handler puts something else under the pointer. Nothing is heard from a keyboard drag, after Escape, or while a cancelled drag springs back. When the handler changes what is under the pointer, the drop target is worked out again, so a drop lands in the folder that opened.  `repeat = false` says acting once is enough: nothing else is heard until the drag moves again. That is for a node whose handler takes the drag somewhere else rather than further in — a path bar, which reflows and slides an ancestor under a still pointer, would otherwise climb the whole path. A folder row, whose contents appear under the pointer, wants the default.

### `drop_flows_sideways`

```odin
drop_flows_sideways :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node) -> bool
```

drop_flows_sideways: Before / After run along the target's parent's axis — left / right in a row or wrap, above / below in a column or a wrap running down.

### `drag_cancel`

```odin
drag_cancel :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg)) -> bool
```

drag_cancel lets the ghost spring back to where it was picked up, then ends the drag (instantly under reduce-motion or from the keyboard).

### `drag_leave_window`

```odin
drag_leave_window :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg)) -> bool
```

drag_leave_window: a pointer drag with files leaves the window: the compositor takes it over with the paths as text/uri-list (and plain text). True when it did; the drag stays active and comes back through drag_ext_* if the pointer returns, or ends with drag_ext_end.  The source is asked `files_out` if it gave one, and `files` otherwise. A `files_out` that hands back a promise has its paths written into the other program only once it says the files are there (dnd_files.odin).

### `drag_ext_enter`

```odin
drag_ext_enter :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), pos: [2]f32, has_files: bool) -> bool
```

drag_ext_enter: a drag came over the window: ours coming back, or another app's (taken only when it carries files).

### `drag_sibling_enter`

```odin
drag_sibling_enter :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), from: ^Ui(Msg), pos: [2]f32) -> bool
```

drag_sibling_enter: a drag from another window of this app (`from`, where it started) came over this one, carried by the compositor. It is taken as that drag, its kind, tag, count and files alike, so a drop here is an ordinary in-app Drop, with the move-or-copy default and Alt's Copy here / Move here as within one window.

### `drag_ext_motion`

```odin
drag_ext_motion :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), pos: [2]f32) -> bool
```

drag_ext_motion moves a cross-app drag: the target under it lights up and the source's cursor learns whether a drop would land.

### `drag_ext_leave`

```odin
drag_ext_leave :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg)) -> bool
```

drag_ext_leave: the drag left the window: another app's is over for us; ours goes on outside.

### `drag_ext_drop`

```odin
drag_ext_drop :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), pos: [2]f32, action: Drop_Action = .Default, mods: Mods = {}, from: ^Ui(Msg) = nil) -> bool
```

drag_ext_drop: a drag the compositor carried was released over the window. Ours, or one from another window of this app (whose window, `from`, then ends its drag: the drop landed in the app), drops as an in-window drop would. Another app's asks for its paths (text/uri-list); drag_ext_data hands them to the target once they arrive, and nothing waits for them meanwhile.

### `drag_ext_data`

```odin
drag_ext_data :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), data: []u8, ok: bool) -> bool
```

drag_ext_data: the data another app's drop asked for arrived (ok false: the source gave none in time). Its paths go to the target the drop landed on, when that is still there and still takes drops, and the platform hears whether it landed.

### `drag_ext_end`

```odin
drag_ext_end :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), dropped: bool, action: Drop_Action = .Default) -> bool
```

drag_ext_end: our drag that the compositor held ended elsewhere.

### `drag_end`

```odin
drag_end :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg)) -> bool
```

drag_end cancels or finishes a drag: the ghost leaves, feedback clears, a previewed source goes back to its slot.

### `drag_key`

```odin
drag_key :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), key: Key, mods: Mods) -> bool
```

drag_key: Ctrl+X picks the focused item up, Ctrl+V drops it on the focused target, Escape cancels. Returns true if consumed.

### `DRAG_GHOST_FADE`

```odin
DRAG_GHOST_FADE :: f32(0.75)
```

DRAG_GHOST_FADE is how much of the item the ghost shows: enough to read it as the thing being carried, little enough to read as a ghost.

## notify.odin

Desktop notifications: what an app says when the person is not looking at it, such as a long job finishing with the window behind others.

A notification is the desktop's, not the app's: the desktop decides whether to show it, where and for how long, and the person's settings win. `Shown` means the desktop took it, not that anybody saw it.

The app names each notification with a `tag` of its own, which comes back with everything that happens to it. A second notification with the same tag replaces the first in place, so a progress or "now playing" notification updates without piling up.

### `Notify_Close_Reason`

```odin
Notify_Close_Reason :: platform.Notify_Close_Reason
```

### `Notification`

```odin
Notification :: struct {
	tag:    u64,
	event:  Notify_Event,
	action: string,
	reason: Notify_Close_Reason,
	error:  string,
}
```

Notification is what became of one. `event` says which of four things happened; `action` is the key of the button the person chose, empty for the notification itself; `reason` says why a Closed one closed; `error` says why a Failed one failed. Activated means the person wants what it offers; Closed means they waved it away, so do not act on both. `action` and `error` are the frame's: copy what you keep.

### `notify`

```odin
notify :: proc($Msg: typeid, title: string, body: string = "", actions: []Notify_Action = nil,
	icon: string = "", urgency: Notify_Urgency = .Normal, timeout_ms: i32 = -1,
	tag: u64 = 0, on_event: Handler(Msg, Notification) = nil) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

notify asks the desktop to show a notification and reports back through `on_event`: Shown or Failed first, then Activated if the person chooses it, then Closed when it goes away. A backend without notifications answers Failed at once, so the app can say it in its window instead.  `actions` are its buttons: each `key` comes back in `Notification.action`. A click on the notification itself is an Activated with an empty action, on every desktop. `tag` names it for `notify_close` and for replacing it; the default 0 is a tag like any other, so two untagged notifications replace each other. `icon` is a desktop icon-theme name (the app's own when empty); `timeout_ms` is -1 for the desktop's choice and 0 for "until dismissed", both advisory.  Do not make `Activated` the only way to the result: Windows ignores actions (its balloons have no buttons) and may not deliver the click (platform/win32/notify.odin). Everything passed is copied, so an `actions` literal in the call is safe.  baldr.notify(Msg, "Render finished", s.file, actions = []baldr.Notify_Action{{"show", "Show me"}}, on_event = proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Notification)) -> Msg { return Notified{e.value} })

### `notify_close`

```odin
notify_close :: proc($Msg: typeid, tag: u64) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

notify_close takes back the notification `tag` is showing. A tag that is not showing is not an error. The app hears a Closed with reason `By_App`, so every way a notification goes away arrives in one place.

## tray.odin

The tray / status item: what an app *is* when its window is closed — a sync client in the notification area, a recorder in the menu bar.

It is declared, like a surface. `App.tray` is asked at start and after every update, and the runtime works out what changed, so a menu rebuilt at runtime needs no API of its own: return a different menu, the way a view returns different widgets. There is no handle and nothing to free.

```odin
tray = proc(s: ^State, out: ^[dynamic]baldr.Tray_Desc(Msg)) {
	append(out, baldr.Tray_Desc(Msg){
		tag = 1, icon = .Cloud, tooltip = s.state_text,
		on_click = Msg(Show_Window{}),
		menu = {
			{label = s.state_text, disabled = true}, // a status line is a disabled item
			{separator = true},
			{label = "Pause syncing", checked = s.paused, msg = Msg(Toggle_Pause{})},
			{label = "Show window", msg = Msg(Show_Window{})},
			{label = "Quit", msg = Msg(Quit_App{})},
		},
	})
}
```

A declared tray holds the run open, as `keep_running` does: an app in the tray with no window has not finished. One that declares neither has.

Not everything arrives. A desktop may have no tray at all (GNOME without the AppIndicator extension) — `on_gone` says so, `on_hosted` says when one turns up — and some hosts open the menu for any click, so the primary click never comes. Keep everything reachable from the menu too.

### `Tray_Status`

```odin
Tray_Status :: platform.Tray_Status
```

### `Tray_Item`

```odin
Tray_Item :: struct($Msg: typeid) {
	label:     string,
	msg:       Maybe(Msg),
	icon:      Icon,
	checked:   Maybe(bool),
	disabled:  bool,
	separator: bool,
	children:  []Tray_Item(Msg),
}
```

Tray_Item is one line of the menu.  `separator = true` ignores everything else. **`checked` present means a checkable item** and absent means a plain one: the desktops draw "a checkbox that is off" and "no checkbox" differently, and an app that means one must not get the other. `children` is a submenu, one level deep where the desktop has them and flattened to the parent where it does not.  The strings are read while the tray is worked out and copied if they are kept, so a label built in the frame's temp memory is fine.

### `Tray_Desc`

```odin
Tray_Desc :: struct($Msg: typeid) {
	tag:       u64,
	title:     string, // what the desktop calls it in its own lists
	tooltip:   string,
	icon:      Icon,
	icon_name: string,
	status:    Tray_Status,
	menu:      []Tray_Item(Msg),
	on_click:  Maybe(Msg), // the primary action, where the desktop sends one
	on_hosted: Maybe(Msg), // a tray exists and this item is in it
	on_gone:   Maybe(Msg), // …and now it is not
}
```

Tray_Desc is a tray item to show. `tag` names it, and a second descriptor with the same tag **updates** it in place rather than adding another.  `icon` is the toolkit's own (a Lucide icon, rasterised for the desktop and redrawn when the desktop's theme changes); `icon_name` is a name from the desktop's icon theme instead, which Linux hosts theme themselves and prefer. Give either.

## desktop.odin

Windows and workspaces: what a taskbar, a dock and a workspace switcher see and do. The compositor lists the open windows (their titles, app ids, screens, parents and states) and each screen's workspaces. `toplevels()` and `workspaces()` return them as last listed, and a `toplevels_changed` / `workspaces_changed` subscription says when they changed, once for each batch of the compositor's changes. What a bar asks of them goes back as commands from update. Where the desktop doesn't offer this (a compositor without wlr-foreign-toplevel-management or ext-workspace, Windows, macOS) the lists are empty and the commands do nothing.

```odin
subscriptions :: proc(s: ^State, out: ^[dynamic]baldr.Sub(Msg)) {
	append(out, baldr.toplevels_changed(Msg, Windows_Changed{}))
}
update :: proc(s: ^State, m: Msg) -> baldr.Cmd(Msg) {
	switch v in m {
	case Raise: return baldr.toplevel_activate(Msg, v.id)
	…
```

### `Workspace_Action`

```odin
Workspace_Action :: platform.Workspace_Action
```

### `toplevels`

```odin
toplevels :: proc() -> []Toplevel
```

toplevels is the open windows as the compositor last listed them, in the order it announced them. Valid until they change (a `toplevels_changed` subscription says when); empty where the desktop doesn't list windows.

### `workspaces`

```odin
workspaces :: proc() -> []Workspace
```

workspaces is the workspaces as the compositor last listed them, each with its screens. Valid until they change (`workspaces_changed`); empty where the desktop doesn't list workspaces.

### `toplevels_changed`

```odin
toplevels_changed :: proc($Msg: typeid, msg: Msg, tag: u64 = 0) -> Sub(Msg)
```

toplevels_changed sends `msg` whenever a window opens, closes or changes (its title, screens or states); `toplevels()` then has the new list.

### `workspaces_changed`

```odin
workspaces_changed :: proc($Msg: typeid, msg: Msg, tag: u64 = 0) -> Sub(Msg)
```

workspaces_changed sends `msg` whenever a workspace or its screen, name or state changes; `workspaces()` then has the new list.

### `toplevel_activate`

```odin
toplevel_activate :: proc($Msg: typeid, id: u64) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

toplevel_activate raises and focuses window `id` (a taskbar button's click).

### `toplevel_set_minimized`

```odin
toplevel_set_minimized :: proc($Msg: typeid, id: u64, minimized: bool) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

toplevel_set_minimized minimises window `id`, or brings it back.

### `toplevel_set_maximized`

```odin
toplevel_set_maximized :: proc($Msg: typeid, id: u64, maximized: bool) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

toplevel_set_maximized maximises window `id`, or restores it.

### `toplevel_set_fullscreen`

```odin
toplevel_set_fullscreen :: proc($Msg: typeid, id: u64, fullscreen: bool) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

toplevel_set_fullscreen makes window `id` fullscreen on its screen, or takes it out of fullscreen.

### `toplevel_close`

```odin
toplevel_close :: proc($Msg: typeid, id: u64) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

toplevel_close asks window `id` to close, as its close button would (the app may ask first).

### `workspace_activate`

```odin
workspace_activate :: proc($Msg: typeid, id: u64) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

workspace_activate switches to workspace `id` on its screen.

### `activate`

```odin
activate :: proc($Msg: typeid, tag: u64 = 0, token := "") -> Cmd(Msg)
```

activate asks the desktop to bring the app's own window — the surface whose `tag` it is (0: the app's first window) — to the front and give it the keyboard (xdg-activation): for MPRIS's Raise, a second launch, a notification's click. `token` is the activation token the app was handed (a D-Bus caller's, a launcher's); without one the app asks the desktop for one itself, which a desktop may grant only after recent input. A window the app is opening again in the same update (a player kept running with it closed) is activated as it shows. The desktop decides what an activation does: it may raise the window, or only mark it as wanting attention.

### `workspace_assign`

```odin
workspace_assign :: proc($Msg: typeid, id: u64, output: string) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

workspace_assign moves workspace `id` to the screen named `output`; it does nothing when that screen has no workspaces or the workspace can't move (Workspace.can_assign).

### `toplevel_spot`

```odin
toplevel_spot :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, id: u64)
```

toplevel_spot says that node `n` is where window `id` is shown — a taskbar button, a dock icon — so the compositor can fly the window into it as it minimises and back out of it as it returns (`zwlr_foreign_toplevel_handle_v1.set_rectangle`). Without it the compositor has to guess.  for w in baldr.toplevels() { b := baldr.button(ui, w.title, Raise{w.id}, key = w.id) baldr.toplevel_spot(ui, b, w.id) }  Declare it every build. Baldr sends the node's rectangle whenever layout moves it, and tells the compositor to forget it when the node goes, the window closes, or the node is no longer on screen. One button stands for several windows (an app group) by saying it once per window. Where several surfaces show the same window (a bar on each screen), the one on the window's own screen is sent last, since the compositor keeps the last; a window on a screen with no bar still gets whichever rectangle shows it. Nothing happens where the desktop does not list windows (no wlr-foreign-toplevel-management, Windows, macOS).

## secret.odin

Secrets in the OS keychain: a token or a password an app must not leave in a file. The desktop keeps it — GNOME Keyring or KWallet over the Secret Service on Linux, Credential Manager on Windows — behind whatever lock the person already has.

They are commands, like file IO, because a keyring can take its time: it may be locked, and unlocking it shows the person a prompt. Nothing waits on the main thread.

```odin
case Sign_In:  return baldr.secret_set(Msg, "acme", "openai", token,
                   on_done = proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Secret)) -> Msg { return Stored{e.value.status} })
case Start:    return baldr.secret_get(Msg, "acme", "openai",
                   proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Secret)) -> Msg { return Token{e.value} })
case Sign_Out: return baldr.secret_delete(Msg, "acme", "openai")
```

A call says *why* it did not do what was asked, because an app's answer differs: `.Not_Found` means ask the person to sign in again; `.No_Keyring` means this desktop has none and the app must decide for itself whether a file is acceptable; `.Locked` means "unlock your keyring" — a locked one is asked to unlock first, for a write as for a read, and this is what is left when it could not be; `.Denied` means the person dismissed its prompt; and `.Failed` that the keyring itself went wrong. Baldr never writes a secret to a file on its own.

Name the service for the app (its reverse-DNS id, say): two apps that pass the same service and account share one secret, as the keyring sees it.

### `Secret_Status`

```odin
Secret_Status :: enum u8 {
	Ok,          // stored, deleted, or found
	Not_Found,   // the keyring works and holds no such secret
	No_Keyring,  // this desktop has no keyring (or it refused to start)
	Denied,      // the person said no: they dismissed the keyring's prompt
	Locked,      // the keyring is locked and nothing could unlock it: tell the person to unlock their keyring
	Failed,      // something else went wrong; `error` says what
}
```

Secret_Status is how a keychain call ended.

### `Secret`

```odin
Secret :: struct {
	service: string,
	account: string,
	value:   []u8,
	status:  Secret_Status,
	error:   string, // what failed, when the status is .Failed
}
```

Secret is what a lookup found. `value` is the app's to keep — and to scrub when it is done with it (`secret_scrub`), the way a password field's text is. It is empty unless `status` is `.Ok`. `error` and the names live only while the message is handled; clone what you keep.

### `secret_get`

```odin
secret_get :: proc($Msg: typeid, service, account: string, on_done: Handler(Msg, Secret), tag: u64 = 0) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

secret_get looks a secret up by service and account. The handler gets a `Secret` whose `value` is the app's, and `secret_scrub` frees it without leaving the bytes behind in freed memory.  Keychain calls run one at a time, in the order they were asked for, so an app may ask for all three of its secrets at once: a locked keyring prompts on the first and the rest go through behind it. None of them blocks the run loop, and the handlers are called as each answers.

### `secret_set`

```odin
secret_set :: proc($Msg: typeid, service, account: string, value: []u8, on_done: Handler(Msg, Secret) = nil, tag: u64 = 0) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

secret_set stores (or replaces) a secret. `value` is copied at once, and the copy is scrubbed once the keyring has it. It takes its turn in the keychain queue, so a `secret_get` of the same account asked for after it reads back what was written rather than racing it.

### `secret_delete`

```odin
secret_delete :: proc($Msg: typeid, service, account: string, on_done: Handler(Msg, Secret) = nil, tag: u64 = 0) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

secret_delete removes a secret — signing out. `.Not_Found` is not an error: there was nothing to remove.

### `secret_scrub`

```odin
secret_scrub :: proc{secret_scrub_value, secret_scrub_bytes}
```

secret_scrub overwrites a secret's bytes and frees them, or overwrites any buffer in place. Call it as soon as the value has been used: a token left in freed memory is a token in whatever allocation lands there next.  baldr.secret_scrub(&s.token)            // the Secret a get handed over baldr.secret_scrub(header[:])           // the line it was pasted into  The bytes go through volatile stores, because a plain loop over memory about to be freed is exactly what an optimiser removes. The buffer form only overwrites: whoever made it still frees it.

### `secret_scrub_value`

```odin
secret_scrub_value :: proc(s: ^Secret, allocator := context.allocator)
```

secret_scrub_value overwrites a secret's bytes and frees them. Call it through `secret_scrub`.

### `secret_scrub_bytes`

```odin
secret_scrub_bytes :: proc(b: []u8)
```

secret_scrub_bytes overwrites `b` in place and frees nothing. Call it through `secret_scrub`.

## access.odin

Accessibility data lives on every node from birth. Roles and actions mirror the AccessKit / ARIA vocabulary so each platform bridge (AT-SPI2, UI Automation, NSAccessibility) is a 1:1 mapping.

### `Role`

```odin
Role :: enum u8 {
	None, Window, Generic, Group, Region,
	Text, Heading, Label, Image,
	Button, Toggle_Button, Link, Checkbox, Radio, Radio_Group, Switch, Slider, Spin_Button,
	Text_Input, Search_Box, Combo_Box,
	List, List_Item, Table, Row, Cell, Column_Header, Tree, Tree_Item,
	Tab_List, Tab, Tab_Panel,
	Menu_Bar, Menu, Menu_Item,
	Dialog, Alert, Status, Progress, Tooltip, Separator, Scroll_Area, Toolbar,
	Terminal,
}
```

### `Live`

```odin
Live :: enum u8 { Off, Polite, Assertive }
```

### `Access`

```odin
Access :: struct {
	role:                  Role,
	name:                  string,
	describe:              string,
	name_owned, desc_owned: bool,
	value, value_min, value_max: f32,
	has_value:             bool,
	value_text:            string, // text_input hook: a field's contents (bullets for password); aliases the widget's storage
	states:                bit_set[A11y_State; u16],
	live:                  Live,
	labelled_by, described_by, controls: Node,
	active:                Node, // the item a field's keys act on while it keeps focus: its active descendant (set_active_item)
}
```

### `INTERACTIVE_ROLES`

```odin
INTERACTIVE_ROLES :: bit_set[Role; u64]{
	.Button, .Toggle_Button, .Link, .Checkbox, .Radio, .Switch, .Slider, .Spin_Button,
	.Text_Input, .Search_Box, .Combo_Box, .Tab, .Menu_Item, .Row, .Column_Header, .List_Item, .Tree_Item,
}
```

INTERACTIVE_ROLES must have an accessible name; the lint enforces it.

### `set_live`

```odin
set_live :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, live: Live)
```

set_live makes `n` a live region: something a reader says out loud when it changes although nothing took focus — a toast, a status line, "3 of 8 copied". Polite waits for a gap in the speech, Assertive interrupts. The tree keeps the set of them, so the accessibility bridge can watch a handful of nodes instead of the whole window.

### `live`

```odin
live :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, level: Live = .Polite, loc := #caller_location)
```

live marks the node just built as a live region: something a screen reader says out loud when it changes although nothing took focus — a status line, "3 of 8 copied", an error raised somewhere else on the screen. Polite waits for a gap in the speech, Assertive interrupts, and `.Off` stops it being one. Use it for what the app is *doing*; a toast and the busy spinner are live already.  baldr.live(ui, baldr.text(ui, s.progress), .Polite)

### `set_role`

```odin
set_role :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, role: Role)
```

### `set_name`

```odin
set_name :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, name: string)
```

set_name sets the accessible name (what a screen reader announces). Widgets call it with their label; apps override with `label = …`.

### `set_active_item`

```odin
set_active_item :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, item: Node)
```

set_active_item names `item` as the one field `n`'s keys act on while focus stays in the field (a launcher's search field walking its results): its active descendant in the accessibility tree. NODE_NONE: none.

### `set_describe`

```odin
set_describe :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, desc: string)
```

### `lint`

```odin
lint :: proc(t: ^Tree) -> (found: int)
```

lint appends a Diag for every accessibility problem the core can see statically: interactive nodes without a name. Hidden and inert subtrees are skipped. Returns how many problems were found.

### `access_dump`

```odin
access_dump :: proc(t: ^Tree, allocator := context.temp_allocator) -> string
```

access_dump renders the accessibility tree as indented text, one node per line: `role "name" [states]`. Nodes with role None are transparent; hidden and inert subtrees are left out. The text is in the frame's temp memory by default: copy it if it must outlive this wake-up.

## anim.odin

Animation. Every node can own a small set of tweened values (hover, press, focus, opacity); only nodes with a running tween cost anything per frame. Widgets never animate explicitly for state changes — paint asks anim_target() for the eased amount of a state, and a change of target starts a tween from the current value. The run loop ticks with the frame clock and requests frames only while something moves.

Accessibility: when Theme.motion.reduce is set (OS reduce-motion), every tween is instant. Nothing in Baldr moves, scales, or flashes for a user who asked it not to.

### `Anim_Prop`

```odin
Anim_Prop :: enum u8 {
	Hover,   // 0..1 hover state layer
	Press,   // 0..1 press state layer
	Focus,   // 0..1 focus ring
	Opacity, // 0..1 node opacity (rest 1)
	Scroll,  // 0..1 scrollbar visibility
	Value,   // 0..1 widget-defined (toggle knob position, …)
	Mark_Length, // a button's mark length, logical px (button_options)
	Mark_Color, // 0..1 through a change of the mark's colour
	Badge,      // 0..1 a button's badge grown in (button_options)
	Height,     // a pages container's eased height, logical px: layout runs again while it moves
	Enter,   // 0..1 enter/exit transition progress (rest 0: a new node with a transition fades in)
	Move,    // 1..0 remaining fraction of a layout move (paint shifts by move_from × value)
	Hide,    // 0..1 a surface slid off its screen edge (hide.odin), on its root
	Progress,    // a button's progress value, eased as it changes (button_options)
	Progress_On, // 0..1 that bar grown in
	Progress_Spin, // 0..1 a button's busy progress going round (a ring) or along (a bar), looping
}
```

### `Easing`

```odin
Easing :: enum u8 { Ease_Out, Ease_In_Out, Spring, Linear }
```

Easing curves. Ease_Out for state and most motion, Ease_In_Out for symmetric moves, Spring for a slight overshoot that settles.

### `Tween`

```odin
Tween :: struct {
	from, to: f32,
	start:    f64, // ms on the tree clock
	duration: f32, // ms; 0 = instant
	easing:   Easing,
	active:   bool,
}
```

### `anim_target`

```odin
anim_target :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, prop: Anim_Prop, to: f32, duration_ms: f32, easing: Easing = .Ease_Out) -> f32
```

anim_target declares where `prop` of node `n` should be. A changed target starts a tween from the current value over `duration_ms` (instant under reduce-motion). The first target a prop gets is where it starts, so a control shown for the first time draws its state at once (a switch that is on, a segmented control's pill); a node's entrance plays because paint starts its .Enter from rest (transition_progress). Returns the current eased value — call it from paint.

### `anim_restart`

```odin
anim_restart :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, prop: Anim_Prop, from, to: f32, duration_ms: f32, easing: Easing = .Ease_Out)
```

anim_restart starts a tween of `prop` from `from` to `to` now, whatever its current value or target (a layout move that begins at 1, an enter fade played again). Instant under reduce-motion.

### `anim_running`

```odin
anim_running :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, prop: Anim_Prop) -> bool
```

anim_running reports whether `prop` of `n` has a tween in flight.

### `anim_value`

```odin
anim_value :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, prop: Anim_Prop) -> f32
```

anim_value reads the current value without changing the target.

### `anim_tick`

```odin
anim_tick :: proc(t: ^Tree, now_ms: f64) -> (active: bool)
```

anim_tick advances every running tween to `now_ms`, dirtying the nodes whose values changed. Returns true while anything is still in motion — the caller must then draw another frame.

### `ease`

```odin
ease :: proc "contextless" (k: f32, e: Easing) -> f32
```

ease maps 0..1 progress through the chosen curve.

### `ease_spring`

```odin
ease_spring :: proc "contextless" (k: f32) -> f32
```

ease_spring overshoots by about 9% around 40% of the way and settles by the end: a damped oscillation.

### `ease_out_cubic`

```odin
ease_out_cubic :: proc "contextless" (k: f32) -> f32
```

### `ease_in_out_cubic`

```odin
ease_in_out_cubic :: proc "contextless" (k: f32) -> f32
```

## applet.odin

Applet surfaces: a Baldr app shown inside a desktop shell's bar, at a slot the shell reserved (the rikkiti-applet-v1 protocol). The shell starts the app with the slot's token, and `kind = .Applet` binds it. The compositor sizes the surface to the slot and says which screen edge the bar is on (ui.surface.edge: a clock stacks its lines in a side bar). It sits on the bar's glass, so nothing is drawn behind it, and its text follows what the compositor measures there; its popups open away from the edge; and it tells the shell the length along the bar its content wants, again whenever that changes. Closed by the compositor (its slot went, or its token was spent), it sends `on_close`, or ends the app when there is none, and is not opened again with that token. Without a shell to embed it (no token, or a compositor without applets) it opens as a small window.

```odin
surfaces :: proc(s: ^State, out: ^[dynamic]baldr.Surface_Desc(Msg)) {
	append(out, baldr.Surface_Desc(Msg){kind = .Applet, title = "Clock", size = {160, 40}})
}
```

### `APPLET_WINDOW_SIZE`

```odin
APPLET_WINDOW_SIZE :: [2]f32{240, 48}
```

APPLET_WINDOW_SIZE is the window an applet opens as, with no shell to embed it and no size of its own.

### `applet_size_hint`

```odin
applet_size_hint :: proc(t: ^Tree, edge: Edge, slot: [2]f32) -> (least, preferred: f32)
```

applet_size_hint measures the length along its bar an applet's content wants at the slot's thickness: the least it can take without breaking a word, and its natural length (in a side bar both are its height at the bar's width). It leaves the tree to be laid out again at the slot's size.

## applet_slot.odin

Applet slots: the bar's side of the rikkiti-applet-v1 protocol.

The protocol is one compositor's own (with rikkiti-backdrop-v1, the only such one in the toolkit; docs/widgets.md, "What the desktop has to support"). The API over it is not: a desktop that cannot embed applets gives an empty box that reports nothing rather than an error.

A bar lists other programs' applets among its own; for each it builds an `applet_slot`, a node that takes part in layout like a box and draws nothing. After each layout the runtime keeps a slot on the bar's layer surface in step with the node: made when it first has a place, moved with the bar's next commit when it moves or the bar changes edge, and removed with the node. The compositor shows the applet's own surface there, on the bar's glass. The app hears the slot's token (to start the applet with, in RIKKITI_APPLET_TOKEN), when an applet attaches or detaches (a new token follows), and the length it would like, which the next build can give it. A `key` keeps one slot per applet across rebuilds, so a rebuild never restarts an applet.

### `Slot_Event`

```odin
Slot_Event :: union { Slot_Token, Slot_Attached, Slot_Detached, Slot_Size_Hint }
```

Slot_Event is what an applet slot reports, each carrying the slot's `key`.

### `Slot_Token`

```odin
Slot_Token :: struct {
	key:   u64,
	token: string,
}
```

Slot_Token: start the slot's applet with `token` (in RIKKITI_APPLET_TOKEN). The string lives only while the message is handled — clone it to keep it.

### `Slot_Attached`

```odin
Slot_Attached :: struct {
	key: u64,
	pid: u32,
}
```

Slot_Attached: an applet bound the slot; `pid` is its process (0 when unknown).

### `Slot_Detached`

```odin
Slot_Detached :: struct {
	key: u64,
}
```

Slot_Detached: the slot's applet went (it exited or crashed); a new token follows, to start another.

### `Slot_Size_Hint`

```odin
Slot_Size_Hint :: struct {
	key:       u64,
	min:       f32,
	preferred: f32,
}
```

Slot_Size_Hint: the length along the bar the applet can't go below and the one it would like, in logical px (0 = no preference).

### `applet_slot`

```odin
applet_slot :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), on_event: Handler(Msg, Slot_Event), min: f32, preferred: f32, label := "",
	key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

applet_slot reserves a place in a bar for another program's applet: a node `preferred` long along the bar, shrinking to `min` in a tight row (the bar's edge decides which way is along), as thick as the bar across, that draws nothing, since the applet's own surface shows there. `on_event` hears the slot's Slot_Token, Slot_Attached, Slot_Detached and Slot_Size_Hint, each carrying `key`, which also keeps one slot per applet across rebuilds. `label` names the place for assistive technology (the applet's controls belong to its own program). Where the desktop can't embed applets (Caps .Applet_Slots) it is an empty box and reports nothing.

## backdrop.odin

Text that knows what is behind it.

A surface drawn straight on the desktop — a corner of system readouts, a clock on the wallpaper — has no background of its own, so nothing about its colours can be settled when it is written: the grey that reads over a dark photograph disappears into a pale one. A halo on every glyph always reads, but looks heavy over a plain wallpaper.
