key_hold makes node `n` hear `keys` going down and coming up, through `on_change`: down once however long the key is held (no repeats), and up when it is let go — wherever the focus has gone meanwhile, and when the window loses the keyboard. A key goes to it while the focus is inside `n` (or nowhere) and the focused control did not use the key itself. Either side of a modifier matches the other (.Alt_L also hears .Alt_R). Call it every build; nil stops it (and lets go of what it held).  baldr.key_hold(ui, editor, {.Space, .Alt_L, .Shift_L}, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Key_Hold)) -> Msg { return Tool_Key{e.value} })

## keyboard_info.odin

What a view may know of the keyboard: which surface has it (`ui.surface.keyboard`), the lock keys that are on, and the active layout — a lock screen puts its password card where the keyboard is, says when Caps Lock is on, and names the layout the password will be typed in. Read in a view like the viewport; views are built again whenever any of it changes, with no message.

### `Lock_Keys`

```odin
Lock_Keys :: platform.Lock_Keys
```

### `keyboard_locks`

```odin
keyboard_locks :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg)) -> Lock_Keys
```

keyboard_locks is the lock keys that are on now — Caps Lock, Num Lock — the locked ones, so a Caps Lock turned on before the view was shown counts. A password field shows Caps Lock of its own accord; a lock screen or a greeter may say it in words too.  if .Caps in baldr.keyboard_locks(ui) do baldr.text(ui, "Caps Lock is on", muted = true)

### `keyboard_layout`

```odin
keyboard_layout :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg)) -> (name: string, layouts: int)
```

keyboard_layout is the active keyboard layout's name, as the desktop's keymap calls it ("English (UK)"; "" when it gives none), and how many layouts there are — a password prompt names the layout when there is more than one. The name is the toolkit's until the layout changes: copy it to keep it.  if name, n := baldr.keyboard_layout(ui); n > 1 do baldr.text(ui, name, muted = true)

## layout.odin

Layout protocol: constraints go down, sizes come up, the parent positions. Single pass. A node that is not dirty and receives the same constraints as last time returns its cached size without visiting its subtree — that is what makes a local change cheap in a large tree.

### `Justify`

```odin
Justify :: enum u8 { Start, Center, End, Space_Between, Space_Around, Space_Evenly } // main axis
```

### `Layout_Props`

```odin
Layout_Props :: struct {
	axis:                       Axis,
	offset:                     [2]f32, // position inside a Stack parent (ignored elsewhere)
	width, height:              f32,
	min_w, min_h, max_w, max_h: f32,
	flex:                       f32,    // > 0: share of the parent's free main-axis space
	padding:                    [4]f32, // top, right, bottom, left
	gap:                        f32,
	align:                      Align,
	justify:                    Justify,
	// content_w is the width a container that scrolls sideways lays its
	// children out to, past its own (`scroll` / `list`'s `content_width`: a
	// zoomed document's widest page). It is the children's minimum, never
	// the container's, which still shrinks with the window (and
	// `min_content_width` still lets a row squeeze it); a child wider than
	// it keeps its width, with the others widened to match.
	content_w:                  f32,
}
```

Layout_Props is everything a node says about its own size and how it lays out children. Zero means "auto" for sizes and "none" for limits.

### `Constraints`

```odin
Constraints :: struct {
	min, max: [2]f32,
}
```

### `UNBOUNDED`

```odin
UNBOUNDED :: f32(1e30)
```

UNBOUNDED marks an axis with no upper limit (a scroll viewport's content axis).

### `is_bounded`

```odin
is_bounded :: proc "contextless" (v: f32) -> bool
```

### `constraints_tight`

```odin
constraints_tight :: proc "contextless" (size: [2]f32) -> Constraints
```

### `constraints_loose`

```odin
constraints_loose :: proc "contextless" (max: [2]f32) -> Constraints
```

### `constrain`

```odin
constrain :: proc "contextless" (c: Constraints, size: [2]f32) -> [2]f32
```

### `pad4`

```odin
pad4 :: proc "contextless" (p: f32) -> [4]f32
```

### `measure_text_mono`

```odin
measure_text_mono :: proc(t: ^Tree, s: string, size: f32, max_w: f32, weight: Font_Weight, mono: bool = false, family: Family_Id = 0, tabular: bool = false) -> [2]f32
```

measure_text_mono is the headless text measurer: fixed advance of half the font size, line height 1.25×. Deterministic, so tests can assert numbers.

### `TOUCH_TARGET`

```odin
TOUCH_TARGET :: f32(44)
```

TOUCH_TARGET is the minimum height of an interactive node, in logical px, while a touchscreen is present.

### `set_touch`

```odin
set_touch :: proc(t: ^Tree, touch: bool)
```

set_touch records whether a touchscreen is present and re-lays out every node, since interactive targets change size (layout_node's minimum).

### `layout`

```odin
layout :: proc(t: ^Tree, viewport: [2]f32)
```

layout runs the layout pass for every queued dirty root. The root is laid out tight to `viewport`. Boundaries whose size changed escalate to their parent, so the pass converges to a consistent tree.

### `layout_node`

```odin
layout_node :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, c: Constraints) -> [2]f32
```

layout_node sizes `n` under `c`, lays out its children, and returns its size. Children's parent-local rects are written; absolute rects are resolved afterwards by resolve_abs.

### `set_viewport`

```odin
set_viewport :: proc(t: ^Tree, viewport: [2]f32)
```

set_viewport records the window's logical size. The run loop calls it as soon as a configure arrives so a view built before the first frame already sees the right size (and size class); layout calls it too.

## lock.odin

Locking the session: a lock screen as a Baldr app.

The protocol is ext-session-lock-v1, a freedesktop standard, so a lock screen written here is not tied to one desktop (docs/widgets.md, "What the desktop has to support"). Where a desktop has no lock protocol, on_finished arrives at once and no window ever stands in for a lock surface.

The app asks for the lock with lock_session, declares one .Lock surface per screen in App.surfaces (by the screen's name, from outputs()), and ends the lock with unlock_session once the user has proved who they are. Tag each lock surface by its screen's name, so a screen going leaves the others in place (tagged by position, the ones after it would move).

```odin
surfaces :: proc(s: ^State, out: ^[dynamic]baldr.Surface_Desc(Msg)) {
	if !s.locking do return
	for o in baldr.outputs() do append(out, baldr.Surface_Desc(Msg){tag = hash.fnv64a(transmute([]u8)o.name), kind = .Lock, output = o.name})
}
…
case Lock:     s.locking = true; return baldr.lock_session(Msg, Locked{}, Lock_Finished{}, on_shown = Msg(Lock_Shown{}))
case Verified: s.locking = false; return baldr.unlock_session(Msg)
```

The lock is the most security-sensitive thing Baldr does, so its rules are few and absolute:

```odin
   - unlock_session is the only way the session unlocks. Baldr never
     unlocks on its own: not when the app quits, returns from run, crashes,
     drops its .Lock surfaces, loses the desktop, or hears on_finished. The
     protocol's promise is that a lock outlives its client, and the
     desktop keeps the session hidden.
   - A lock surface shows the app's own drawing and nothing else: the
     screen's size, opaque, no frame, no glass (what is behind it is the
     session it hides). Without a lock protocol there is no lock surface,
     never a window standing in for one.
   - While the lock is live, only .Lock surfaces open. Anything else
     declared then waits for the unlock (a notification's toast opens after
     it); what was open already stays open, and the desktop hides it.
     Menus and popovers on a lock surface open inside it.
```

The keyboard follows the desktop (the lock surface on the screen the user is at); text fields, secret ones included, the pointer and touch work as on any surface.

### `lock_session`

```odin
lock_session :: proc($Msg: typeid, on_locked: Msg, on_finished: Msg, on_shown: Maybe(Msg) = nil) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

lock_session asks the desktop to lock the session. `on_locked` comes when it has: every screen is hidden, and no frame of the session shows until unlock_session. `on_shown` comes once every .Lock surface has had a frame of its own on screen since then: the lock screen is up (a locker that sleeps the machine waits for this). `on_finished` comes when the desktop refused (another lock is live, or it cannot lock) or, after on_locked, ended the lock itself; the protocol then asks the app to unlock_session.  While the lock is live — from this command until unlock_session — the app's .Lock surfaces open, and no other surface does. Ignored, with a message on stderr, while a lock the app asked for is still live.

### `unlock_session`

```odin
unlock_session :: proc($Msg: typeid) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

unlock_session unlocks the session, and is the only thing that does. The .Lock surfaces close with it. Before the desktop has answered lock_session, it withdraws the lock: the desktop's answer is waited for, and a session it goes on to lock is unlocked at once. An app may quit straight after: the desktop has the unlock before the connection closes. Nothing happens when no lock is live.

## markdown_doc.odin

The document behind a read-only markdown view: the markdown source exactly as it was given, and the blocks parsed out of it — one per source line, the same grouping `document_set_markdown` gives the editors, so a view and an editor never disagree about what a block is.

It is built for text that arrives a few characters at a time, for minutes. `md_append` parses only what the new bytes completed: every block before the last is left alone, so a reply streaming at thirty updates a second costs its last line rather than the whole reply. The source is kept, which is what lets a selection be source byte offsets (bytes added at the end cannot move one made earlier) and what makes "copy as markdown" exact.

### `Md_Block`

```odin
Md_Block :: struct {
	meta:     Block_Meta,
	from, to: int, // source byte range of the line, its newline not included
	body:     int, // source byte where the inline text starts
	// A fence's language and a picture's src as offsets: the strings in
	// `meta` are slices of `src`, which an append reallocates.
	// md_parse_from derives the strings again from these.
	lang_at:  [2]int,
	src_at:   [2]int,
	in_fence: bool,
	open:     bool,
}
```

Md_Block is one source line's worth of block: what it is, where it is in the source, and where its inline text starts (after "# ", "- ", "> "). `in_fence` is the state *entering* the line, so a parse may restart here. `open` marks the provisional last block — a line whose newline has not arrived yet, which the next append may change.

### `Markdown_Doc`

```odin
Markdown_Doc :: struct {
	src:       [dynamic]u8,
	blocks:    [dynamic]Md_Block,
	rev:       u64, // bumped by every change; a view relays out when it moves
	id:        u64, // names this document for its lifetime, so a view can tell a new one from the one it cached at the same address
	parsed_to: int, // source bytes already parsed into finalised blocks
	in_fence:  bool, // the fence state after those bytes
	fence_lang: string, // …and the language the open fence named (a slice of src, re-derived after every parse)
	fence_at:  [2]int,  // …which is this range of it
	tail:      [4]u8, // an incomplete UTF-8 sequence held back until it completes
	tail_len:  int,
	allocator: runtime.Allocator,
}
```

Markdown_Doc is a markdown document an app owns and a `markdown_view` shows, as a `Document` is owned and shown by a `text_area`. Give it to `md_set` whole, or grow it with `md_append` as a reply streams.  Zero value is usable: the first call allocates with the context's allocator, or `md_init`'s. `md_destroy` frees it. Everything it hands back (`md_text`, a block's range) is a view into memory it owns and is invalidated by the next change — clone what you keep.

### `md_init`

```odin
md_init :: proc(md: ^Markdown_Doc, allocator := context.allocator)
```

md_init prepares a document with an allocator of its own. Calling it is optional: any of the others does the same with the context's allocator.

### `md_destroy`

```odin
md_destroy :: proc(md: ^Markdown_Doc)
```

md_destroy frees the document's source and blocks.

### `md_text`

```odin
md_text :: proc(md: ^Markdown_Doc) -> string
```

md_text is the markdown source as it stands: everything given, minus an incomplete UTF-8 sequence still waiting for its remaining bytes. It is a view into the document — the next change invalidates it.

### `md_clear`

```odin
md_clear :: proc(md: ^Markdown_Doc)
```

md_clear empties the document: a reply being retried, or an alternative taking its place.

### `md_set`

```odin
md_set :: proc(md: ^Markdown_Doc, text: string)
```

md_set replaces the document with `text` — a stored message, or opening a chat. The text is copied. For a reply that is still arriving, set what there is (or "") and `md_append` the rest: appending is what stays cheap.

### `md_append`

```odin
md_append :: proc(md: ^Markdown_Doc, delta: string)
```

md_append adds `delta` to the end of the document and parses what it completed, leaving every block before the last alone. A delta may split anywhere — mid-word, mid-UTF-8 sequence, mid-code-fence, mid-table-row — which is what a stream of tokens does:  - an incomplete UTF-8 sequence at the end is held back until its remaining bytes arrive, so no replacement character is ever shown; - a line whose newline has not arrived is a provisional block, remade by the next append; - an open fence stays open: its lines are code as they arrive.

### `md_block_text`

```odin
md_block_text :: proc(md: ^Markdown_Doc, i: int) -> string
```

md_block_text is one block's inline source — what a renderer lays out and what "copy as text" reads. It is a view into the document.

### `md_markdown`

```odin
md_markdown :: proc(md: ^Markdown_Doc, from, to: int) -> string
```

md_markdown is the source behind a byte range — what "copy as markdown" copies. `from` and `to` are source byte offsets; they are clamped, and a range that falls inside a rune is widened to whole runes. It is a view into the document.

## node.odin

### `Node`

```odin
Node :: distinct u64
```

Node is a generational handle into a Tree's node pool: low 32 bits index, high 32 bits generation. A stale handle (its slot was freed and reused) fails `node_get` instead of aliasing another node. Zero is "no node".

### `Node_Flag`

```odin
Node_Flag :: enum u8 {
	Alive,
	// invalidation
	Dirty_Layout,      // size/position may have changed; layout pass must visit
	Dirty_Paint,       // appearance changed; owning layer must re-emit this node
	Dirty_Root,        // queued in Tree.dirty_roots
	Layer_Dirty,       // set on a Layer node when any node it paints is Dirty_Paint
	// structure
	Drop_Target,       // a drop may ring it: the ring is drawn outside its rect, so paint_bounds reserves the room
	Relayout_Boundary, // layout below here never changes this node's size given the same constraints
	Layer,             // owns a cached draw list (root, scroll viewports, opacity groups, popovers)
	Clips,             // children are clipped to this node's rect
	Keep,              // parked by the reconciler instead of destroyed when not emitted
	Scroll_V,          // children may overflow vertically; Scroll_State.offset.y shifts them
	Scroll_H,          // …horizontally
	Exiting,           // playing its exit transition; not hit-testable, not focusable, destroyed when done
	Overlay,           // the overlay root: a second top-level node over the viewport, hit only through its children
	Animate_Moves,     // children glide to a new position when a rebuild moves them (paint-time shift, layout untouched)
	Animate_Resize,    // with Animate_Moves: they glide on a window resize too (an icon view's reflow)
	Revealed,          // a parked (Keep) node was shown again this build; the builder that owns it consumes the flag
	Hit_Transparent,   // never hit-tested, nor its children (a drag ghost under the pointer)
	Hit_Children,      // its own space lets the pointer through; only its children are hit (set_hit_children)
	Inert,             // draws, but it and its subtree take no pointer, focus or place in the accessibility tree (set_inert)
	Input_Area,        // where its surface takes the pointer, when the surface declares input = .Marked
	Backdrop_Area,     // where its surface is blurred behind, when the surface declares backdrop_region = .Marked
	Theme_Area,        // it and its subtree are built and painted in a theme of the app's own (theme_area)
	Glass_In_App,      // its glass is over the app's own content: it and its subtree are built and painted in the worst-case theme (in_app_theme)
	Ring_Inside,       // paints its own focus indication (a cursor row, the picked radio): no ring around the whole node
	Tab_First,         // Tab reaches it before the siblings built ahead of it: a part drawn over another (a slider's mute icon over its track)
	Glows,             // its text or icons have a glow behind them (Clear glass): it paints GLOW_OVERHANG past its rect
	Quiet,             // no hover or press wash: what it does instead (a dock icon growing) says what the pointer is on (button_options quiet)
	// behaviour
	Hidden,
	Disabled,
	Focusable,
	Keys_Only_Focus,   // Tab reaches it, a press does not: a tool button, a toolbar, a transport (button_options focus_on_click = false)
	Pointer_Only_Focus, // the mirror: a press focuses it, Tab passes it by — a read-only paragraph a chat has hundreds of (Qt's ClickFocus, HTML's tabindex=-1)
	// interaction
	Hovered,
	Pressed,
	Focused,
	Active,
}
```

Node_Flag is one bit of per-node state. Invalidation bits are set by the mutation procs and cleared by the passes; structure bits are set by builders; interaction bits by event dispatch.

### `Node_Flags`

```odin
Node_Flags :: bit_set[Node_Flag; u64]
```

u64 because there are more than 32 flags; the extra bits land in padding Node_Data already had, so it costs no size.

### `Kind`

```odin
Kind :: union {
	Box_Kind,
	Text_Kind,
	Custom_Kind,
	Text_Input_Kind, // text_input hook (widget_text_input.odin)
}
```

Kind is the small, hot per-kind data. Rare or heavy state (text buffers, scroll velocities, custom user pointers) lives in Tree side tables.

### `Custom_Kind`

```odin
Custom_Kind :: struct {
	size:     [2]f32,
	paint:    proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, d: ^Node_Data, l: ^Layer),
	user:     rawptr,
	overhang: f32, // px the painter may draw beyond the rect (shadows, rings): damage and culling include it
	paint_over: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, d: ^Node_Data, l: ^Layer), // drawn after the children (guides, indicators)
	measure:    proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, d: ^Node_Data, cc: Constraints) -> [2]f32, // custom layout: the node's size under `cc` (a text area's wrapped height)
}
```

Custom_Kind is the node door for widgets that paint themselves: a fixed intrinsic `size` and a `paint` proc that appends Draw_Cmds after the node's own background (and before its children). Interaction still goes through the ordinary flags, handlers, and side tables.

### `Text_Kind`

```odin
Text_Kind :: struct {
	str:    string, // owned by the tree allocator
	size:   f32,    // font size, logical px
	wrap_w: f32,    // max width the text was measured with (0 = unwrapped); set by layout
	weight: Font_Weight, // Regular, Medium or Semibold: the theme's role sets it, `text`'s weight overrides
	mono:   bool,        // the code face, not the UI one
	family: Family_Id,   // a family of its own — an app's wordmark, an editor's chosen face — 0 for the theme's
	nowrap: bool,   // never wrap: one line, cut at its box when it is given less room
	// Figures of one width (OpenType `tnum`), so a changing number keeps
	// its width: a stopwatch, a column of figures. Only the digits'
	// advances change, not the face.
	tabular: bool,
	// A secret shown on purpose (a revealed key, a recovery phrase): the
	// tree's copy is overwritten before it is freed, and the accessibility
	// bridge gets the node without its characters. `text(sensitive = true)`.
	sensitive: bool,
	ellipsis:  Ellipsis, // trim with "…" when the text does not fit its width (one line, or `max_lines`)
	max_lines: int,      // with ellipsis: wrap up to this many lines before trimming (0 = one line)
	display:   string,   // the trimmed text actually drawn (owned by the tree allocator); "" = str
	align:     Text_Align, // where each line sits within the node when lines differ in width
	inset_y:   f32,        // how far its lines sit below its top: an interactive text grown to its target size is centred in it (layout sets it)
	spills:    bool,       // wider than its box (a word or a glyph that cannot break, an ellipsis with no room): paint clips it at its sides, so it never draws over a neighbour or outside its damage (layout sets it)
	drawn_h:   f32,        // the height its lines take, which a row squeezed shorter than a line does not cut: its damage covers it (layout sets it)
}
```

### `Text_Align`

```odin
Text_Align :: enum u8 { Start, Center, End }
```

### `Font_Weight`

```odin
Font_Weight :: enum u8 { Regular, Medium, Semibold }
```

Font_Weight is how heavy text is set: Regular (400), Medium (500) or Semibold (600), from the UI typeface's weight axis.

### `Ellipsis`

```odin
Ellipsis :: enum u8 { None, End, Middle }
```

Ellipsis says where a text that does not fit loses characters: at the end ("Quarterly rep…") or in the middle, which keeps file extensions ("Quarterly…2026.pdf"). The accessible name stays the full text.

### `Kind_Tag`

```odin
Kind_Tag :: enum u8 { None, Box, Text, Custom, Text_Input }
```

### `kind_tag`

```odin
kind_tag :: proc "contextless" (k: Kind) -> Kind_Tag
```

### `Node_Data`

struct

Node_Data is one slot of the pool. Keep it small: layout and paint walk these arrays and cache misses are the cost model.

### `Diag`

```odin
Diag :: struct {
	msg: string,
	loc: runtime.Source_Code_Location,
}
```

Diag is a diagnostic produced by the core (unbalanced scope, lint hit). Headless callers read Tree.diags; the run loop logs them in debug builds.

### `Stats`

```odin
Stats :: struct {
	layout_nodes:   int, // nodes laid out since reset
	paint_nodes:    int, // nodes emitted since reset
	layers_rebuilt: int,
	term_rows:      int, // terminal rows drawn again (the rest were kept)
}
```

### `Text_Measure`

```odin
Text_Measure :: #type proc(t: ^Tree, s: string, size: f32, max_w: f32, weight: Font_Weight, mono: bool, family: Family_Id, tabular: bool) -> [2]f32
```

Text_Measure measures a run of text. `max_w` of 0 means no wrapping. The headless default is a fixed-advance approximation; the runtime installs runa.

### `Tree`

struct

Tree owns the node pool and everything the passes need. It is not parameterised by the app's message type so layout/paint/a11y are compiled once; Ui($Msg) embeds it.

### `tree_init`

```odin
tree_init :: proc(t: ^Tree, allocator := context.allocator)
```

tree_init prepares an empty tree with a root Box node that is also the root layer and relayout boundary. All allocations use `allocator`.

### `tree_destroy`

```odin
tree_destroy :: proc(t: ^Tree)
```

### `node_new`

```odin
node_new :: proc(t: ^Tree, kind: Kind, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

node_new allocates a node of `kind`. It is unattached until node_append / node_insert_before. Layout is dirty from birth.

### `node_get`

```odin
node_get :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node) -> ^Node_Data
```

node_get resolves a handle. Returns nil for NODE_NONE, stale, or freed handles — callers may rely on that instead of checking validity first. The pointer is into the tree's own storage and is only good until the next node is made: hold the `Node` handle across a build, never this.

### `node_valid`

```odin
node_valid :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node) -> bool
```

### `node_append`

```odin
node_append :: proc(t: ^Tree, parent, child: Node)
```

node_append attaches `child` as the last child of `parent`.

### `node_insert_before`

```odin
node_insert_before :: proc(t: ^Tree, parent, child, before: Node)
```

node_insert_before attaches `child` under `parent` immediately before `before` (NODE_NONE = append). A child that is already attached somewhere is detached first, so this also implements "move".

### `node_detach`

```odin
node_detach :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node)
```

node_detach unlinks a node from its parent without freeing it.

### `node_destroy`

```odin
node_destroy :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node)
```

node_destroy frees a node and its whole subtree. Handles into it go stale.

### `node_first`

```odin
node_first :: proc(t: ^Tree, p: Node) -> Node
```

node_first / node_next iterate children: `for c := node_first(t, p); c != NODE_NONE; c = node_next(t, c)`.

### `node_parent`

```odin
node_parent :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node) -> Node
```

### `node_within`

```odin
node_within :: proc(t: ^Tree, n, ancestor: Node) -> bool
```

node_within reports whether `n` is `ancestor` or one of its descendants.

### `node_child_count`

```odin
node_child_count :: proc(t: ^Tree, p: Node) -> (n: int)
```

node_child_count counts a node's children.

### `mark_layout_dirty`

```odin
mark_layout_dirty :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node)
```

mark_layout_dirty flags `n` and its ancestors up to (and including) the nearest relayout boundary, which is queued for the layout pass. Paint is dirtied too, since a size change repaints.

### `mark_paint_dirty`

```odin
mark_paint_dirty :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node)
```

mark_paint_dirty flags `n` for re-emission and its owning layer for rebuild. Cheap; safe to call redundantly.

### `set_text`

```odin
set_text :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, s: string)
```

set_text replaces a Text node's string. No-op (and no dirtying) when the text is unchanged, so builders can call it unconditionally.

### `set_sensitive`

```odin
set_sensitive :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, on: bool)
```

set_sensitive marks a text node's string a secret shown on purpose (a revealed key, a recovery phrase). The tree's copy is overwritten before it is freed, when the text is replaced and when the node goes; and the accessibility bridge gets the node without its content, since anything on the session accessibility bus can read node text. Nothing is hidden on screen. Use `text(sensitive = true)`, or this on a node built another way.

### `set_hidden`

```odin
set_hidden :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, hidden: bool)
```

### `set_hit_children`

```odin
set_hit_children :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, on: bool)
```

set_hit_children makes a container's own space let the pointer through, so only its children are hit: for a control centred by ordinary layout over something that takes the pointer itself (a toolbar over a canvas), where the row holding it would otherwise take every press across its width. Unlike `Hit_Transparent` and `inert`, the children stay hittable; it is how the overlay root behaves. Hit-testing only: the node still lays out, draws and is in the accessibility tree, and a child's events still bubble through it, so an `on_click` on it would fire from a child's click. It stays until changed: pass it every build.

### `set_invalid`

```odin
set_invalid :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, invalid: bool)
```

set_invalid marks a form control's value as wrong, until changed: a field draws its frame in the theme's danger colour, and Linux and Windows readers are told (AT-SPI's invalid entry, UIA's IsDataValidForForm). macOS's accessibility protocol has no such property, so say why with `set_describe` as well, which every reader speaks with the field. On a field built of parts (a number field, a search field, a rich editor) it reaches the part that edits, where a reader lands.

### `set_required`

```odin
set_required :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, required: bool)
```

set_required marks a form control as one that must be filled in, until changed: readers on all three platforms are told. It reaches a field's editing part as set_invalid does, and changes nothing drawn: say so in the label too.

### `set_tab_stop`

```odin
set_tab_stop :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, stop: bool)
```

set_tab_stop takes a focusable `n` out of the Tab order (stop = false) or puts it back: a press still focuses it and a screen reader still reaches it, as Qt's click focus and HTML's tabindex=-1 do. For a form whose keyboard path is its fields alone. It stays until changed.

### `set_inert`

```odin
set_inert :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, inert: bool)
```

set_inert makes `n`'s subtree draw as usual but take no part in interaction, as HTML's `inert` does: hit testing passes through it to the nearest ancestor that isn't inert, so hover, press, click, right-click, the wheel and tooltips go there; Tab skips its controls and assistive technology doesn't see them. Focus inside it moves to the nearest focusable ancestor, or goes. For a shell's edit mode, which shows applets as they look and picks each up whole. It stays until changed: pass it every build. Another program's applet in an `applet_slot` inside it still takes the pointer: the applet protocol cannot pass it on.

### `set_style`

```odin
set_style :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, s: Style)
```

### `set_layout`

```odin
set_layout :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, l: Layout_Props)
```

### `set_layer`

```odin
set_layer :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, layer: bool)
```

set_layer turns a node into (or back from) a layer with its own cached draw list.

### `Clipboard`

```odin
Clipboard :: struct {
	user:     rawptr,
	get:      proc(user: rawptr, allocator := context.allocator) -> (string, bool),
	set:      proc(user: rawptr, text: string) -> bool,
	types:    proc(user: rawptr, allocator := context.allocator) -> []string,                     // optional: what is offered now
	get_data: proc(user: rawptr, mime: string, allocator := context.allocator) -> ([]u8, bool), // optional: one form of it
	set_data: proc(user: rawptr, items: []Clip_Item) -> bool,                                    // optional: several forms at once
	primary_get: proc(user: rawptr, allocator := context.allocator) -> (string, bool),          // optional: the primary selection (a middle click's paste)
	primary_set: proc(user: rawptr, text: string) -> bool,                                      // optional
}
```

Clipboard is the text clipboard hook the platform installs on the tree so widgets (text input) can copy and paste without knowing the backend.

### `Timer`

```odin
Timer :: struct {
	at_ms: f64,
	node:  Node,
}
```

Timer is a pending wake-up: repaint `node` (or just wake) at `at_ms`.

### `tree_wake_at`

```odin
tree_wake_at :: proc(t: ^Tree, at_ms: f64, node: Node = NODE_NONE)
```

tree_wake_at asks for a wake-up at `at_ms` on the tree clock and, when it fires (in anim_tick), a repaint of `node`. Duplicate requests for the same node keep the earlier time.

## notices.odin

What a Baldr binary carries, and what its licences ask be passed on.

Every Baldr app embeds Baldr's fonts, because `#load` puts the bytes in the binary whether or not the app draws with them: Inter and JetBrains Mono (SIL Open Font Licence 1.1) and Twemoji Mozilla (CC-BY 4.0). It also embeds Lucide's icons as path data (ISC), tables built from Unicode's data files (Unicode License v3), and on Linux the bindings generated from the Wayland protocol definitions, each with its own notice. An app shipped on an installer or a package must carry those notices with it.

So the notices are embedded too, and `notices()` hands them over. It follows the `#config` choices: an app that swaps the UI face with `-define:BALDR_UI_FONT=…` gets a notice saying that face is its own to account for. Only the build knows what went into it, which is why this cannot live in the app. A theme's system family (`Theme.font_family`) changes what is drawn, not what is carried, so it changes nothing here.

### `Notice`

```odin
Notice :: struct {
	name:    string, // "Inter"
	use:     string, // "the UI typeface"
	licence: string, // "SIL Open Font Licence 1.1"
	text:    string, // the notice in full, as it must be passed on
}
```

Notice is one thing this binary carries that its licence asks be passed on: what it is, what Baldr uses it for, the licence's name, and the full text to show. Every string is static — embedded in the binary — so it lives as long as the program and needs no freeing.

### `notices`

```odin
notices :: proc() -> []Notice
```

notices is what this build embeds that asks to be passed on, in a stable order. An app shows them in its About or settings, alongside its own. A system family a theme sets (`Theme.font_family`) is not here: the binary does not carry it. The slice and its strings are static: nothing to free.  for n in baldr.notices() { baldr.text(ui, n.name, role = .Title) baldr.text(ui, n.use, muted = true) baldr.text(ui, n.text, size = 12) }

### `notices_text`

```odin
notices_text :: proc(allocator := context.allocator) -> string
```

notices_text is every notice run together as one block of plain text, for an app that would rather write a LICENCES file at build time or print them than lay them out. The caller owns it.

## paint.odin

Paint: each Layer node owns a cached draw list. A frame re-emits only the layers that contain a paint-dirty node; clean layers resubmit their cache. Damage is the union of the old and new rects of everything that changed.

### `Style`

```odin
Style :: struct {
	bg, fg:   Color,
	bg2:      Color,        // if alpha > 0: bottom colour of a vertical gradient (bg is the top)
	radius:   f32,
	square:   Corners, // the corners `radius` leaves square (a bar's at the screen edge; square_corners)
	border_w: f32,
	border:   Color,
	opacity:  f32,
	shadow:   Shadow_Style,
	material: Material, // frosted glass; zero = none
	glow:     Glow,     // a halo behind this node's own text or icon, whatever is behind it (icon_view's `label_over`); zero = none
	enter, exit: Transition, // played when the node appears / is removed (see transition())
}
```

Style is the resolved visual of a node (theme tokens are resolved into it by the widget layer).

### `Transition_Kind`

```odin
Transition_Kind :: enum u8 {
	None,
	Fade,
	Fade_Up,   // fade + slide up into place (from +12 px)
	Fade_Down, // fade + slide down into place (from −12 px)
	Fade_Scale, // fade + grow from TRANSITION_SCALE out of the point nearest its anchor: a menu that pops out of the button that opened it
	Slide_Start, // a slide of its whole width from (or out to) the start, shown whole through its container's clip: a page going back comes in this way
	Slide_End,   // …from (or out to) the end: a page going deeper comes in this way (pages)
}
```

Transition: how a node appears or leaves. Zero = none (instant).

### `Transition`

```odin
Transition :: struct {
	kind:     Transition_Kind,
	duration: f32, // ms; 0 = theme motion.base
	easing:   Easing,
	whole:    bool, // its backing (fill, border, shadow, glass) shows whole throughout and only what is on it fades: an overlay over controls never shows them through itself
}
```

### `TRANSITION_SCALE`

```odin
TRANSITION_SCALE :: f32(0.96)
```

How small a Fade_Scale starts. 4% reads as a pop rather than a zoom: enough to arrive from its anchor, small enough that the text inside, which is not re-rasterised at intermediate sizes (`scaled`), never looks the wrong size.

### `Material`

```odin
Material :: struct {
	tint:       Color,
	blur:       f32, // px; > 0 enables
	saturation: f32, // 1 = unchanged
	noise:      f32, // grain: fraction of an sRGB step of dither (0.012 ≈ half a step; hides banding without reading as static)
	highlight:  Color, // a bright 1 px line along the top edge, inside the corners (zero: none)
	edge:       Color, // a 1 px line just inside its outline, under the highlight (zero: none)
	shadow:     Shadow_Style, // the drop shadow a card of it casts (zero: the node's own)
}
```

Material: frosted glass behind a surface. `tint` is mixed over the blurred scene by its alpha — and is also the OPAQUE colour used when the theme's glass is Solid (OS "reduce transparency"), so text on it must be readable either way (over the app's own content the lint requires tint alpha ≥ 0.55; glass_in_app). Zero = none.

### `has_material`

```odin
has_material :: proc "contextless" (m: Material) -> bool
```

### `Shadow_Style`

```odin
Shadow_Style :: struct {
	offset: [2]f32,
	blur:   f32,
	color:  Color,
}
```

Shadow_Style: a soft drop shadow (elevation), as CSS box-shadow draws it: `blur` is its blur radius, a Gaussian of blur / 2 reaching 1.5 × blur past the offset shape. Zero = none.

### `has_shadow`

```odin
has_shadow :: proc "contextless" (s: Shadow_Style) -> bool
```

### `shadow_reach`

```odin
shadow_reach :: proc "contextless" (blur: f32) -> f32
```

shadow_reach is how far past its shape a shadow of blur radius `blur` draws: three sigmas of its Gaussian, and a pixel.

### `FOCUS_RING_OVERHANG`

```odin
FOCUS_RING_OVERHANG :: FOCUS_RING_GAP + FOCUS_RING_WIDTH + 1 // the default ring's: outside the node, plus a pixel of anti-aliasing
```

How far a focus ring and a drop ring reach outside a node, logical px.

### `paint_bounds`

```odin
paint_bounds :: proc "contextless" (d: ^Node_Data, ring: f32 = FOCUS_RING_OVERHANG) -> Rect
```

paint_bounds is the node's rect grown by whatever it draws outside it (rings, shadow, glide, overhang, glow): what a repaint dirties. `ring` is the focus ring's overhang in the theme it paints with.

### `Draw_Cmd`

```odin
Draw_Cmd :: union {
	Draw_Rect,
	Draw_Shadow,
	Draw_Backdrop,
	Draw_Text,
	Draw_Icon,
	Draw_Wave,
	Draw_Line,
	Draw_Image,
	Draw_Canvas,
	Draw_Theme_Icon,
	Draw_Clip_Push,
	Draw_Clip_Pop,
	Draw_Glow_Push,
	Draw_Glow_Pop,
	Draw_Layer,
	Draw_Cells,
	Draw_Typeset,
}
```

### `Draw_Theme_Icon`

```odin
Draw_Theme_Icon :: struct { rect: Rect, name, dir: string, color: Color, fallback: Icon } // an icon theme's icon (name and dir alias the node's), drawn once loaded; color tints one the desktop may recolour, its alpha fades any; fallback: the Lucide icon drawn when nothing provides it
```

### `Draw_Clip_Push`

```odin
Draw_Clip_Push :: struct { rect: Rect }
```

### `Draw_Glow_Push`

```odin
Draw_Glow_Push :: struct { glow: Glow } // the text and icons that follow, until the matching pop, get this glow behind them (zero: none); text on Clear glass
```

### `Layer`

```odin
Layer :: struct {
	cmds:   [dynamic]Draw_Cmd,
	bounds: Rect,
}
```

### `paint`

```odin
paint :: proc(t: ^Tree)
```

paint rebuilds every dirty layer's draw list. Call after layout.

### `frame`

```odin
frame :: proc(t: ^Tree, viewport: [2]f32)
```

frame is layout + paint.

### `rect_intersects`

```odin
rect_intersects :: proc "contextless" (a, b: Rect) -> bool
```

### `rect_clip`

```odin
rect_clip :: proc "contextless" (a, b: Rect) -> Rect
```

### `draw_list`

```odin
draw_list :: proc(t: ^Tree, allocator := context.temp_allocator) -> []Draw_Cmd
```

draw_list flattens every layer into one z-ordered command slice, in the frame's temp memory by default; the strings in it alias the nodes' own.

### `take_damage`

```odin
take_damage :: proc(t: ^Tree, allocator := context.temp_allocator) -> []Rect
```

take_damage returns the rects that changed since the last call and resets the list. Empty means "present nothing". The slice is in temp memory by default.

### `damage_bounds`

```odin
damage_bounds :: proc "contextless" (rects: []Rect) -> (r: Rect)
```

## paint_bounds_check.odin

### `Bounds_Fault`

```odin
Bounds_Fault :: struct {
	loc:     runtime.Source_Code_Location, // where the node was built
	builder: string,
	cmd:     typeid, // the kind of drawing: Draw_Text, Draw_Rect, …
	drawn:   Rect,   // the visible part of what it drew
	bounds:  Rect,   // what a repaint of it clears
}
```

Bounds_Fault is a node that drew outside its paint bounds, the part of the window a repaint of it clears: what it drew beyond them can stay on screen after it changes, and lands on its neighbours. Collected in `Tree.bounds_faults` while `Tree.debug_bounds` is on (BALDR_DEBUG_BOUNDS=1 for a running app), once per builder line and kind of drawing.

## path.odin

Paths into pixels: the SVG renderer's filling and stroking, for an app to call — an image editor's lasso into a selection mask, a shape layer, a stroke around a selection.

A Path holds moves, lines and curves in pixels, flattened as they are added (curves within SVG_TOLERANCE of a pixel). path_fill and path_stroke turn it into coverage in a Mask, the same scanline rasteriser svg_render uses (svg_draw.odin): each pixel's exact covered area, or with `antialias = false` whether its centre is inside. A mask sits anywhere in the path's space (its `origin`), so a document of any size is drawn a piece at a time, and only the rows and columns the path reaches are visited. image_paint lays a colour through a mask into a picture.

Nothing here uses the tree or a window: every proc is safe on any thread, with its working memory its own.

### `Mask`

```odin
Mask :: struct {
	origin:    [2]int,
	size:      [2]int,
	alpha:     []u8,
	allocator: runtime.Allocator,
}
```

Mask is a coverage buffer: how much of each pixel a shape covers, 0 (outside) to 255 (covered), over the rectangle `size` whose top-left pixel is at `origin` in the space paths are drawn in. `alpha` is size.x × size.y bytes, a row after another, the mask's own (mask_destroy).

### `Mask_Op`

```odin
Mask_Op :: enum u8 { Add, Subtract, Intersect, Replace }
```

Mask_Op says how a shape's coverage `c` goes into what a mask holds `m`: Add keeps the greater (a union: adding a shape twice changes nothing), Subtract takes it away (min(m, 255 − c)), Intersect keeps the lesser and clears everything outside the shape, Replace makes the mask the shape alone. They are an image editor's selection modes. Two shapes meeting edge to edge join without a seam when they are one path, filled once.

### `Fill_Rule`

```odin
Fill_Rule :: enum u8 { Nonzero, Evenodd }
```

Fill_Rule says which parts of a path that crosses itself are inside: Nonzero, any part the outline winds around; Evenodd, a part inside an odd number of its loops (a ring from two circles either way round).

### `Line_Join`

```odin
Line_Join :: enum u8 { Miter, Round, Bevel }
```

Line_Join is how a stroke turns a corner; Line_Cap how an open end ends.

### `Line_Cap`

```odin
Line_Cap :: enum u8 { Butt, Round, Square }
```

### `mask_make`

```odin
mask_make :: proc(origin, size: [2]int, allocator := context.allocator) -> (m: Mask, ok: bool)
```

mask_make is a clear mask of `size` pixels whose top-left pixel is at `origin`. False, with nothing allocated, when a side is not positive or the memory cannot be had. The mask is the caller's: mask_destroy.

### `mask_destroy`

```odin
mask_destroy :: proc(m: ^Mask)
```

mask_destroy frees a mask's pixels. Safe on the zero value and twice.

### `mask_at`

```odin
mask_at :: proc(m: ^Mask, p: [2]int) -> u8
```

mask_at is the coverage at `p` in the paths' space: 0 outside the mask.

### `Path`

```odin
Path :: struct {
	svg:       Svg_Path,
	allocator: runtime.Allocator,
}
```

Path is a shape to fill or stroke: subpaths of lines and curves, in pixels, built with path_move, path_line, path_quad, path_cubic, path_arc and path_close (or path_rect, path_ellipse, path_svg). Its memory is the allocator's it was made with (path_make); path_destroy frees it. Its fields are Baldr's.

### `path_make`

```odin
path_make :: proc(allocator := context.allocator) -> Path
```

path_make is an empty path whose memory comes from `allocator`.

### `path_destroy`

```odin
path_destroy :: proc(p: ^Path)
```

path_destroy frees a path. Safe on the zero value and twice.

### `path_clear`

```odin
path_clear :: proc(p: ^Path)
```

path_clear empties a path to be built again, keeping its allocator.

### `path_move`

```odin
path_move :: proc(p: ^Path, to: [2]f32)
```

path_move starts a subpath at `to`.

### `path_line`

```odin
path_line :: proc(p: ^Path, to: [2]f32)
```

path_line draws a straight line to `to`.

### `path_quad`

```odin
path_quad :: proc(p: ^Path, ctrl, to: [2]f32)
```

path_quad draws a quadratic curve to `to`, pulled towards `ctrl`.

### `path_cubic`

```odin
path_cubic :: proc(p: ^Path, c1, c2, to: [2]f32)
```

path_cubic draws a cubic curve to `to`, leaving towards `c1` and arriving from `c2`.

### `path_arc`

```odin
path_arc :: proc(p: ^Path, radius: [2]f32, rotation: f32, large, sweep: bool, to: [2]f32)
```

path_arc draws part of an ellipse to `to`, as SVG's `A` does: `radius` its radii, `rotation` its x axis's turn in degrees, and of the four arcs that fit, the `large` one or not, going clockwise (`sweep`) or not. Radii too small to reach are grown until they do; a zero radius draws a line.

### `path_close`

```odin
path_close :: proc(p: ^Path)
```

path_close joins the subpath back to where it started, so a stroke turns that corner rather than capping two ends. A fill closes every subpath anyway.

### `path_rect`

```odin
path_rect :: proc(p: ^Path, r: Rect)
```

path_rect adds a closed rectangle.

### `path_ellipse`

```odin
path_ellipse :: proc(p: ^Path, centre, radius: [2]f32)
```

path_ellipse adds a closed ellipse about `centre` with `radius`.

### `path_svg`

```odin
path_svg :: proc(p: ^Path, d: string) -> bool
```

path_svg adds SVG path data ("M 10 10 h 20 v 20 z"), in pixels. As SVG asks, what comes before the first error is kept; false says there was one.

### `path_bounds`

```odin
path_bounds :: proc(p: ^Path) -> (r: Rect, ok: bool)
```

path_bounds is the box around every point of the path (its curves as drawn), false when it has none. A stroke reaches half its width (more at a miter) beyond it.

### `path_fill`

```odin
path_fill :: proc(p: ^Path, m: ^Mask, rule := Fill_Rule.Nonzero, op := Mask_Op.Add, antialias := true)
```

path_fill fills `p` into `m` by `rule`, combining its coverage by `op`. With `antialias` false each pixel is covered or not by whether its centre is inside. Only the part of the path over the mask is drawn.

### `path_stroke`

```odin
path_stroke :: proc(p: ^Path, m: ^Mask, width: f32, join := Line_Join.Miter, cap := Line_Cap.Butt, miter_limit: f32 = 4, dashes: []f32 = nil, dash_offset: f32 = 0, op := Mask_Op.Add, antialias := true)
```

path_stroke strokes `p` into `m`, `width` pixels wide, combining its coverage by `op`: corners turned by `join` (a miter longer than `miter_limit` times the width is bevelled), open ends by `cap`. With `dashes`, lengths on and off in turn (an odd list goes round twice, as in SVG), the stroke is dashed from `dash_offset` along it.

### `image_paint`

```odin
image_paint :: proc(img: ^Image, m: ^Mask, color: Color)
```

image_paint lays `color` into `img` through `m` (placed at its origin in the picture's pixels): each pixel takes the colour at the colour's alpha times the mask's coverage, over what is there. The blend is on the stored sRGB values, as svg_render's and an image editor's normal layer blend are, and the picture keeps straight alpha. The part painted is touched (image_touch), so a picture on show is uploaded again in part.

## path_open.odin

Opening a file the app itself made, and showing one in its folder: an exported spreadsheet opened in the person's spreadsheet program, or its folder shown so they can attach it to an email.

Deliberately not `open_url`, which takes URLs out of content and so allows only http, https and mailto. A path is the app's own; what is checked here is what it is, so a mistake upstream cannot turn "open my spreadsheet" into "run this".

### `PATH_MAX_LEN`

```odin
PATH_MAX_LEN :: 4096
```

PATH_MAX_LEN is the longest path handed on. Every filesystem in use stops far below this; it is here so a runaway string cannot become an argument.

### `Path_Opened`

```odin
Path_Opened :: struct {
	path: string,
	ok:   bool,
}
```

Path_Opened is what became of `open_path` or `reveal_path`: the path as it was asked for, and whether the desktop took it. False means no handler is registered for that kind of file, the desktop refused, the person dismissed a chooser, or the path was not one Baldr opens — the app can then say where the file is instead. The string lives only while the message is handled; clone what you keep.

### `open_path`

```odin
open_path :: proc($Msg: typeid, path: string, on_done: Handler(Msg, Path_Opened) = nil) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

open_path asks the desktop to open a local file with whatever the person uses for that kind of file. The toolkit never picks the program and never runs a command line; the desktop's handler does (the OpenURI portal on Linux, ShellExecuteW on Windows). A folder opens as itself in the file manager, unlike `reveal_path`.  The path must exist, be a regular file or a folder, and not be executable (`path_openable`); a refused path comes back at once as `on_done` with `ok = false`.  case Open_Export: return baldr.open_path(Msg, s.export_path, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Path_Opened)) -> Msg { return Tell_Them_Where_It_Is{} if !e.value.ok else Nothing{} })

### `reveal_path`

```odin
reveal_path :: proc($Msg: typeid, path: string, on_done: Handler(Msg, Path_Opened) = nil) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

reveal_path asks the desktop to show a local file in its folder, picked out where the file manager can do that ("Show in folder").  A folder is revealed as a file is: its parent opens with the folder picked out (Explorer's `/select,`, the portal's OpenDirectory). To show a folder's contents, use `open_path`. Where there is no portal the folder opens with nothing picked out, since no file manager selects a file from a command line; the handler is told the same either way.

### `path_openable`

```odin
path_openable :: proc(path: string, dir_ok := false) -> bool
```

path_openable says whether Baldr will hand this path to the desktop. It is about what the path *is*, not where it came from:  - not empty, no NUL, no longer than PATH_MAX_LEN; - it exists, and `stat` can say what it is; - a regular file, or — when `dir_ok` — a folder; - not executable by anyone; - not a kind of file a desktop runs rather than opens, whatever its permissions say: a program, a script, an installer, a shortcut or a launcher (`PATH_RUNS`), and on macOS an application bundle.  Handing a handler any of those turns "open" into "run". Windows reports no execute bit on a file at all, and a `.app` is a folder, so the bits alone would let a saved attachment named `report.pdf.exe` start. `open_path` and `reveal_path` both allow folders; `dir_ok` lets an app ask the narrower question.

## png_write.odin

Writing PNG: a picture copied to the clipboard goes as image/png, and an image editor saves and exports the same way. Odin's core reads PNG but does not write it, and the dependency rule keeps zlib out, so the deflate compressor is here: LZ77 over a 32 KB window (hash chains, one step of lazy matching), each block coded with Huffman codes built for it (RFC 1951's dynamic blocks), and a block that would not shrink stored as it is. Each row takes the PNG filter that leaves it smallest by the usual measure (the sum of its bytes as signed values).

### `image_encode_png`

```odin
image_encode_png :: proc(img: ^Image, allocator := context.allocator) -> (png: []u8, ok: bool)
```

image_encode_png is `img` as a PNG file: 8-bit RGBA, or RGB when every pixel is opaque, straight alpha as the Image has it. The bytes are `allocator`'s; false for an empty or malformed Image. Safe on any thread (it reads only `img`).

## pointer_area.odin

Pointer area: a plain node that takes the pointer the way a picture is handled (the wheel, a double-click, a pointer shape of its own) without becoming a control.

### `pointer_area`

```odin
pointer_area :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, on_scroll: Handler(Msg, Scroll) = nil, on_double_click: Maybe(Msg) = nil,
	cursor: Cursor = .Default, cursor_image: ^Image = nil, hotspot: [2]int = {})
```

pointer_area lets node `n` (a picture in a viewer) take the pointer without becoming a control: no role, no focus, no hover or press look. `on_scroll` is a wheel or touchpad scroll over it, with `Scroll.pos` where the pointer is over it (a zoom about the pointer); a scroll view around it no longer takes the wheel there. `on_double_click` is two presses on it in quick succession, close together (a double-tap too): fit ↔ 100 %. `cursor` is the pointer's shape over it and, while a press that started on it is held, anywhere (Grab over a picture that pans, Grabbing while it does, Hidden in a slideshow once the pointer has been still); `cursor_image` makes it a picture the app drew instead (a brush's outline, which moves with the pointer as a drawn outline cannot), in the screen's pixels (`device_scale`), with `hotspot` the pixel that is the pointer's point; change the picture (a new `version`) and the cursor follows. A pen over it takes the same cursor. A control inside it keeps its own presses, wheel and shape. Call it every build with what it answers now; all nil and `.Default` removes it. Give what it does a keyboard route too (a key, a button): it adds nothing to the accessibility tree. With `pointer_track` for where the pointer is and whether it is held, that is a draggable picture.  baldr.pointer_area(ui, pic, on_scroll = zoom_at, on_double_click = Msg.Fit_Or_Actual, cursor = .Grabbing if s.panning else .Grab if s.pannable else .Default)

### `pointer_area_picture`

```odin
pointer_area_picture :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg)) -> (^Image, [2]int, bool)
```

pointer_area_picture is the picture the pointer is now, and its hotspot, when an area's cursor is one.

## pointer_samples.odin

Every pointer sample: a canvas hears the whole path the pointer or pen took, not one position a frame. A 1000 Hz mouse or a 200 Hz pen moves many times between two frames, and a brush stroke is that path, with its timing (a velocity brush, smoothing), the buttons and keys held (a right-drag, Shift for a straight line), and for a pen its pressure, tilt, rotation, distance and which end is down. The samples come in one message a frame, so an app is not woken a thousand times a second.

### `Pen_Tool`

```odin
Pen_Tool :: enum u8 { Mouse, Pen, Eraser, Brush, Pencil, Airbrush, Finger, Lens }
```

Pen_Tool is what made a sample: the mouse, or a tablet tool — a pen, its eraser end, a brush, a pencil, an airbrush, a finger, a puck, a lens.

### `Mouse_Buttons`

```odin
Mouse_Buttons :: bit_set[Mouse_Button; u8]
```

Mouse_Buttons is the buttons held; a pen's tip down is Left, its barrel buttons Right and Middle.

### `Sample_Kind`

```odin
Sample_Kind :: enum u8 { Move, Press, Release, Enter, Leave }
```

Sample_Kind is what a sample is: a move, a button (or the pen's tip) pressed or let go, or a pen coming near the tablet over the node and going away (a brush outline follows a hovering pen between the two).

### `Pointer_Sample`

```odin
Pointer_Sample :: struct {
	kind:     Sample_Kind,
	pos:      [2]f32,        // from the node's top-left, sub-pixel
	time_ms:  f64,           // the event's own time, on the compositor's clock: differences are what mean anything
	button:   Mouse_Button,  // Press / Release: which
	buttons:  Mouse_Buttons, // held once this sample is taken
	mods:     Mods,
	tool:     Pen_Tool,
	pressure: f32,           // 0..1; a mouse: 1 while a button is held
	tilt:     [2]f32,        // degrees from upright, along x and y
	rotation: f32,           // degrees, where the pen reports it
	distance: f32,           // 0..1 above the tablet while hovering; -1 where it isn't reported
	contact:  bool,          // the pen's tip is down (a mouse: its left button is)
	clicks:   int,           // Press: 1, 2 for a double click, 3 for a triple (the same button, soon and near the last); else 0
}
```

Pointer_Sample is one input event over a node that hears them.

### `Pointer_Samples`

```odin
Pointer_Samples :: struct {
	samples: []Pointer_Sample,
	size:    [2]f32,
}
```

Pointer_Samples is a frame's samples over a node, oldest first, and the node's size. `samples` is the node's own: valid while the message is handled (the next frame's samples go elsewhere), so copy what you keep.

### `pointer_samples`

```odin
pointer_samples :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, on_samples: Handler(Msg, Pointer_Samples), hover := false)
```

pointer_samples makes node `n` (a canvas) hear every pointer or pen sample through `on_samples`: all of them while a press that began on it is held, wherever they go, through to the release; and with `hover`, while the pointer or pen is over it with nothing held too (a brush outline following the pen). They come in one message a frame. A pen's tip also presses and drags as the left button everywhere else, so the rest of the window works with it; `pointer_track` still gives the one position a frame for what needs no more. nil stops it.

### `pointer_sample`

```odin
pointer_sample :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), s: Pointer_Sample, pos: [2]f32)
```

pointer_sample takes one input event at `pos` (tree coordinates): the areas it falls to keep it. A press over an area holds it; the release that lets the last button go is its last held sample.

### `mouse_sample`

```odin
mouse_sample :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), kind: Sample_Kind, button: Mouse_Button, time_ms: u32, pos: [2]f32, counted := false)
```

mouse_sample takes a mouse event of `kind` at `pos` as a sample, after the pointer has acted on it (so `buttons` is what is held now). `counted` says the press was already counted — by `mouse_button_event`, which counts *before* the press acts — so it is read here rather than counted twice.

### `mouse_button_event`

```odin
mouse_button_event :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), button: Mouse_Button, pressed: bool, at: [2]f32, time_ms: u32) -> (changed: bool)
```

mouse_button_event is a mouse button as the run loop delivers it, and the one place its order lives: counted first, then acted on, then taken as a sample. A widget that reads a double click (selecting a word) reads `ui.pointer.clicks.count` while the press is handled, so counting after would hand it the previous press's count. Tests go through this too, so they exercise the same order.

### `pen_event`

```odin
pen_event :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), e: platform.Ev_Pen, at: [2]f32) -> (changed: bool)
```

pen_event acts on a tablet tool's frame: the tip is the left button, the first barrel button the right and the second the middle, coming near and going away the pointer arriving and leaving (so a pen works the whole window), and the frame is a sample, with its pressure, tilt, rotation and distance, for a node that hears them. `at` is the frame's position in the tree. True when something visible changed.

## pointer_track.odin

Pointer tracking: where the pointer is over a node as it moves.

### `Pointer_At`

```odin
Pointer_At :: struct {
	pos, size: [2]f32,
	inside:    bool,
	held:      bool,
	buttons:   bit_set[Mouse_Button], // which buttons are holding it; empty when none is
	press:     [2]f32,
}
```

Pointer_At is where the pointer is over a node that tracks it: `pos` from the node's top-left, the node's `size`, whether the pointer is `inside` it (or anything in it), and whether a button pressed over it is still `held` — and while it is, `press`, where that press was over it. Reports come at most once a frame, so the press and the first moves can arrive as one report already moved; a drag goes by `pos - press` and loses none of its start. `buttons` is which buttons hold it, so a node with a menu can tell a right press from a left one; it is empty when none is down, and `held` is `buttons != {}`.

### `pointer_track`

```odin
pointer_track :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, on_hover: Handler(Msg, Pointer_At), buttons: bit_set[Mouse_Button] = {})
```

pointer_track makes `n` report where the pointer is over it through `on_hover`: at most once a frame while the pointer moves over it or anything inside it, then once with `inside` false when the pointer leaves it or the surface, or the node goes away. While a button pressed over it is held, it goes on reporting until the release, outside it too, with `held` set. Unlike hoverable, it also hears the pointer arriving without a move (given back after a grab, or a surface appearing under it). Nothing is reported that it already knew. nil stops it. `buttons` is which buttons may hold it: `{}`, the default, is any; `{.Left}` makes a right or middle press neither hold it nor report, which a node that also has a `context_area` wants, since a menu's press is not a drag's start. What is held is reported in `Pointer_At.buttons` either way.  baldr.pointer_track(ui, shelf, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Pointer_At)) -> Msg { return Dock_Pointer{e.value.pos.x, e.value.inside} })

## render.odin

Render: turns a Tree's logical-pixel draw list into device-pixel gpu.Cmds.

render_prepare is the only bridge between the retained draw lists and the gpu package. It scales and pixel-snaps rects and clips, lays text out through the Tree's Text_Engine at the logical size (so line breaks are the ones layout measured), rasterises glyphs at the device size, and hands atlas pages to the renderer through an Atlas_Sink. Nothing here calls the gpu package's procs directly: the run loop supplies a sink that wraps gpu.atlas_create / gpu.atlas_upload, and headless tests pass nil.

### `Corner`

```odin
Corner :: gpu.Corner
```

Corner names a corner of a rounded shape; Corners are those it keeps square, its other corners taking its radius (Style.square, square_corners).

### `Corners`

```odin
Corners :: gpu.Corners
```

### `Pixels`

```odin
Pixels :: gpu.Pixels
```

Pixels is how a magnified picture is sampled: `Auto` blends smooth into hard-edged squares between 2 and 4 device pixels a picture pixel, and `Smooth` / `Hard` force one of them. A builder takes it as `pixelated` (nothing said: Auto); it is named in a `Draw_Image` or `Draw_Canvas`.

### `Atlas_Sink`

```odin
Atlas_Sink :: struct {
	user:    rawptr,
	create:  proc(user: rawptr, format: gpu.Page_Format, size: [2]u32) -> gpu.Page,
	upload:  proc(user: rawptr, page: gpu.Page, x, y, w, h: u32, pixels: []u8),
	picture: proc(user: rawptr, size: [2]u32) -> gpu.Page, // one picture's own RGBA8 texture with mip levels (gpu.picture_create); nil: pictures stay in the atlas
	destroy: proc(user: rawptr, page: gpu.Page),            // a picture's texture that is no longer wanted
}
```

Atlas_Sink is how render_prepare gives glyph atlas pages to the renderer. `create` makes a texture of `format` and `size` and returns its handle; `upload` copies a tightly packed `w × h` block of pixels (1 byte/px for Alpha8, 4 for RGBA8) to `(x, y)` of `page`; `picture` and `destroy` make and free a big (or much reduced) picture's texture of its own. Uploads are staged by the renderer and land before the next render, which is after render_prepare returns, so emitting a Glyph before its page's upload call is fine.

### `render_prepare`

```odin
render_prepare :: proc(t: ^Tree, scale: f32, sink: ^Atlas_Sink, out: ^[dynamic]gpu.Cmd, offset: [2]f32 = {})
```

render_prepare appends the device-pixel commands for the whole tree to `out`, in z-order. `scale` is device px per logical px (<= 0 is treated as 1). Rect and clip edges are rounded to device pixels; radius and border width are scaled. Text is drawn only when the tree has a Text_Engine (tree_use_text_engine); atlas pages are created and their dirty regions uploaded through `sink`, or merely numbered when `sink` is nil. Per-frame scratch goes to context.temp_allocator.

### `render_popup`

```odin
render_popup :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, origin: [2]f32, scale: f32, sink: ^Atlas_Sink, out: ^[dynamic]gpu.Cmd)
```

render_popup appends the device-pixel commands of popup `n`'s surface: its card, with tree point `origin` at the surface's top-left.

### `PICTURE_MINIFY`

```odin
PICTURE_MINIFY :: 2
```

PICTURE_MINIFY: a picture drawn at more of its pixels than this to a screen pixel gets a texture of its own with mip levels; bilinear sampling from the atlas skips texels past two and shimmers.

### `PICTURE_IDLE_BYTES`

```odin
PICTURE_IDLE_BYTES :: 192 * 1024 * 1024
```

PICTURE_IDLE_BYTES is how much of the pictures with textures of their own that have not been drawn for PICTURE_IDLE_MS stays on the GPU (a viewer's last few photos, to go back to) before the oldest go.

### `image_slot`

```odin
image_slot :: proc(te: ^Text_Engine, img: ^Image) -> (slot: runa.Atlas_Slot, ok: bool)
```

image_slot uploads `img` (downscaled by an integer factor when it does not fit an atlas page) once per version and returns its slot.

### `IMAGE_ATLAS_PAGES`

```odin
IMAGE_ATLAS_PAGES :: 4
```

IMAGE_ATLAS_PAGES is how many pages (4 MB each) pictures fill before the ones not drawn lately make way.

### `icon_slot`

```odin
icon_slot :: proc(te: ^Text_Engine, which: Icon, px: int) -> (slot: runa.Atlas_Slot, ok: bool)
```

icon_slot returns the atlas slot of `which` at `px` device pixels, rasterising and packing it on first use.

## reveal.odin

Reveal on hover: a node shown only while the pointer is over what it belongs to, or focus is inside it — an album cover's Play, a tab's close, a row's actions. Hover does not rebuild the view, so this is decided as the tree paints and hit-tests: a revealed node fades in over the theme's fast duration, and one that is not shown is not drawn and cannot be hit, so a click on its place lands on what it belongs to.

### `reveal_within`

```odin
reveal_within :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, owner: Node, shown := false)
```

reveal_within shows node `n` only while the pointer is over `owner` (or anything in it, `n` included) or focus is inside `owner` — or while `shown` says so: the item the keyboard is on, whose action should be in sight. Call it every build; a node built without it again is always shown. Its action must be reachable some other way too — the keyboard reaches it through focus, which reveals it — as a hidden control is nothing to someone who cannot hover.

## reveal_cmd.odin

Bringing a row into view, as a command.

A list is rebuilt every time anything changes, so "scroll to this row" cannot be a value the view passes in: it would scroll there again on every build. It is a command instead, the shape `focus` uses: the ask happens once, where the app decided it, and asking for the same row twice is calling it twice. The ask is kept for a few builds, because the command usually causes the screen holding the list to be built; one no view takes in that time is dropped, so a key that never appears cannot be claimed later by an unrelated screen.

`update` returns a command (`reveal_key`, `reveal_index`, `hold`, `hold_at`); a view that learns the row while building asks directly (`reveal_now` and its three peers). Either way it is the same ask, claimed by the same views, and a window holds a set of them, so two asks in one update are two asks.

### `Reveal_Ask`

```odin
Reveal_Ask :: struct {
	view:     u64,        // the list, table or icon view's own reconciliation key
	key:      u64,        // a `list` row, by the key `key_of` gives it
	index:    int,        // a `table` or `icon_view` row, by position
	at:       f32,        // where in the viewport it lands, 0..1 from the top
	by_index: bool,       // which of the two above names the row
	surface:  Maybe(u64), // which window's view, by its surface tag; nil is whichever has it
	internal: bool,       // the toolkit asking one of its own views (reveal_own)
	in_build: bool,       // asked from inside a build (reveal_now), so a build is owed to it
	tries:    int,        // builds this ask has waited for a view to take it
}
```

Reveal_Ask is what a reveal command carries. Built by `reveal_key` and `reveal_index` rather than by hand.

### `reveal_key`

```odin
reveal_key :: proc($Msg: typeid, view: u64, row: u64, at: f32 = 0.33, surface: Maybe(u64) = nil) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

reveal_key brings a `list` row into view, the row named by the key `key_of` gives it. `view` is the list's own `key`, which is how this finds it among the surfaces; it need only be unique among the app's own views (the lists the toolkit builds inside `table`, `icon_view` and the emoji picker are kept apart), and a multi-window app can name the window with `surface`. `at` is where in the viewport the row lands, a third of the way down by default: a message against the very top reads as the start of a conversation when it is not. Revealing the last row pins the list to the bottom and resumes following, so it reports `at_end` straight away.  LOG :: u64(0x109) baldr.list(ui, len(s.rows), key_of = msg_key, key = LOG)   // in the view return baldr.reveal_key(Msg, LOG, msg_key(nil, len(s.rows) - 1))  // in update

### `reveal_index`

```odin
reveal_index :: proc($Msg: typeid, view: u64, row: int, at: f32 = 0.33, surface: Maybe(u64) = nil) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

reveal_index brings a `table` or `icon_view` row into view — those name a row by its position, which is what they are given to build. Otherwise exactly `reveal_key`.

### `reveal_now`

```odin
reveal_now :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), view: u64, row: u64, at: f32 = 0.33, surface: Maybe(u64) = nil)
```

reveal_now brings a row into view, asked from inside the build that knows which row it is. Otherwise exactly `reveal_key`.  // in the view, right after typesetting said which row the chapter is baldr.reveal_now(ui, READ_LIST, row, at = 0)

### `reveal_now_index`

```odin
reveal_now_index :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), view: u64, row: int, at: f32 = 0.33, surface: Maybe(u64) = nil)
```

reveal_now_index is reveal_now for a `table` or an `icon_view`, which name a row by its position. Otherwise exactly `reveal_index`.

### `hold_now`

```odin
hold_now :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), view: u64, row: u64, point: [2]f32, at: [2]f32,
	axes: Hold_Axes = {.X, .Y}, surface: Maybe(u64) = nil)
```

hold_now keeps a point of a row still, asked from inside the build. Otherwise exactly `hold`, with the same rules as `reveal_now`.

### `hold_now_at`

```odin
hold_now_at :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), view: u64, at: [2]f32,
	axes: Hold_Axes = {.X, .Y}, surface: Maybe(u64) = nil)
```

hold_now_at is `hold_at` asked from inside the build.

### `Hold_Axis`

```odin
Hold_Axis :: enum u8 { X, Y }
```

Hold_Axis is an axis a hold keeps still. A hold that names only `.Y` leaves the horizontal offset exactly where the reader left it — which is what a document zoomed by its page wants, since the page's width is the app's business and the view's scroll across it is the reader's.

### `Hold_Axes`

```odin
Hold_Axes :: bit_set[Hold_Axis; u8]
```

### `Hold_Ask`

```odin
Hold_Ask :: struct {
	view:     u64,        // the list's own reconciliation key
	key:      u64,        // the row, by the key `key_of` gives it — 0 with by_spot
	point:    [2]f32,     // px into that row
	at:       [2]f32,     // px into the viewport
	axes:     Hold_Axes,  // which offsets it may move; {} is both
	by_spot:  bool,       // no row named: keep whatever the list has at `at` now
	surface:  Maybe(u64), // which window's list, by its surface tag; nil is whichever has it
	internal: bool,       // the toolkit asking one of its own lists
	in_build: bool,       // asked from inside a build (hold_now), so a build is owed to it
	tries:    int,        // builds this ask has waited
}
```

Hold_Ask is what a hold command carries: put this point of that row at this spot in the viewport, once, in the build that changed the scale. `axes` says which offsets it may move; an empty set means both, so the zero value moves both.

### `hold`

```odin
hold :: proc($Msg: typeid, view: u64, row: u64, point: [2]f32, at: [2]f32,
	axes: Hold_Axes = {.X, .Y}, surface: Maybe(u64) = nil) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

hold keeps a point still while everything around it changes size: the page under the pointer, or the middle of the view, must not move while a document zooms. Return it from `update` with the zoom that needs it, so it applies in the build that changed the scale, before the range is chosen, and no frame is built from the old offsets. `point` is px into the row and `at` px into the viewport. It is a command rather than a parameter because an app names the row it tracks on every build, so naming cannot be the ask.

### `hold_at`

```odin
hold_at :: proc($Msg: typeid, view: u64, at: [2]f32,
	axes: Hold_Axes = {.X, .Y}, surface: Maybe(u64) = nil) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

hold_at keeps whatever is at that spot of the viewport where it is, naming no row: the list works out which row is there and how far into it from the offset it is actually on when the ask is claimed. Prefer it to `hold` wherever the app knows the position only from the last build: a list does not rebuild for a scroll that keeps the same rows on screen, so an app's own note of the position can be behind, and the list's never is. What is kept is a fraction of the row, not pixels, so the app has nothing to scale.  // in update, in the same breath as the zoom return baldr.hold_at(Msg, PAGES, at = {0, s.pointer.y}, axes = {.Y})

## rich.odin

Rich paragraph layout: one block of text whose runs differ in face (bold, italic, code), wrapped at UAX #14 break opportunities. runa lays out one style per paragraph, so each run is shaped on its own (through the engine's paragraph cache) and the lines are filled here, greedily, from the runs' advances. The result answers everything an editor asks: the wrapped lines with their byte ranges and widths, the x of any byte offset, the offset nearest an x, and the segments to draw (one Draw_Text per run per line). Without a text engine the headless rule applies: half the size per rune.

### `Rich_Style`

```odin
Rich_Style :: struct {
	size:   f32,
	bold:   bool,
	italic: bool,
	mono:   bool, // inline code
	attrs:  Attrs,
	href:   string,
	family: Family_Id, // the family an editor was set to (0: the theme's fonts)
}
```

Rich_Style is what a run is drawn with.

### `Rich_Seg`

```odin
Rich_Seg :: struct {
	start, end: int, // bytes of the block
	x, width:   f32, // from the line's start
	run:        int, // index into the layout's styles
	level:      u8,  // bidi embedding level: odd runs right to left, so its first byte is at its right edge
}
```

Rich_Seg is one run's part of one line.

### `Rich_Line`

```odin
Rich_Line :: struct {
	start, end: int,
	width:      f32,
	x:          f32, // from the block's left: 0 in flowing text, the column's text edge in a table row
	y:          f32, // from the block's top: `line_h` apart in flowing text, and a cell's own stack in a table row
	segs:       []Rich_Seg, // in visual order, left to right
	bidi:       bool,       // holds right-to-left text: its segments are reordered and split by direction
}
```

### `Rich_Layout`

```odin
Rich_Layout :: struct {
	lines:  []Rich_Line,
	cum:    []f32,
	styles: []Rich_Style,
	runs:   []Run, // the block's runs the layout was built for (plain: one run)
	line_h: f32,
	ascent: f32,
	height: f32, // the whole block's: `line_h` a line, or the tallest cell in a table row
}
```

Rich_Layout is a block's wrapped layout: lines, the cumulative x of every byte (cum[len(text)] is the total width), and the style of every run.  A line's `y` is what stacks it, not its index: in flowing text the two say the same thing, but a table row's cells each wrap within their own column, so the row holds one line per cell line and they are not one stack.

### `rich_layout`

```odin
rich_layout :: proc(t: ^Tree, text: string, runs: []Run, base: Rich_Style, wrap_w: f32, allocator := context.temp_allocator) -> Rich_Layout
```

rich_layout lays `text` out with `runs` (empty = one plain run) at the block's base `style`, wrapped at `wrap_w` (0 = one line). Everything is allocated with `allocator`.

### `rich_spans`

```odin
rich_spans :: proc(lay: ^Rich_Layout, line: int, a, b: int) -> [][2]f32
```

rich_spans is where bytes [a, b) of `line` are drawn: one x range per piece they touch, several where directions mix, in the frame's temp memory.

### `rich_x`

```odin
rich_x :: proc(lay: ^Rich_Layout, line: int, off: int) -> f32
```

rich_x is the x of byte `off` from the start of its line.

### `rich_hit`

```odin
rich_hit :: proc(lay: ^Rich_Layout, text: string, line: int, x: f32) -> int
```

rich_hit is the grapheme boundary of `line` nearest to `x`.

### `rich_line_at`

```odin
rich_line_at :: proc(lay: ^Rich_Layout, x, y: f32) -> int
```

rich_line_at is the line a point falls on, `x` and `y` from the block's top-left. Flowing text has one line at each height, so `y` decides it; a table row has one line per cell at the same height, so `x` decides which cell, and a point past every cell's text takes the nearest.

### `rich_line_of`

```odin
rich_line_of :: proc(lay: ^Rich_Layout, off: int, text_len: int) -> int
```

rich_line_of finds the wrapped line holding `off` (an offset at a wrap boundary belongs to the line it ends, except at the block's end).

### `table_cell_min`

```odin
table_cell_min :: proc(lay: ^Rich_Layout, text: string, cell: [2]int) -> f32
```

table_cell_min is the narrowest a cell can be without its text sticking out: its longest run between break opportunities, measured from a flow layout of the row. Spaces and hard breaks separate; anything else is one piece, so a long word decides a column's minimum.

### `table_fit_cols`

```odin
table_fit_cols :: proc(widths: []f32, mins: []f32, wrap_w: f32)
```

table_fit_cols brings a table's columns inside `wrap_w`.  Scaling them all down in proportion is fine until a column is squeezed below its own longest word, which then sticks out of it — and a cell that sticks out of its column runs on under the next one. So every column keeps at least its minimum, and what is left over is shared out in proportion to how much each one asked for beyond that: the column with the long prose gives up the most, which is the one that had the most to give.  If even the minimums do not fit, they are scaled down together. There is nothing else to be done at that point, and the cells break mid-word.

### `table_row_layout`

```odin
table_row_layout :: proc(t: ^Tree, text: string, runs: []Run, base: Rich_Style, cols: []f32, raw: bool, allocator := context.temp_allocator, align: []Table_Align = nil) -> Rich_Layout
```

table_row_layout lays a table row out: every cell wrapped inside its own column, the row as tall as its tallest cell, so a long cell grows the row downwards rather than running under the next column. The row's lines are one per cell line, each carrying its own `y`, because a row's cells are not one stack (two cells' second lines share a height but not a run of bytes); they come out in byte order, so anything walking them by offset still walks them in order.

## scroll.odin

Scroll containers. A node flagged Scroll_V / Scroll_H lets its children overflow along that axis; layout shifts them by Scroll_State.offset, so hit-testing, focus, and painting all see the true positions — nothing is special-cased downstream. Wheel input bubbles from the node under the pointer to the nearest ancestor that can still move in that direction. An overlay scrollbar fades in while scrolling and can be dragged.

### `SCROLL_END_EPS`

```odin
SCROLL_END_EPS :: f32(2)
```

SCROLL_END_EPS is how near the end still counts as at it: a fractional line left over from a layout must not stop a chat following its own last message.

### `scroll_state_new`

```odin
scroll_state_new :: proc "contextless" () -> Scroll_State
```

scroll_state_new is a fresh state whose scrollbar is hidden (no activity yet).

### `scroll_clamp`

```odin
scroll_clamp :: proc "contextless" (st: Scroll_State, off: [2]f32) -> [2]f32
```

scroll_clamp keeps an offset inside [0, content - viewport] per axis.

### `scroll_offset`

```odin
scroll_offset :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node) -> [2]f32
```
