scroll_offset returns the current offset of a scroll container (zero for anything else).

### `scroll_viewport`

```odin
scroll_viewport :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node) -> [2]f32
```

scroll_viewport is how much of a scroll container is visible: the size inside its padding, as the last layout measured it (zero for anything else, and before the first layout). An app needs it to say where in the view it wants something: `hold_at` is px into the viewport, so holding the middle still across a zoom asks for half of this. Read it in the build that uses it rather than keeping it, since a window resize changes it.

### `scroll_to`

```odin
scroll_to :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, off: [2]f32) -> bool
```

scroll_to sets the offset (clamped) and re-lays the container. Returns true if it moved.

### `scroll_by`

```odin
scroll_by :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, delta: [2]f32) -> bool
```

scroll_by moves by a delta (logical px). Returns true if it moved.

### `scroll_event`

```odin
scroll_event :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), pos: [2]f32, delta: [2]f32, precise := false, mods: Mods = {}) -> bool
```

scroll_event dispatches a wheel/trackpad delta at `pos`: a control under the pointer that listens for scrolls (`on_scroll`) takes it; else the innermost scroll container that can move in that direction; otherwise it bubbles up. `precise` marks a touchpad. Returns true if anything moved or a message was queued.

### `scroll_to_smooth`

```odin
scroll_to_smooth :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, off: [2]f32) -> bool
```

scroll_to_smooth eases the offset to `off` over a short time (keyboard jumps, reveals): the same clamping as scroll_to, instant under reduce-motion. Wheel, drag or fling input takes over immediately.

### `scroll_fling`

```odin
scroll_fling :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, velocity: [2]f32)
```

scroll_fling starts a decelerating scroll at `velocity` px/s (a touch pan let go). Instant stop under reduce-motion.

### `scroll_ancestor_axis`

```odin
scroll_ancestor_axis :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, axis: int) -> Node
```

scroll_ancestor_axis returns the nearest ancestor of `n` (or `n`) that scrolls along the given axis (0 = horizontal, 1 = vertical), or NONE.

### `scrollbar_geometry`

```odin
scrollbar_geometry :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, axis := 1) -> (track, thumb: Rect, ok: bool)
```

scrollbar_geometry returns the overlay scrollbar's track and thumb rects — the vertical one by default, the one along the bottom with `axis = 0` — or ok = false when nothing overflows that way. Where a container scrolls both ways each track stops short of the other's end, so the two thumbs never sit on top of each other in the corner.

### `scrollbar_visible`

```odin
scrollbar_visible :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node) -> f32
```

scrollbar_visible is the 0..1 fade amount of the scrollbar for painting: full while scrolling, dragging, or with the pointer in the bar zone; dimmed while the pointer rests anywhere inside the container; gone otherwise.

### `scrollbar_zone`

```odin
scrollbar_zone :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, axis := 1) -> (zone: Rect, ok: bool)
```

scrollbar_zone is the strip along the right edge — the bottom edge with `axis = 0` — where presses go to the scrollbar and the pointer counts as hovering it (wider than the bar).

### `scroll_ancestor`

```odin
scroll_ancestor :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node) -> Node
```

scroll_ancestor returns `n` or its nearest ancestor that scrolls (or NONE).

### `scroll_hover_update`

```odin
scroll_hover_update :: proc(t: ^Tree, hit: Node, pos: [2]f32) -> (changed: bool)
```

scroll_hover_update tracks which container (and whose bar zone) the pointer is over so the bar can reveal itself. Returns true if paint changed.

### `scroll_into_view`

```odin
scroll_into_view :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, instant := false) -> (moved: bool)
```

scroll_into_view scrolls every scrolling ancestor, on whichever axis it scrolls, so that `n` is visible (keyboard focus must never land off-screen). Smooth unless `instant` (a resize never animates). Returns true if any moved.

### `scroll_key`

```odin
scroll_key :: proc(t: ^Tree, key: Key, hover_claims := true, mods: Mods = {}) -> bool
```

scroll_key scrolls the container that has keyboard focus inside it (else the one under the pointer) by a page or to an end. Returns true if it moved.  `hover_claims` is false when the window bound this key with `shortcut`: the pointer merely resting over a scroll is a weaker claim on a Page key than a binding is. A scroll the keyboard is inside still takes the key first either way.

### `scrollbar_press`

```odin
scrollbar_press :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, pos: [2]f32) -> bool
```

scrollbar_press starts a thumb drag if `pos` is in the bar zone: on the thumb it grabs, on the track it jumps the thumb there. Returns true if the press was consumed.

### `scrollbar_drag`

```odin
scrollbar_drag :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, pos: [2]f32) -> bool
```

### `scrollbar_release`

```odin
scrollbar_release :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node)
```

## scroll_input.odin

Scroll on a control: a wheel or touchpad scroll over a button that listens for it (`on_scroll`: a tray icon's Scroll, a volume applet, a workspace switcher) goes to it instead of to a scroll view around it, and focus stays where it is.

### `Scroll`

```odin
Scroll :: struct {
	steps:   [2]int,
	delta:   [2]f32,
	precise: bool,
	mods:    Mods,
	pos:     [2]f32,
}
```

Scroll is a wheel or touchpad scroll over a control. `steps` are whole wheel clicks along each axis, +y when the wheel turns down (the way content would move up), a touchpad's smooth motion adding up to a step every SCROLL_STEP_PX; `delta` is this event's logical px; `precise` says it came from a touchpad; `mods` are the modifiers held (Shift turns a vertical wheel sideways before it arrives); `pos` is where the pointer is over the node that took it, from its top-left (a zoom about the pointer).

### `SCROLL_STEP_PX`

```odin
SCROLL_STEP_PX :: platform.SCROLL_STEP_PX
```

SCROLL_STEP_PX is how far a touchpad moves for one wheel step.

## selection.odin

Selection is the app-owned set of selected rows of a table (or list), mutated in place by the widget the way `Text` is by a text input: the app keeps it in its state, passes a pointer to the builder, reads it whenever it likes, and hears about changes through `on_select`. `cursor` is the row keyboard navigation and Shift ranges work from; `anchor` is where the current Shift range started. The zero value is an empty single-select; set `multi` for Ctrl / Shift / Ctrl+A.

### `Selection`

```odin
Selection :: struct {
	rows:   map[int]struct{},
	cursor: int, // -1 = none
	anchor: int, // -1 = none
	multi:  bool,
	// version counts changes to `rows`, so anything watching the selection
	// (the accessibility bridge, which must say when the set changed) can
	// see a different set rather than only a different size.
	version: u64,
	_init:  bool,
}
```

### `selection_make`

```odin
selection_make :: proc(multi := false, allocator := context.allocator) -> Selection
```

### `selection_destroy`

```odin
selection_destroy :: proc(s: ^Selection)
```

### `selection_contains`

```odin
selection_contains :: proc(s: ^Selection, row: int) -> bool
```

### `selection_count`

```odin
selection_count :: proc(s: ^Selection) -> int
```

### `selection_clear`

```odin
selection_clear :: proc(s: ^Selection)
```

### `selection_set`

```odin
selection_set :: proc(s: ^Selection, row: int)
```

selection_set makes `row` the only selected row and the cursor/anchor.

### `selection_toggle`

```odin
selection_toggle :: proc(s: ^Selection, row: int)
```

selection_toggle flips one row (multi only; single-select just sets it).

### `selection_range`

```odin
selection_range :: proc(s: ^Selection, row: int)
```

selection_range selects anchor..row inclusive (replacing the selection), keeping the anchor; the cursor moves to `row`.

### `selection_all`

```odin
selection_all :: proc(s: ^Selection, count: int)
```

## selection_scope.odin

One selection across several messages.

Selecting *within* a reply is the markdown view's own job, and within a document the editor's. What neither can own is a selection that starts in one message and ends in another: somebody above them has to say which views a drag has entered, tell each one which part of the selection is theirs, and copy them in order. That somebody is a *scope* — a container an app marks with `selection_scope`, usually the column its conversation is built in.

The scope owns no text and paints nothing. It keeps two ends — a view and a byte offset in it — and writes each view's own selection from them, so the drawing, the copying and the offsets stay exactly where they were. That is also why a streaming reply cannot disturb it: the ends name a node and an offset into what that node holds, and bytes landing at the end of the last message move neither.

### `selection_scope`

```odin
selection_scope :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, on := true, loc := #caller_location)
```

selection_scope makes `n` the owner of one selection across the text views inside it (a conversation's column), so a drag that starts in one message and ends in another selects everything between, each view drawing its own part, and Ctrl+C copies the lot in order, separated by blank lines. It gathers the markdown views and text areas (read-only included) inside it, in build order. A selection within one view stays that view's own (double-click a word, triple-click a block) and becomes the scope's only when the drag leaves it. Escape lets the selection go; a press anywhere in the scope starts a new one. The setting stays until changed (`on = false` removes it).

### `selection_scope_text`

```odin
selection_scope_text :: proc(t: ^Tree, scope: Node, allocator := context.temp_allocator) -> string
```

selection_scope_text is what a scope has selected, as text: the messages it covers in order, separated by a blank line. "" when nothing is. The string is the frame's temp memory by default — clone what you keep.

## svg.odin

A small SVG path engine for icons: parses `d` strings (M L H V C S Q T A Z and relative forms), flattens curves and arcs to polylines, and rasterises strokes (round caps and joins) and fills (non-zero) into an 8-bit coverage bitmap by evaluating a signed distance per pixel — against the segments near that pixel only, bucketed on a coarse grid, so a 128 px icon costs about as little as a 24 px one. Results are cached in the glyph atlas.

### `Svg_Subpath`

```odin
Svg_Subpath :: struct {
	pts:    [dynamic][2]f32,
	closed: bool,
	filled: bool,
}
```

### `svg_flatten`

```odin
svg_flatten :: proc(d: string, scale: f32, allocator := context.temp_allocator) -> []Svg_Subpath
```

svg_flatten parses `d` scaled by `scale`. '|' starts a new shape at the origin and '#' marks a filled shape (see icons_data.odin). The subpaths are in temp memory by default.

### `svg_raster`

```odin
svg_raster :: proc(paths: []Svg_Subpath, w, h: int, stroke_w: f32, allocator := context.temp_allocator) -> []u8
```

svg_raster draws `paths` (already scaled to pixels) into a w×h coverage bitmap: strokes `stroke_w` wide with round caps and joins, plus non-zero fills for filled subpaths. Edges get a 1 px anti-aliasing ramp. The bitmap is in temp memory by default.

## svg_doc.odin

SVG documents as icon themes draw them: shapes and paths, groups, `use` and `symbol`, nested `svg`, transforms, fills (nonzero and evenodd) and strokes (joins, caps, dashes), linear and radial gradients, patterns, clip paths, masks, group opacity, embedded PNG and JPEG pictures, and the filter primitives icons use. Styles come from presentation attributes, `style`, and `<style>` sheets (type, class and id selectors, descendant and child combinators, `!important`), and simple text in Baldr's own faces (svg_text.odin). Pure Odin: core's XML parser, then svg_draw.odin's rasteriser and svg_paint.odin's paints and filters. Colours composite in sRGB, as browsers do, so icons look as their designers drew them.

### `Svg_Colors`

```odin
Svg_Colors :: struct {
	text, highlight, positive, neutral, negative: Color,
	symbolic:                                     bool,
}
```

Svg_Colors are the colours an icon takes from where it is drawn. `text` is what `currentColor` starts as and what KDE's `.ColorScheme-Text` sets; highlight, positive, neutral and negative go to `.ColorScheme-Highlight`, `-PositiveText`, `-NeutralText` and `-NegativeText`. `symbolic` applies GTK's rule for `*-symbolic` icons: every painted fill and stroke takes `text`, or the colour its class names (`success`, `warning`, `error`, or the KDE class).

### `Svg_Info`

```odin
Svg_Info :: struct {
	recolored, monochrome: bool,
}
```

Svg_Info is what svg_render saw while drawing with Svg_Colors: whether a given colour painted anything (`recolored`), and whether everything painted took the text colour (`monochrome`: the picture's alpha is then a mask to draw in any colour). Masks don't count; embedded pictures, gradients, patterns and filters count as the document's own colours.

### `svg_render`

```odin
svg_render :: proc(data: []u8, w, h: int, colors: ^Svg_Colors = nil, info: ^Svg_Info = nil, allocator := context.allocator) -> (img: Image, ok: bool)
```

svg_render draws an SVG document into a w × h Image (RGBA8, sRGB, straight alpha), fitting its viewBox by its preserveAspectRatio. With `colors`, the icon follows them (see Svg_Colors), and `info` says how. Fails when the document doesn't parse or a side is over SVG_MAX_SIDE. The Image's pixels are the caller's, from `allocator` (image_destroy). Safe on any thread: all its working memory is its own.

### `svg_size`

```odin
svg_size :: proc(data: []u8) -> (size: [2]f32, ok: bool)
```

svg_size is a document's own size in pixels: its width and height, or what its viewBox gives.

## text.odin

Text: measurement, shaping, and glyph rasterisation through runa.

One Text_Engine per app owns the embedded fonts (Inter Variable for UI text, Twemoji for colour emoji), runa's shape cache, the glyph atlas (alpha pages for mono glyphs, RGBA pages for colour), the rasterised-glyph cache, a small cache of laid-out lines, and the GPU page handles the renderer hands back (render.odin). Install it on a Tree with tree_use_text_engine; layout then measures through runa and render_prepare draws through the same laid-out lines.

Consistency rule: measurement and rendering both go through text_lines with the same (string, size, wrap width) and the same Paragraph_Opts, so a Text node is drawn with exactly the line breaks it was measured with. Sizes are logical px per em (CSS font-size); device scaling happens only in render_prepare.

Fonts are #load'ed: no file IO at runtime. runa's Cache and Atlas are single-threaded; the engine must only be touched from the UI thread.

### `Font_Slot`

```odin
Font_Slot :: enum u8 {
	UI,     // UI text, regular weight: Latin, Greek, Cyrillic. Defines line height and ascent.
	Bold,   // the same UI font at semibold (variable wght axis), for headline/title roles.
	Italic, // the UI face's italic companion (rich text)
	Bold_Italic,
	Medium, // the UI font at medium (variable wght axis): labels a design sets in 500
	Medium_Italic,
	Mono,   // JetBrains Mono: code (the code editor, `mono` text). Its own line height.
	Emoji,  // Twemoji Mozilla: COLRv0 colour emoji.
}
```

Font_Slot indexes the embedded font stack, in fallback order: the first font whose cmap covers a codepoint shapes it.

### `BALDR_ITALIC_FONT`

```odin
BALDR_ITALIC_FONT :: #config(BALDR_ITALIC_FONT, "../assets/fonts/InterVariable-Italic.ttf")
```

BALDR_ITALIC_FONT is the italic companion of the UI typeface.

### `BALDR_MONO_FONT`

```odin
BALDR_MONO_FONT :: #config(BALDR_MONO_FONT, "../assets/fonts/JetBrainsMono-Variable.ttf")
```

BALDR_MONO_FONT is the monospace typeface, embedded like the UI one.

### `BALDR_UI_FONT`

```odin
BALDR_UI_FONT :: #config(BALDR_UI_FONT, "../assets/fonts/InterVariable.ttf")
```

BALDR_UI_FONT is the UI typeface, a variable TTF with a wght axis, embedded at compile time. Override with -define:BALDR_UI_FONT=<path relative to baldr/text.odin> to try another face (see examples/02_theme_lab).

### `BOLD_WEIGHT`

```odin
BOLD_WEIGHT :: #config(BALDR_BOLD_WEIGHT, 600)
```

BOLD_WEIGHT is the wght value used for the Bold slots (Semibold).

### `MEDIUM_WEIGHT`

```odin
MEDIUM_WEIGHT :: #config(BALDR_MEDIUM_WEIGHT, 500)
```

MEDIUM_WEIGHT is the wght value used for the Medium slots.

### `ATLAS_PAGE_SIZE`

```odin
ATLAS_PAGE_SIZE :: 1024
```

ATLAS_PAGE_SIZE is the side of every atlas page in device px. Alpha pages are 1 MB, RGBA pages 4 MB; a page holds ~2000 body-size glyphs at 2×.

### `MAX_GLYPH_PX`

```odin
MAX_GLYPH_PX :: 512
```

MAX_GLYPH_PX is the largest device px-per-em that is rasterised into the atlas. Larger text lays out and measures normally but emits no glyphs.

### `MAX_TEXT_SIZE`

```odin
MAX_TEXT_SIZE :: 4096
```

MAX_TEXT_SIZE is the largest logical font size that is measured or laid out; beyond it text measures as zero and draws nothing.

### `LINES_CACHE_MAX`

```odin
LINES_CACHE_MAX :: 256
```

LINES_CACHE_MAX is the soft cap of cached laid-out paragraphs. Past it the least-recently-used half is evicted. A rebuild walking a long view in order looks like LRU's worst case, but widgets such as `markdown_view` keep their own per-block caches, so unchanged text never reaches `text_lines` and a bigger cap buys nothing. A slow first layout of a long view is answered by `list` with `estimated_height`, not by this cap.

### `Text_Engine`

struct

Text_Engine is everything text needs at runtime. See the file comment. Zero value is not usable: call text_engine_init and check its result.

### `text_engine_init`

```odin
text_engine_init :: proc(te: ^Text_Engine, allocator := context.allocator) -> bool
```

text_engine_init loads the embedded fonts and prepares the caches and atlas. Everything is allocated with `allocator`. Returns false (and leaves the engine safely destroyable) if a font fails to parse — which cannot happen with the shipped assets but is handled rather than asserted.

### `text_engine_destroy`

```odin
text_engine_destroy :: proc(te: ^Text_Engine)
```

text_engine_destroy frees everything the engine owns. GPU pages created through an Atlas_Sink are the renderer's to destroy. Safe on a partially-initialised engine.

### `tree_use_text_engine`

```odin
tree_use_text_engine :: proc(t: ^Tree, te: ^Text_Engine)
```

tree_use_text_engine makes `t` measure text through `te` (and lets render_prepare draw it). Passing nil, or an engine that failed to init, reverts to the headless mono measurer. Layout is invalidated either way, and the families the tree's theme names are looked for.

### `text_measure`

```odin
text_measure :: proc(t: ^Tree, s: string, size: f32, max_w: f32, weight: Font_Weight, mono: bool = false, family: Family_Id = 0, tabular: bool = false) -> [2]f32
```

text_measure is the Text_Measure installed by tree_use_text_engine: the width and total height, in logical px, of `s` at `size`, wrapping at `max_w` when it is > 0. Height is lines × text_line_height; "" is one line. Falls back to the mono measurer when the tree has no engine.

### `text_line_height`

```odin
text_line_height :: proc(te: ^Text_Engine, size: f32, mono := false, family: Family_Id = 0) -> f32
```

text_line_height is the distance between baselines at `size`, from the UI font's hhea metrics (ascent − descent + line gap, scaled by size / upem) — the mono font's for code (`mono`), and the family's own where one is drawing (`family`, or the one the theme names: a taller face gets taller rows rather than clipped ones). Used for measurement and for placing every line, whatever fallback fonts a line contains.

### `text_ascent`

```odin
text_ascent :: proc(te: ^Text_Engine, size: f32, mono := false, family: Family_Id = 0) -> f32
```

text_ascent is the baseline's distance below the top of a line at `size`, from the UI font's metrics (the mono font's for code, the family's own where one is drawing).

### `text_advance`

```odin
text_advance :: proc(te: ^Text_Engine, size: f32, family: Family_Id = 0) -> f32
```

text_advance is the width of one cell of the mono font at `size` (the advance of "0"); the code editor lays its gutter and tab stops on it.

## text_edit.odin

Text: the app-owned editable buffer behind text_input, plus the pure editing primitives the widget is built from — byte-range insert/delete and UAX #29 grapheme / word boundary movement through runa's iterators.

Everything here is independent of the tree: offsets are byte offsets into UTF-8, every proc clamps to the buffer, and invalid UTF-8 is tolerated (runa's iterators decode a bad byte as one U+FFFD of width 1), so nothing in this file can panic on user input.

Boundary procs walk from a nearby place a walk may safely restart (grapheme_restart, word_restart), so a keystroke costs the distance to it, not the whole string, and rules that depend on history (regional indicator pairs, emoji sequences) stay exact.

### `Text`

```odin
Text :: struct {
	buf:    [dynamic]u8,
	secret: bool,
}
```

Text is an editable UTF-8 buffer owned by the app. The zero value is usable: the first edit allocates with context.allocator (or the allocator given to text_make). The widget edits it in place through the ^Text passed to text_input, so the app's field is always current — there is nothing to copy back in `update`.  A `secret` Text (a passphrase; text_input sets it for `password`) never leaves its bytes behind: it grows by moving into a new block and zeroing the old one, and zeroes what it deletes, replaces or frees. text_scrub clears it once the secret has been used.

### `text_make`

```odin
text_make :: proc(initial := "", allocator := context.allocator) -> Text
```

text_make returns a Text holding `initial`, allocating with `allocator`.

### `text_destroy`

```odin
text_destroy :: proc(t: ^Text)
```

text_destroy frees the buffer. Safe on the zero value and after a previous destroy.

### `text_reserve`

```odin
text_reserve :: proc(t: ^Text, capacity: int)
```

text_reserve makes room for `capacity` bytes up front.

### `text_scrub`

```odin
text_scrub :: proc(t: ^Text)
```

text_scrub zeroes the buffer, room past its end included, and empties it: for a passphrase once it has been read.

### `text_set`

```odin
text_set :: proc(t: ^Text, s: string)
```

text_set replaces the whole contents with `s`. `s` may alias the buffer.

### `str`

```odin
str :: proc(t: Text) -> string
```

str is a view of the buffer's current contents. Valid until the next edit.

### `text_insert`

```odin
text_insert :: proc(t: ^Text, at: int, s: string) -> int
```

text_insert inserts `s` at byte offset `at` (clamped to the buffer) and returns the offset just after the inserted bytes. `s` must not alias the buffer.

### `text_delete`

```odin
text_delete :: proc(t: ^Text, lo, hi: int) -> int
```

text_delete removes the bytes in [lo, hi) (clamped and ordered) and returns the number removed.

### `grapheme_next`

```odin
grapheme_next :: proc(s: string, i: int) -> int
```

grapheme_next returns the first grapheme boundary after byte offset `i` (len(s) at the end).

### `grapheme_prev`

```odin
grapheme_prev :: proc(s: string, i: int) -> (prev: int)
```

grapheme_prev returns the last grapheme boundary before byte offset `i` (0 at the start).

### `grapheme_snap`

```odin
grapheme_snap :: proc(s: string, i: int) -> int
```

grapheme_snap returns the nearest grapheme boundary at or before `i`, so an offset the app left inside a cluster (or past the end) becomes a valid caret position.

### `grapheme_count`

```odin
grapheme_count :: proc(s: string) -> (n: int)
```

grapheme_count is the number of grapheme clusters in `s`.

### `grapheme_index`

```odin
grapheme_index :: proc(s: string, i: int) -> (n: int)
```

grapheme_index is the number of grapheme boundaries before byte offset `i` — the caret's position counted in clusters.

### `grapheme_offset`

```odin
grapheme_offset :: proc(s: string, idx: int) -> int
```

grapheme_offset is the byte offset of the `idx`-th grapheme boundary (0 → 0; past the end → len(s)). The inverse of grapheme_index.

### `word_next`

```odin
word_next :: proc(s: string, i: int) -> int
```

word_next returns the end of the word at or after byte offset `i`: whitespace after the caret is skipped, then the caret lands after the next run of non-space characters (the Ctrl+Right convention).

### `word_prev`

```odin
word_prev :: proc(s: string, i: int) -> int
```

word_prev returns the start of the word before byte offset `i`: the start of the last run of non-space characters that begins before the caret (the Ctrl+Left convention).

### `word_at`

```odin
word_at :: proc(s: string, i: int) -> (lo, hi: int)
```

word_at returns the word segment containing byte offset `i` (a run of whitespace counts as a segment, so double-clicking a gap selects the gap). At the very end it returns the last segment; an empty string gives (0, 0).

### `sanitize_input`

```odin
sanitize_input :: proc(s: string, allocator := context.temp_allocator) -> string
```

sanitize_input drops the characters a single-line field cannot hold — C0 and C1 control characters, including newlines and tabs — keeping every other byte (invalid UTF-8 included; the buffer and iterators tolerate it). Returns `s` itself when nothing had to go; otherwise a copy in `allocator` (temp memory by default).

## theme_icon.odin

Theme icons: an icon from the desktop's icon theme by name, found and decoded on a worker thread (SVG through svg_render, PNG, XPM) and drawn from the glyph atlas once ready; its space is kept meanwhile, and a size of it already loaded stands in, scaled, as does its picture from before the icon theme changed. An icon whose colours the desktop sets (symbolic, or styled with currentColor or KDE's ColorScheme classes) is drawn in the node's colour and follows it: one that takes only the text colour becomes a mask, tinted as it is drawn; one that also uses the accent or status colours is rendered again for each colour it is shown in. A full-colour icon keeps its own colours. The icon theme is the desktop's (the settings portal, else GTK's or KDE's settings), and a new theme, or a change to its files, shows without a restart.

### `THEME_ICON_MAX_PX`

```odin
THEME_ICON_MAX_PX :: 512
```

THEME_ICON_MAX_PX is the largest a theme icon is rasterised, in device px.

### `theme_icon`

```odin
theme_icon :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), name: string, size: f32 = 0, color: Color = {}, label: string = "",
	fallback: Icon = .None, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

theme_icon draws the icon theme's `name` (a .desktop file's Icon=, a tray item's IconName, "folder", "network-wireless-signal-good-symbolic", or an absolute path) at `size` logical px, ICON_SIZE by default. Its space is kept while it loads, and meanwhile the nearest size of it already loaded draws in its place, scaled (a dock growing its icons), as does its picture from before the icon theme changed; only its very first appearance draws nothing. A name nothing provides draws `fallback` instead (a Lucide icon; none: the theme's application-x-executable, else Lucide's app window). A symbolic or recolourable icon takes `color` (the text colour by default); a full-colour one fades with `color`'s alpha. Decorative unless `label` names it (then Image).

### `theme_icon_dir`

```odin
theme_icon_dir :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, dir: string)
```

theme_icon_dir gives a theme icon one more directory to look in first: a tray item's IconThemePath, holding loose icons or a theme's layout.

### `THEME_ICON_GENERIC`

```odin
THEME_ICON_GENERIC :: "application-x-executable"
```

THEME_ICON_GENERIC is a program's icon in every icon theme: what a theme icon nothing provides falls back to when it names no fallback of its own.

### `THEME_ICON_FALLBACK_MAX`

```odin
THEME_ICON_FALLBACK_MAX :: f32(24)
```

THEME_ICON_FALLBACK_MAX is the largest a fallback Lucide icon is drawn, centred in a theme icon's larger place.

## type_ahead.odin

Type-ahead in tables and icon views: with the view focused and no field focused, typing selects the first row whose label starts with what was typed. Letters within TYPE_AHEAD_MS build the prefix; the same letter again steps to the next row starting with it, wrapping. The match is selected and scrolled into view. The app gives the labels through `label_of`, since rows off screen have no nodes to read.

### `Label_Of`

```odin
Label_Of :: proc(user: rawptr, index: int) -> string
```

Label_Of gives row `index`'s label (a file's name) for type-ahead.

## typeset.odin

Text into a picture: an image editor's text layer, an image viewer's caption, a screenshot's annotation. The words become pixels in the document at the document's size (a 300 px heading), in any installed family at any weight, with the geometry to edit them in place.

typeset lays the text out once. runa shapes each paragraph, with the family asked for, the toolkit's fonts behind it and the system's for any script they lack (text_layout). The lines are filled here, greedily at UAX #14 break opportunities, so that tracking counts when a line is filled, a space where a line wraps hangs past the edge instead of pushing the words in, and a justified line knows its spaces. The shaper hands each paragraph back in the order it is seen; it is put back into the order it is read to be wrapped, and each line then into the order it is seen (UAX #9 L1 and L2), so right-to-left text wraps as it should.

What comes back answers an editor: the box, the ink, the lines, where the caret goes, the offset under a point, and a selection's rectangles. typeset_draw and typeset_mask fill the glyphs' outlines with exact coverage (path.odin's rasteriser); colour emoji keep their colours.

### `TYPESET_MAX_SIZE`

```odin
TYPESET_MAX_SIZE :: 20000
```

TYPESET_MAX_SIZE is the largest em typeset takes, in pixels.

### `Typeset_Style`

```odin
Typeset_Style :: struct {
	family:      string,     // an installed family (installed_fonts); "" is the theme's own typeface
	size:        f32,        // the em, in the picture's pixels
	weight:      int,        // 1–1000: 400 regular, 700 bold (0 is 400); the family's nearest face, or a variable one set to it
	italic:      bool,
	align:       Text_Align, // each line in the box; Start follows the paragraph's direction (the right in Hebrew)
	justify:     bool,       // spread each line a wrap ended to the box's width; a paragraph's last line keeps `align`
	tracking:    f32,        // space added between characters, in thousandths of an em (a type panel's unit); negative tightens
	line_height: f32,        // baseline to baseline, in pixels (0: the typeface's own)
	wrap_width:  f32,        // the box's width, lines wrapping at it (0: lines end only at line breaks)
	runs:        []Typeset_Run, // stretches set differently from the rest; empty is one run in the style's own face
	indent:      f32,        // the first line of each paragraph starts this far in, in pixels; negative hangs it out to the left
	drop_cap_lines: int,     // the paragraph's first letter is set to span this many lines, and they start past it (0: none)
	drop_cap_gap:   f32,     // pixels between the dropped letter and the text beside it (0: a tenth of its size)
	hanging_punctuation: bool, // let quotes, stops, commas and hyphens sit a little outside the margin (typeset_hang_out)
	breaking:    Typeset_Breaking, // how the lines are chosen: one at a time (the default) or all together
	hyphen_limit: int,       // most lines in a row that may end in a hyphen: 0 is two, negative is no limit
	objects:     []Typeset_Object, // boxes the app draws itself, each at a U+FFFC in the text
}
```

Typeset_Style says how text is set into a picture. The zero value (with a size) is the theme's typeface, regular, unwrapped.

### `Typeset_Object`

```odin
Typeset_Object :: struct {
	at:       int,    // the byte offset of its U+FFFC in the text
	size:     [2]f32, // the room it takes, in the picture's pixels
	baseline: f32,    // how far below the baseline its bottom edge sits
	label:    string, // what it is, for anything that reads the text aloud ("" is nothing to say)
}
```

Typeset_Object is a box in the text that the **app** draws: a picture in a line, a formula, an inline chart. It stands where the text has a U+FFFC (OBJECT REPLACEMENT CHARACTER), and typesetting only makes room for it — where it ended up comes back in `Typeset.objects`, and the app draws its own picture there.  text := "The scholar \ufffc wrote it."  // U+FFFC: a picture goes here at := strings.index(text, "\ufffc") style.objects = []baldr.Typeset_Object{{at = at, size = {14, 16}, baseline = 3, label = "Li Bai"}}  `baseline` is how much of the box hangs **below** the baseline: 0 sits it on the line, and a value near a third of its height sets it like a letter with a descender. The line grows to hold it, so a tall picture opens its own line rather than overlapping the ones around it.  A U+FFFC with no object of its own takes no room and draws nothing, rather than the empty box a font would otherwise give it.  The box takes the room it asks for, so one **wider than the column** makes its line wider than the measure rather than being shrunk: how a picture should be scaled to a page is the app's call, not the typesetter's, and it is the app that has the picture.

### `Typeset_Object_At`

```odin
Typeset_Object_At :: struct {
	index: int,    // into the `objects` the style was given
	at:    int,    // the byte offset, as given
	line:  int,    // the line it sits on
	rect:  Rect,   // where to draw it
	label: string, // what it is, as the style said; the Typeset's own copy
}
```

Typeset_Object_At is where a Typeset_Object was placed: `rect` is in the picture's pixels from its box's top-left, as everything else in a Typeset is, and `line` is the line it sits on (`Typeset.lines`). An object whose U+FFFC is not in the text does not appear here at all.

### `Typeset_Breaking`

```odin
Typeset_Breaking :: enum u8 {
	Greedy,
	Even,
}
```

Typeset_Breaking is how a paragraph's lines are chosen.  `.Greedy` fills one line at a time, taking words until the next will not fit. It is what a browser and every text editor do, and it is the default for a reason that is not typographic: a line's break never depends on what comes after it, so typing a word at the end of a paragraph cannot re-break the lines above the caret. Text that is being edited in place wants that.  `.Even` chooses every break in the paragraph together — Knuth and Plass's total fit, runa's `break_lines` — to even how loose or tight the lines are. It is what a typesetter does and what a page of a book wants: fewer gaping justified lines, fewer hyphens, a rag that does not jag. It also makes limits a greedy fill cannot express, because it can trade one line against another: `hyphen_limit`, and no hyphen on the last full line of a paragraph (which would leave a reader a fragment of a word to finish on). It costs about 0.02 ms a paragraph on top of the shaping (spikes/typeset_cost).  Two things fall back to `.Greedy` on their own: a paragraph holding a tab (a tab stop's width depends on where the line starts, which is what the breaking is still deciding), and one with no `wrap_width` to break to.

### `TYPESET_HYPHEN_LIMIT`

```odin
TYPESET_HYPHEN_LIMIT :: 2
```

TYPESET_HYPHEN_LIMIT is how many lines in a row may end in a hyphen when `Typeset_Style.hyphen_limit` is left at zero. Two is the usual house rule; a third in a row reads as a ladder down the margin.

### `Typeset_Run`

```odin
Typeset_Run :: struct {
	end:         int,    // the byte this run reaches, from the text's start; the last run ends at len(text)
	family:      string, // "" takes the style's
	size:        f32,    // the em in pixels; 0 takes the style's
	weight:      int,    // 0 takes the style's
	italic:      bool,
	small_caps:  bool,   // set the run's lowercase letters as the face's own small capitals (`smcp`), where it has them
	shift:       f32,    // the baseline moves up by this many pixels: a superscript is positive, a subscript negative
	tracking:    f32,    // thousandths of an em; 0 takes the style's
	ink:         Color,  // what to draw it in; a zero alpha takes the colour the draw call was given
	underline:   bool,
	strike:      bool,
}
```

Typeset_Run is a stretch of the text set differently from its neighbours: a word in italic, a footnote's raised number, a title in another face (a book's paragraph is runs, not one style). Runs are in order and together cover the text; `end` is the byte the run reaches, so the first covers `0 ..< runs[0].end` and the last must end at `len(text)`. `family`, `size`, `weight` and `tracking` take the style's value when left empty or zero; the flags (`italic`, `small_caps`, `underline`, `strike`) are the run's own, since a `false` cannot be told from unset. So once a text has runs, every run says whether it is italic.  **A run boundary breaks shaping**, as a style change does in any typesetter: a ligature or a kern never spans one. Set a word in italic and the "fi" where the italic starts is two glyphs, which is right — the two halves are in different faces.

### `typeset_small_caps_ready`

```odin
typeset_small_caps_ready :: proc "contextless" () -> bool
```

typeset_small_caps_ready says whether `Typeset_Run.small_caps` does anything: it does. A run asking for small capitals is shaped with the face's own `smcp`, as CSS `font-variant: small-caps` means it — lowercase letters become small capitals, capitals are left alone. A face without `smcp` is set in its ordinary letters, not faked ones (scaled-down capitals carry the wrong stem weight); ask `typeset_has_small_caps` about the face in hand. The embedded Inter and JetBrains Mono have no `smcp`.

### `typeset_has_small_caps`

```odin
typeset_has_small_caps :: proc(t: ^Tree, family: string, weight := 0, italic := false) -> bool
```

typeset_has_small_caps reports whether the face `family` resolves to at `weight` carries the font's own small capitals, so a `Typeset_Run` asking for them is set in real ones rather than left in its ordinary letters. An app offering small capitals as a setting can grey it out, or choose another face, instead of letting a reader wonder why nothing happened. False for a family this machine does not have (the theme's own typeface has no `smcp`), or a tree with no text engine. Call it on the tree's thread: the first use of a family and weight loads its face.

### `Typeset_Line`

```odin
Typeset_Line :: struct {
	start, end: int,
	rect:       Rect,
	baseline:   f32,
	hyphen:     bool, // it broke at a soft hyphen, so a hyphen is drawn at its end and counted in its width
}
```

Typeset_Line is one line of a Typeset: the bytes of the text on it (its line break, if it ends at one, belongs to no line), its box from the Typeset's top-left (as wide as its characters, a hanging space not counted; the box's width when justified), and its baseline's y.  **A line is as tall as what is on it.** Its height and baseline come from the runs that actually reach it, so a footnote's small number does not change the leading of the line carrying it, and a line holding a heading's run is tall where its neighbours are not. `style.line_height` overrides that for every line when it is set.

### `Typeset`

```odin
Typeset :: struct {
	text:      string,
	size:      [2]f32,
	ink:       Rect,
	lines:     []Typeset_Line,
	family:    string,
	weight:    int,
	glyphs:    []Typeset_Glyph,
	spans:     [][2]int, // each line's glyphs: first, count
	runs:      []Typeset_Run, // as given, always at least one (the style's own); Typeset_Glyph.run indexes it
	objects:   []Typeset_Object_At, // where each box the style gave was placed, in reading order
	drop_cap_run: int,     // the run holding the dropped first letter, last in `runs`; -1 when there is none
	allocator: runtime.Allocator,
	// serial tells one setting from another when their addresses cannot:
	// freeing Typesets and setting new ones can reuse the same memory, so a
	// widget comparing `^Typeset` alone would draw the old words. Stamped by
	// `typeset` from a counter on the Tree, so a copy carries the same
	// serial — a copy *is* that setting — while a fresh setting never
	// repeats one.
	serial:    u64,
}
```

Typeset is text set for a picture (typeset). Positions are in the picture's pixels from its box's top-left: `size` is the box (the wrap width, or the widest line, by every line's height), `ink` where the glyphs' pixels fall (an italic's overhang or a descender may reach past the box). `family` is the family it was set in, as the machine names it: "" is the theme's typeface, which is also what sets text in a family this machine does not have. `weight` is the weight it was set at. Everything is the allocator's it was made with (typeset_destroy); the glyphs point at fonts the tree's text engine holds, so a Typeset is drawn while the tree lives. The other fields are Baldr's.

### `typeset`

```odin
typeset :: proc(t: ^Tree, text: string, style: Typeset_Style, allocator := context.allocator) -> (ts: Typeset, ok: bool)
```

typeset sets `text` in `style` for drawing into a picture. Paragraphs end at line breaks ("\n", "\r\n", U+2028, U+2029). It is false, with nothing allocated, when the tree has no text engine (a headless tree without one) or the size is not between 0 and TYPESET_MAX_SIZE. The Typeset is the caller's (`allocator`): typeset_destroy. Call it on the tree's thread: the first use of a family and weight loads its face.

### `typeset_destroy`

```odin
typeset_destroy :: proc(ts: ^Typeset)
```

typeset_destroy frees a Typeset. Safe on the zero value and twice.

### `typeset_line_of`

```odin
typeset_line_of :: proc(ts: ^Typeset, offset: int) -> int
```

typeset_line_of is the line the caret at byte `offset` is on: where a wrap splits the text, the line after it.

### `typeset_caret`

```odin
typeset_caret :: proc(ts: ^Typeset, offset: int) -> Rect
```

typeset_caret is where the caret goes at byte `offset`: a rectangle of no width at its x, as tall as its line, from the box's top-left. An offset inside a character is placed as far through it as its graphemes say (a caret inside a ligature); one where a wrap splits the text is at the start of the next line.

### `typeset_hit`

```odin
typeset_hit :: proc(ts: ^Typeset, pos: [2]f32) -> int
```

typeset_hit is the byte offset of the caret place nearest `pos` (from the box's top-left): on the line under it, the first or last line above or below the text, the nearest grapheme boundary across. Where a wrap ends a line, the place before its last character is as far as that line goes; the one after it is the next line's first.

### `typeset_rects`

```odin
typeset_rects :: proc(ts: ^Typeset, lo, hi: int, allocator := context.allocator) -> []Rect
```

typeset_rects is the rectangles covering bytes `lo..hi` of the text, from the box's top-left: one per line, and more where a line mixes directions. A selection's highlight, or, given one character's bytes, where that character is. The slice is `allocator`'s.

### `typeset_draw`

```odin
typeset_draw :: proc(ts: ^Typeset, img: ^Image, at: [2]f32, color: Color)
```

typeset_draw sets the text into `img` with its box's top-left at `at` in the picture's pixels (fractions allowed), in `color`. The glyphs' outlines are filled with exact coverage and laid on as image_paint lays a colour, on the stored sRGB values. Colour emoji keep their own colours and fade with `color`'s alpha. The part drawn is touched (image_touch).

### `typeset_mask`

```odin
typeset_mask :: proc(ts: ^Typeset, m: ^Mask, at: [2]f32, op := Mask_Op.Add)
```

typeset_mask puts the text's coverage into `m` by `op`, its box's top-left at `at` in the mask's space: a text layer's mask, or a selection made from type. Colour glyphs count by their shape.

## uri.odin

text/uri-list, the form files take on the clipboard and in a drag: one `file://` URI per line, percent-encoded, CRLF-separated, `#` lines are comments.

### `uri_list_encode`

```odin
uri_list_encode :: proc(paths: []string, allocator := context.allocator) -> string
```

uri_list_encode writes local `paths` as a text/uri-list. The string is the caller's to free.

### `uri_list_decode`

```odin
uri_list_decode :: proc(text: string, allocator := context.allocator) -> []string
```

uri_list_decode returns the local paths of a text/uri-list. Comment lines and other schemes are skipped, and so is anything that is not plainly a file on this machine: a URI naming another host (whose path would otherwise be read as one here), a relative path, or one that decodes to a NUL. The slice and its strings are the caller's to free.

## url.odin

Opening a link: an app hands a URL to the desktop, which opens it in the person's own browser or mail app. The toolkit never picks the program and never runs a command line — the desktop's handler does both (the OpenURI portal, else xdg-open, on Linux; ShellExecuteW on Windows).

Only http, https and mailto are opened. Links often come from untrusted text, and `file:`, `data:`, `javascript:` or a program's own scheme is how such a link does something nobody asked for. An app that wants one of those opens it itself, having decided it is safe.

### `URL_MAX`

```odin
URL_MAX :: 8192
```

URL_MAX is the longest URL that is handed on. Browsers stop caring long before this; it is here so a runaway string cannot become an argument.

### `Url_Opened`

```odin
Url_Opened :: struct {
	url: string,
	ok:  bool,
}
```

Url_Opened is what became of `open_url`: the URL as it was asked for, and whether the desktop took it. It is false when nothing is registered for the link, the desktop refused it, the person dismissed a chooser, or the URL was not one Baldr opens — the app then shows the link to copy. The string lives only while the message is handled; clone what you keep.

### `open_url`

```odin
open_url :: proc($Msg: typeid, url: string, on_done: Handler(Msg, Url_Opened) = nil) -> Cmd(Msg)
```

open_url asks the desktop to open `url` with whatever the person uses for it: a browser for http and https, a mail app for mailto. Nothing else is opened (see url_openable), and a URL that is refused comes back at once as `on_done` with `ok = false` rather than silently doing nothing.  case Link: return baldr.open_url(Msg, e.value, proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Url_Opened)) -> Msg { return Link_Failed{e.value.url} if !e.value.ok else Nothing{} })

### `url_openable`

```odin
url_openable :: proc(url: string) -> bool
```

url_openable says whether `open_url` will hand this URL to the desktop: http, https or mailto, no control characters or spaces, a host after the scheme (an address for mailto), and no longer than URL_MAX. A mailto that names a file to attach is refused: some mail apps attach it without asking, so a link in a message could send the reader's own files away. A link view can ask first and show an unopenable link as plain text.

## widget_chart.odin

A chart: what a series of numbers looks like.

The app hands over its series and says what shape they take — a line, an area, bars, a scatter — and the widget does the rest: bounds, ticks that land on round numbers in the desktop's own notation, a grid that stays out of the way, a legend, the value under the pointer, and a keyboard path through every point for someone who cannot use one.

Three rules it keeps, because a chart that breaks them misleads:

```odin
   - **One vertical scale.** Two measures of different size are two charts,
     or one indexed to a common base — never two axes, which lets the
     drawing imply a relationship that the numbers do not have.
   - **Identity is never colour alone.** Two or more series always carry a
     legend, four or fewer are also labelled where they end, and a scatter's
     series take a marker shape of their own (chart_palette.odin explains
     what that is protecting).
   - **The text is text.** Values, ticks and labels wear the theme's text
     colours; the coloured thing is the mark beside them, which is what
     carries identity.
```

### `Chart_Kind`

```odin
Chart_Kind :: enum u8 {
	Line,    // a value that moves through time or another continuous run
	Area,    // …the same, with what is under it filled: a total, not a rate
	Bar,     // a magnitude per category, read against a zero baseline
	Scatter, // two measures against each other, one point per observation
}
```

Chart_Kind is the shape a series takes. A chart can mix them — bars with a line over them is the common one — by setting `kind` per series.

### `Chart_Point`

```odin
Chart_Point :: struct {
	x, y: f64,
}
```

Chart_Point is one observation. `x` is the position along the bottom — an index for categories, or any number for a continuous run — and `y` the value.

### `Chart_Series`

```odin
Chart_Series :: struct {
	name:   string,
	points: []Chart_Point,
	kind:   Maybe(Chart_Kind), // nil: the chart's own kind
	color:  Color,
}
```

Chart_Series is one line, area, set of bars or cloud of points. `color` left zero takes the series' slot in the palette, which is the usual thing to do: the slots are chosen to be told apart (chart_palette.odin).

### `Chart_Hit`

```odin
Chart_Hit :: struct {
	series: int,
	index:  int,
	point:  Chart_Point,
	name:   string, // the series' name, for the message that wants to say it
}
```

Chart_Hit is a point the reader picked, by pointer or by key.

### `chart`

```odin
chart :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), series: []Chart_Series, kind: Chart_Kind = .Line,
	label: string = "", describe: string = "", x_labels: []string = nil,
	y_min: Maybe(f64) = nil, y_max: Maybe(f64) = nil, legend := true, grid := true,
	height: f32 = 240, width: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0,
	on_select: Handler(Msg, Chart_Hit) = nil, x_min: Maybe(f64) = nil, x_max: Maybe(f64) = nil, x_grid: f64 = 0,
	tag: u64 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

chart draws `series`. `kind` is what they are unless a series says otherwise; `label` names the chart for a screen reader and `describe` adds a sentence of context. `y_min` / `y_max` pin the value axis where the data alone would mislead (a rate read against 0–100); left out, they come from the data, rounded outwards to the tick. `x_labels` names the categories along the bottom, one per point. `x_min` / `x_max` pin the bottom axis the same way, so a window of time keeps its place and a series that starts partway across is drawn from where it starts. `x_grid` draws a vertical line at every multiple of it along x; the lines scroll as the range moves, so a steady value still reads as live. The value under the pointer is shown; arrow keys walk the points and Up / Down change series, and every series is in the accessibility tree.  baldr.chart(ui, sales[:], kind = .Bar, label = "Sales by month", x_labels = months[:], on_select = proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Chart_Hit)) -> Msg { return Picked{e.value} })

## widget_chrome.odin

A window's own title bar, for a window the desktop is not decorating (`Surface_Desc.undecorated`). A shell wants one; so does any app that puts something where the title would be — a browser's tabs, an editor's file name, a player's transport.

What it must not be is a picture of a title bar. A real one moves the window when dragged, answers a double click as the desktop says, opens the desktop's own window menu on a right click, resizes from its edges, and has buttons a keyboard can reach and a screen reader can name. The move and the resize are the desktop's to run — the widget asks for them, carrying the serial of the press that asked, which is what stops a window seizing the pointer whenever it likes.

It follows the desktop's choices for a title bar (Titlebar_Prefs): the buttons on the left or the right, which of minimise and maximise show, what a double click does, and whether the wheel rolls the window up to its bar, as the desktop's own title bars do. Rolled up, the window is only its bar: laid out at its full size still, so nothing in it moves, with what is below the bar out of reach of the pointer, the keyboard and assistive technology until it comes back down.

### `Chrome_Button`

```odin
Chrome_Button :: enum u8 { Minimize, Maximize, Close }
```

Chrome_Button is one of the three a window offers.

### `Chrome_Buttons`

```odin
Chrome_Buttons :: bit_set[Chrome_Button; u8]
```

### `CHROME_ALL`

```odin
CHROME_ALL :: Chrome_Buttons{.Minimize, .Maximize, .Close}
```

CHROME_ALL is the usual set. A dialog often wants `{.Close}` alone.

### `Titlebar_Action`

```odin
Titlebar_Action :: enum u8 {
	Maximize, // maximise, or restore: the usual
	Minimize,
	Roll_Up,  // roll the window up to its bar, or back down
	Menu,     // the desktop's window menu
	None,
}
```

Titlebar_Action is what a double click on a title bar the window draws itself does.

### `Titlebar_Wheel`

```odin
Titlebar_Wheel :: enum u8 { None, Roll_Up }
```

Titlebar_Wheel is what the wheel does over such a title bar: nothing, or (Roll_Up) up rolls the window up to its bar and down brings it back.

### `Titlebar_Prefs`

```odin
Titlebar_Prefs :: struct {
	button_layout: string,
	double_click:  Titlebar_Action,
	wheel:         Titlebar_Wheel,
}
```

Titlebar_Prefs are the desktop's choices for a title bar a window draws itself (window_chrome). `button_layout` is GNOME's button-layout: the buttons before the colon go on the left and those after it on the right, in that order — "close,minimize,maximize:" as on a Mac, ":minimize,maximize,close" the usual (and what "" means). A button left out is not shown, except close, which always is; other names (appmenu, icon, spacer) are not the window's and are passed over. The zero value is the usual in every way: buttons on the right, a double click maximising, the wheel doing nothing.  A desktop that says (GNOME's settings, through the Settings portal) is followed by every window; an app that owns these settings passes them in `App.titlebar` and `set_titlebar_prefs` instead, as it does its accessibility preferences.

### `titlebar_action_of`

```odin
titlebar_action_of :: proc(name: string) -> (Titlebar_Action, bool)
```

titlebar_action_of reads a double-click action as desktops write it: GNOME's action-double-click-titlebar ("toggle-maximize", "minimize", "toggle-shade", "menu", "none"; "lower" and the one-way maximises are the nearest there is) and a compositor's own config words ("maximize", "roll_up", "nothing"). False for a word it doesn't know.

### `set_titlebar_prefs`

```odin
set_titlebar_prefs :: proc(t: ^Tree, p: Titlebar_Prefs)
```

set_titlebar_prefs gives this tree's title bars the choices `p` says, for a desktop that owns them and would rather say than be asked (the app reads its own settings): once set, the desktop's leave this tree alone, as with set_a11y_prefs. `App.titlebar` says it for every surface at start-up. Call it before the view builds its title bar, or it shows in the next build.

### `window_chrome`

```odin
window_chrome :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), title: string, ink: Maybe(Color) = nil, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

window_chrome opens a title bar across the top of an undecorated window — a row with the title in it, which stays open so the app can build its own controls into the same bar. Close it with `end`, and put the window's own buttons in with `window_buttons` before you do.  Dragging the bar moves the window, a double click maximises or restores it (or what the desktop says: minimises, rolls it up, opens the window menu, nothing), and a right click opens the desktop's own window menu; the wheel rolls it up and down where the desktop says so. The window's resize edges come with it: an undecorated window has to catch the pointer near its own borders, or it cannot be resized at all.  baldr.window_chrome(ui, "Notes"); { defer baldr.end(ui) baldr.window_buttons(ui, on_close = Msg(Quit{})) }

### `window_buttons`

```odin
window_buttons :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), buttons: Chrome_Buttons = CHROME_ALL, on_close: Maybe(Msg) = nil, ink: Maybe(Color) = nil, loc := #caller_location)
```

window_buttons puts the window's own buttons in the bar, where the desktop wants them: at its end, or before the title (Titlebar_Prefs), in the desktop's order. Call it after whatever the app builds into the bar, so its controls sit beside the title rather than past the close button. An app with nothing of its own calls the two back to back. `buttons` are the ones the window offers (a dialog: `{.Close}`); of those, the desktop's layout says which of minimise and maximise show. Close always does.  baldr.window_chrome(ui, "Notes"); { defer baldr.end(ui) tabs(ui, s)                                   // the app's own baldr.window_buttons(ui, on_close = Msg(Quit{}))   // …and the window's }

### `Window_Hook`

```odin
Window_Hook :: struct {
	user:    rawptr,
	surface: u32,
	act:     proc(user: rawptr, surface: u32, act: platform.Window_Act, edges: platform.Window_Edges, at: [2]f32) -> bool,
	// close asks for this window to go as if the desktop had asked, so one
	// place decides what closing means (an app's own on_close, a last window
	// ending the run).
	close:   proc(user: rawptr, surface: u32),
	// roll rolls the window up to `height` (its bar's bottom), or back down
	// to the size it had. False when it can't.
	roll:    proc(user: rawptr, surface: u32, up: bool, height: f32) -> bool,
}
```

Window_Hook is how a tree asks the desktop about its own frame: installed by the run loop, which owns the backend, and zero in a headless test — where every request simply fails, which is what a test should see.

## widget_emoji_picker.odin

emoji_picker: a grid of every emoji the bundled face can draw, with a search field, the nine Unicode groups, skin tones and the app's own recents.

It is one Tab stop, like `table` and `icon_view`: the grid takes the keyboard and the arrows move a cursor inside it, rather than putting two thousand Tab stops in the window. Typing goes to the search field.

### `emoji_picker`

```odin
emoji_picker :: proc(
	ui: ^Ui($Msg),
	on_pick: Handler(Msg, string),
	recents: []string = nil,
	skin: Skin_Tone = .None,
	on_skin: Handler(Msg, Skin_Tone) = nil,
	width: f32 = 0,
	height: f32 = 320,
	label := "Emoji",
	key: u64 = 0,
	loc := #caller_location,
) -> Node
```

emoji_picker builds the picker and sends `on_pick` the chosen emoji — the sequence itself, with the skin tone already applied, ready to insert.  `recents` is the app's own list, most recent first (the first EMOJI_RECENT_MAX are shown), and `skin` the chosen tone: both outlive the window, so the app stores them, hearing a tone change through `on_skin`.  The search field takes the keyboard when the picker appears. The whole grid is one Tab stop after it: arrows move the cursor, Home and End go to the ends of a row, Page Up and Down move a screen, and Enter or Space picks. Accessibility: the grid is a List named by `label` whose active item is the cell under the cursor, and every cell is named by its CLDR short name ("grinning face"), which is also what search matches.

### `Emoji_Cell_Ref`

```odin
Emoji_Cell_Ref :: struct { picker: Node, index: int }
```

Emoji_Cell_Ref, Emoji_Jump_Ref and Emoji_Tone_Ref say what a node inside a picker means, so a click can be routed without reading its tag.

### `Emoji_Jump_Ref`

```odin
Emoji_Jump_Ref :: struct { picker: Node, group: int }
```

### `Emoji_Tone_Ref`

```odin
Emoji_Tone_Ref :: struct($Msg: typeid) { h: Handler(Msg, Skin_Tone), tone: Skin_Tone }
```

## widget_graph.odin

A node graph: boxes with ports, wires between them, and a canvas that pans and zooms under them — a shader graph, a signal chain, a build pipeline.

The app owns the graph. It hands over its nodes and wires each build and hears what the reader did — a node moved, a wire made or cut, a selection changed — as messages, the way a text field hands back an edit. The widget owns only how the canvas is being looked at (pan and zoom), because that is a property of the looking, not of the graph.

The nodes are accessibility objects with names, their connections are in their descriptions, and every gesture — select, move, connect, cut — has keys behind it.

### `Graph_Port`

```odin
Graph_Port :: struct {
	id:   u64,
	name: string,
}
```

Graph_Port is a plug on a node: `id` is the app's own, `name` is what a reader is told.

### `Graph_Node`

```odin
Graph_Node :: struct {
	id:       u64,
	title:    string,
	pos:      [2]f32,
	inputs:   []Graph_Port,
	outputs:  []Graph_Port,
	color:    Color, // the header's accent; zero takes the theme's primary
	selected: bool,
	width:    f32, // 0: GRAPH_NODE_W
}
```

Graph_Node is one box. `pos` is its top-left in graph coordinates — the app's own space, which the canvas pans and zooms over — and the widget reports a drag as a new `pos` rather than moving it behind the app's back.  `inputs` and `outputs` are borrowed for the build that passes them and copied here, as a text's string is: they must still be alive when the view runs, so keep them in the app's state rather than building a slice literal in a procedure that has already returned.

### `Graph_Wire`

```odin
Graph_Wire :: struct {
	from_node, from_port: u64,
	to_node, to_port:     u64,
}
```

Graph_Wire joins one node's output to another's input.

### `Graph_Event`

```odin
Graph_Event :: union {
	Graph_Moved,     // a node was dragged, or nudged with the keyboard
	Graph_Connected, // a wire was made
	Graph_Cut,       // …or cut
	Graph_Selected,  // the selection changed
	Graph_Activated, // a node was opened (double click, or Enter)
	Graph_Deleted,   // the reader asked for a node to go
}
```

Graph_Event is what the reader did. One handler hears them all, because a graph is a node that reports several kinds of thing (`on_event`).

### `Graph_Connected`

```odin
Graph_Connected :: struct { from_node, from_port, to_node, to_port: u64 }
```

### `Graph_Activated`

```odin
Graph_Activated :: struct { node: u64 }
```

### `graph`

```odin
graph :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), nodes: []Graph_Node, wires: []Graph_Wire,
	on_event: Handler(Msg, Graph_Event) = nil, label: string = "", describe: string = "",
	height: f32 = 360, width: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0, grid := true,
	tag: u64 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

graph draws `nodes` and `wires` on a canvas that pans and zooms.  The reader can drag a node, drag from one port to another to wire them, click a wire to cut it, drag the background to pan, and Ctrl + wheel to zoom — and can do all of it from the keyboard: Tab and the arrows move between nodes, Shift + arrows move the node itself, Enter opens it, Delete asks for it to go, and Ctrl + the arrows pan the canvas.  baldr.graph(ui, s.nodes[:], s.wires[:], on_event = proc(e: baldr.Ev(baldr.Graph_Event)) -> Msg { return Graph_Changed{e.value} })

## widget_inline_edit.odin

Editing in place: a label becomes a text field where it stands. A table cell (`cell_edit`), an icon view tile and a tree item (`on_submit` on their builders) all do it the same way — the field takes focus with the name selected up to its extension, Enter or leaving the field commits, Escape reverts, Tab commits and asks for the next cell. The views ask the app to start one through `on_edit` (F2, or a slow second click on the selected item — a double-click activates instead), so the app decides which cells may change and what a commit does.

### `Cell_Ref`

```odin
Cell_Ref :: struct {
	row, col: int,
}
```

Cell_Ref names a table cell: the row's data index and the column's index.

### `INLINE_EDIT_LINES`

```odin
INLINE_EDIT_LINES :: 8
```

INLINE_EDIT_LINES is as tall as an editor over a label grows before it scrolls inside itself; a name needing more than this is pathological.

### `cell_edit`

```odin
cell_edit :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), value: string, on_submit: Handler(Msg, string), on_cancel: Maybe(Msg) = nil,
	select_stem := true, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

cell_edit is a table cell holding an editor for `value` instead of its text: build it in place of `cell_text` for the cell the app is editing (after its `on_edit` asked). `on_submit` gets the new text (Ev.tag is the row); `on_cancel` is sent on Escape. `select_stem` selects up to the extension, as a rename wants; false selects it all.  if s.editing == (Cell_Ref{i, 0}) { baldr.cell_edit(ui, name, proc(e: baldr.Ev(string)) -> Msg { return Renamed{int(e.tag), e.value} }, on_cancel = Msg(Edit_Done{})) } else { baldr.cell_text(ui, name, icon = .File) }

## widget_lines.odin

Lines drawn over a node: polylines, dashed, with a dash offset that can move. An image editor's overlays are these — marching ants round a selection, a transform box rotated with its layer, a crop frame and its thirds, guides, the pixel grid at high zoom, a lasso as it is drawn: thin lines over a `canvas` that move every frame and cannot be boxes. They are drawn as the renderer's own capsules rather than rasterised into an image: a node in a `stack` over the canvas, placed with `offset`, whose points are in its own logical coordinates. A build costs a copy of the points; a frame costs walking the dashes, which is arithmetic, not rasterising.

### `Polyline`

```odin
Polyline :: struct {
	points: [][2]f32,
	closed: bool,
}
```

Polyline is one run of points in a `lines` node: an open stroke, or a loop when `closed`. A selection with holes is several — one loop round the outside and one round each hole — and they share the node's thickness, colour and dash phase, so the ants march together.

### `LINES_MAX_DASHES`

```odin
LINES_MAX_DASHES :: 8
```

LINES_MAX_DASHES is how many lengths a dash pattern may have. Past this it is not a dash pattern, it is a path.

### `lines`

```odin
lines :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), runs: []Polyline, thickness: f32 = 1, color: Color = {},
	dashes: []f32 = nil, dash_offset: f32 = 0, outline: Color = {}, crisp := true,
	width: f32 = 0, height: f32 = 0, offset: [2]f32 = {}, label: string = "",
	key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

lines draws `runs` (one or more polylines) over whatever is behind it, in the node's own logical coordinates: put it in a `stack` over a `canvas` with an `offset` to the canvas's corner. `thickness` is logical px, so a 1 px line stays 1 px at any zoom. `dashes` alternates on and off lengths along the path (`{4, 4}` even dashes, `{1, 3}` dots; an odd count repeats with on and off swapped, as SVG's does), and a rising `dash_offset` slides the pattern forward, so the ants march. `outline` fills the gaps between dashes, or without dashes is a halo two pixels wider under the line, which keeps it readable over any picture. `crisp` (the default) puts horizontal and vertical lines on whole device pixels, so a guide is one hard line rather than two half-lit ones; angled lines are never moved. It is decoration: no pointer or keyboard (an editor hit-tests its own geometry), and no place in the accessibility tree unless given a `label`. With no `width` and `height` it is as big as its points, from its own origin.  ants := f32(baldr.now_ms(ui) / 40)                          // 25 px a second baldr.lines(ui, {{outline, true}}, color = baldr.BLACK, outline = baldr.WHITE, dashes = {4, 4}, dash_offset = ants, width = view.w, height = view.h)

## widget_list_row.odin

A full-width row for a list or a popover's page: a control centre's power menu, its sound outputs, networks and devices.

### `Row_End`

```odin
Row_End :: enum u8 { None, Check, Switch, Chevron }
```

Row_End is what a list_row shows at its end, after its value: a check while it is on (the device in use), a switch showing it (a tunnel), or a chevron (a page to open).

### `Row_Variant`

```odin
Row_Variant :: enum u8 { Plain, Tile, Accent, Avatar }
```

Row_Variant is how a list_row shows its icon: plain before its label, or in a tile (a launcher's results), an app's own icon filling it, an icon or glyph on a quiet tile, or on one tinted with the accent (a calculator), or in a circle (`Avatar`: a chat app's conversation list). `Avatar` keeps a mixed list lined up: a picture at the tile's 36 px beside an initial at an icon's 16 would start the labels at different places, so the leading slot is one circle whatever it holds.

### `Row_Size`

```odin
Row_Size :: enum u8 { Regular, Large }
```

Row_Size is how big a list_row's label is: Regular, or Large (a calculator's answer).

### `list_row`

```odin
list_row :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), label: string, on_click: Msg, icon: Icon = .None, theme_icon := "", glyph := "",
	image: ^Image = nil, image_round := true, detail := "", detail_mark: [2]int = {},
	unread: int = 0, value := "",
	end: Row_End = .None, on := false, selected := false, hint := "", variant: Row_Variant = .Plain, size: Row_Size = .Regular,
	disabled := false, on_context: Maybe(Msg) = nil, tag: u64 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

list_row is one full-width row: at its start `icon`, or `theme_icon` (an icon theme's, `icon` standing in when the theme has none), or `glyph` (an emoji or a letter), or `image` (a decoded picture, which takes precedence: cropped to fill its variant's leading slot, round unless `image_round = false`; pass the initial as `glyph` too for a contact with no picture); then the label with a quieter `detail` under it (`detail_mark`: the byte range a search matched, drawn in full colour and heavier); at its end `value` ("Connected", "80 %"), an `unread` pill and what `end` asks for, `on` saying whether a check or switch shows on. `variant` puts the icon in a tile and `size = .Large` makes the label big. `selected` marks it the list's current row, the accent behind it and a bar at its start, without moving focus, so a search field walking its results keeps it (and names the row with set_active_item), and shows `hint` at its end ("↵ open": its first word in a keycap). The whole row is one control: a click, Enter or Space queues `on_click`, a right-click or the Menu key `on_context`, and what sits at its end is part of it, not a control of its own; Up and Down walk rows inside a popover. Role Button, or Radio (.Check) or Switch (.Switch) with Checked while on, Selected while selected; named by its label and described by its detail, value and unread count.

## widget_markdown.odin

markdown_editor is the live-preview markdown editor: the Document holds markdown source, one line per block, and every block is shown rendered — headings, lists, quotes, code, marks applied — except the ones the selection touches, which show their source with the marks dimmed so they can be edited in place. Editing is plain text editing of the source, with the text area's keys; on top: Enter continues a list, quote or task prefix (an empty item ends it), Tab / Shift+Tab nest list items, Ctrl+B / I / E wrap or unwrap the selection in **, * and `, Ctrl+K makes a link, a click on a rendered task's box ticks it, Ctrl+click on a link sends `on_link`. Save the source as it is; it is the markdown. Accessibility: Text_Input named `label` with the source as its value.

```odin
baldr.markdown_editor(ui, &s.notes, on_change = …, label = "Notes", flex = 1)
```

### `markdown_editor`

```odin
markdown_editor :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), doc: ^Document, on_change: Handler(Msg, int) = nil, label: string = "Markdown",
	placeholder: string = "", width: f32 = 0, height: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0, on_link: Handler(Msg, string) = nil,
	on_context: Handler(Msg, Editor_Context) = nil, disabled := false, focus := false, tag: u64 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

## widget_markdown_view.odin

markdown_view: a reply, rendered. It shows a Markdown_Doc and nothing else — no caret, no undo, no source revealed under a selection — so an answer can be read while it is still arriving. The app owns the document and grows it with `md_append`; the view lays out only the blocks that changed, so a reply streaming at thirty updates a second does not re-lay what the person is reading further up.

It draws through `rich_draw_lines`, the same path the rich editor paints with, so the same markdown looks the same read or edited.

### `MD_VIEW_DEFAULT_WIDTH`

```odin
MD_VIEW_DEFAULT_WIDTH :: f32(560)
```

MD_VIEW_DEFAULT_WIDTH is what a view takes when nothing bounds it.

### `markdown_view`

```odin
markdown_view :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), md: ^Markdown_Doc, label: string = "", width: f32 = 0,
	max_width: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0, tag: u64 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

markdown_view shows `md` as rendered markdown: headings, paragraphs, lists, quotes, code and rules, with **bold**, *italic*, `code`, ~~strike~~ and links styled inline. It is read-only by design — a reply is not a document being edited.  The app owns `md` and keeps it alive while the view is built; growing it with `md_append` is what a streaming reply does, and only the blocks that changed are laid out again. The node sizes itself to its content, so it sits in a list of messages.  `label` names it for a screen reader (else "Message"). Links, pictures and the reading width are set with `md_view_options` and `md_view_image`.

### `md_view_selection`

```odin
md_view_selection :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node) -> (lo, hi: int)
```

md_view_selection is the source byte range the person has selected in the view built as `n`, low first. Both zero means nothing is selected. The offsets index the document's own text (`md_text`), so `md_markdown` turns them into the markdown behind the selection.

### `md_view_selected_text`

```odin
md_view_selected_text :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, allocator := context.temp_allocator) -> string
```

md_view_selected_text is the selection as clean text, ready for the clipboard: the words as they are drawn, a list item's marker written out ("- ", "1. ", "[x] ") and one newline between blocks. No markup, no invisible characters. The string is the caller's to free (temp by default). Empty when nothing is selected.

### `md_view_pointer_button`

```odin
md_view_pointer_button :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), pressed: bool, pos: [2]f32) -> bool
```

md_view_pointer_button places or extends a selection. It is called before the general press handling, as a text area's is.

### `md_view_pointer_move`

```odin
md_view_pointer_move :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), pos: [2]f32) -> bool
```

md_view_pointer_move extends a selection while the button is held.

### `md_view_key`

```odin
md_view_key :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), key: Key, mods: Mods) -> bool
```

md_view_key gives a focused reply the keys a reader needs: Ctrl+A to select it, Ctrl+C to copy what is selected, Escape to let it go. Nothing else is taken, so the window's own shortcuts still work.

### `md_view_link_at`

```odin
md_view_link_at :: proc(t: ^Tree, n: Node, pos: [2]f32) -> string
```

md_view_link_at is the link target under `pos` (absolute), "" when there is none. It is a view into the document's runs — clone what you keep.

### `md_view_options`

```odin
md_view_options :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, on_link: Handler(Msg, string) = nil,
	on_image: Handler(Msg, string) = nil, language: proc(name: string) -> ^Language = nil,
	prose_width: f32 = 0)
```

md_view_options sets what a reply does beyond being read: `on_link` hears a link being pressed, with its target, and the app decides whether to open it (`open_url` only takes http, https and mailto). Each setting stays until changed.  The target `on_link` and `on_image` carry is the *view's* memory, and the view lets it go the next time that block is laid out — which, while a reply streams, is straight away. Open it or clone it in the same update; do not put it in the app's state as it stands.  `prose_width` caps how wide *prose* is and centres it in the view; tables, code blocks and pictures share its left edge but may run on to the view's right edge. 0 (the default) leaves everything full width. For a wide window: the table gets room, the paragraphs stay readable (45-75 characters; WCAG 1.4.8 asks for under 80). Inter at 14.5 px averages about 7 px a character, so 680 is about 95; measure your own body face.  baldr.md_view_options(ui, reply, prose_width = 680) // in a 1,200 px view

### `md_view_image`

```odin
md_view_image :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, src: string, img: ^Image)
```

md_view_image hands the view a picture it is to draw for `src` — the target of a `![alt](src)` in the document. The view never fetches anything: what it is not given, it shows as its alt text. Call it while building, every build, for each picture the app has; `img` stays the app's.  The view holds the pointer until the app hands over another picture for that `src`, the view goes, or `image_forget(ui, img)` says it is about to be freed — a build that skips it (an unchanged `component`) must not make pictures disappear. So call `image_forget` before freeing one. The pixels come from the app's memory; the toolkit never puts them on disk.

## widget_menu_chip.odin

menu_chip: a pill that shows a choice and opens a menu of the others.

### `menu_chip`

```odin
menu_chip :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), label: string, open: bool, on_click: Msg, disabled := false,
	tag: u64 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

menu_chip is a compact pill for choosing in place among a few things: it shows the current one (a sound output's "Speakers", a screen's name) and a chevron, and a click, Enter or Space sends `on_click`, which the app answers by opening a `menu` anchored to the node it returns; `open` is that menu's state. It sits in the theme's fill_strong, so it reads on a tile or on glass; a label too long for its room ends in "…" and shows whole on hover. Accessibility: a Button named `label`, with a popup, Expanded while its menu is open.  output := baldr.menu_chip(ui, s.output_name, s.outputs_open, Msg.Toggle_Outputs) if baldr.menu(ui, output, s.outputs_open, Msg.Close_Outputs) { … }

### `MENU_CHIP_PADDING`

```odin
MENU_CHIP_PADDING :: [4]f32{4, 6, 4, 10}
```

The design's chip: padding top, right, bottom, left; its label's size; its chevron's.

## widget_pages.odin

Pages: one page of a set shown in the same place, sliding between them.

### `pages`

```odin
pages :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), page: u64, depth := 0, label := "", key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location)
```

pages shows one page of a set in the same place: a popover's grid of tiles, then a tile's page, then back again. Build only the current page inside it and close it with end; `page` names that page (one value per page) and `depth` how deep it is (the grid 0, a tile's page 1, a page opened from that 2). When `page` changes, the new page slides in from the end while the old one slides out to the start, whole and edge to edge (the other way round when `depth` goes down), and the container eases its height to the new page's over motion.base, keeping its width; under reduce motion the change is at once. A page that leaves keeps its nodes, so its scroll, fields and state are there when it comes back. Going deeper moves focus to the new page's first control; coming back returns it to the control that opened the page (both only when focus was in the pages, or nowhere). A Group named by `label`.

## widget_search_field.odin

search_field: the box at the top of a sidebar — a magnifier, a placeholder, and a clear cross once there is something to clear.

It is a `text_input` inside its own frame, so selection, input methods, the clipboard, the context menu and the caret are the one machinery every field uses. What is its own is the behaviour: Escape clears it (and only goes on to close what is around it once it is empty), the cross appears with the text and does not take the keyboard away from the field, and it reads as a Search_Box.

### `search_field`

```odin
search_field :: proc(
	ui: ^Ui($Msg),
	value: ^Text,
	placeholder := "Search",
	on_change: Handler(Msg, string) = nil,
	on_submit: Handler(Msg, string) = nil,
	on_cancel: Maybe(Msg) = nil,
	label := "",
```
