```odin
	width: f32 = 0,
	flex: f32 = 0,
	disabled := false,
	focus := false,
	tag: u64 = 0,
	key: u64 = 0,
	loc := #caller_location,
) -> Node
```

search_field is a search box over the app's own `Text` buffer.  `on_change` fires on every keystroke (debounce it in the app if the search is expensive: the toolkit cannot know what a query costs) and `on_submit` on Enter, for a search that should run at once. `on_cancel` is sent when Escape is pressed on a field that is already empty, so a sidebar can close its search bar; Escape on a field with text clears it and sends `on_change`. The cross clears the field, sends `on_change` and leaves the keyboard in the field.  Accessibility: role Search_Box named by `label` (else the placeholder); the magnifier is decoration and the cross is a Button named "Clear search", the Tab stop after the field.

### `Search_Clear_Ref`

```odin
Search_Clear_Ref :: struct($Msg: typeid) {
	field:     Node,
	on_change: Handler(Msg, string),
}
```

Search_Clear_Ref is what a cross knows: the field it empties, and the handler to tell afterwards.

### `search_text`

```odin
search_text :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node) -> string
```

search_text is the field's contents, for an app that would rather read the widget than keep its own copy. It aliases the `Text` the app passed in, so it lives as long as that does.

## widget_sparkline.odin

A sparkline: a small graph of a value over time, beside the number it shows — nothing but its line (a system monitor's sidebar tile at 64 × 28).

It is not a small chart: it is read at a glance, next to a number that says the value exactly, so it has no axes, legend or keyboard path and takes no focus. What it keeps is what makes a line at this size honest: a scale that can be pinned (a CPU is read against 0–100 %, not its own busiest moment), a history that grows in from the right the way time runs, and a stroke that stays inside its box at its peaks.

### `sparkline`

```odin
sparkline :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), values: []f32, width: f32 = 64, height: f32 = 28, color: Color = {}, fill := false,
	y_min: Maybe(f32) = nil, y_max: Maybe(f32) = nil, span: int = 0, label: string = "", key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

sparkline draws `values` (oldest first) as a line `width` × `height`, for a live value beside its number (a list row's `value`, a sidebar's tile). `span` is how many values the width holds: a history still filling up grows in from the right and older values leave at the left (0: as many as there are, edge to edge). `y_min` / `y_max` pin the scale (0 and 100 for a percentage); left out, it runs from 0, or the lowest value if that is below 0, to the highest. `color` is the line's ({}: the accent) and `fill` shades what is under it. It is decorative, as the number beside it is what is read; with a `label` it is an Image named that, described by its range and latest value.  baldr.sparkline(ui, s.cpu_history[:], color = cpu_blue, fill = true, y_min = 0, y_max = 100, span = 180)

## widget_styled_text.odin

styled_text — a paragraph of styled runs the **app** built.

For text that is not CommonMark (`markdown_view`'s input): a chat's own flavour — `*bold*`, `_italic_`, `~strike~`, `` `code` ``, auto-linked URLs, where CommonMark reads `*x*` as italic and has no strike — parsed and cached by the app. It takes text plus `[]Run`, laid out as one wrapped paragraph, selectable, copyable as plain text across the runs, with a click on a link run reaching `update`.

The whole paragraph is laid out once through the same `rich_layout` the editors and `markdown_view` use, and painted through the same `rich_draw_lines`, so it wraps inside runs (a row of `text` nodes would wrap only between them) and a link, inline code or strike looks the same as in an editor.

A run is `Run` from document.odin: the byte it ends at, its `Attrs` (Bold, Italic, Underline, Strike, Code, Link, Highlight) and, for a link, its `href`. There is no per-run colour: that would widen a core type for one widget.

### `styled_text`

```odin
styled_text :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), text: string, runs: []Run = nil,
	on_link: Handler(Msg, string) = nil, role: Text_Role = .Body, size: f32 = 0,
	ink: Color = {}, on: Color = {},
	width: f32 = 0, max_width: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0, label: string = "",
	key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

styled_text lays `text` out as one wrapped paragraph in `runs`' styles. `runs` cover the bytes in order — each up to its `end` — and an empty `runs` is plain text. Both are copied, so the app may free or rebuild them; nothing is kept beyond the call.  `on_link` is sent the run's `href` when a link run is clicked. The app decides what that means: `open_url` for an http one, something of its own for a scheme only it knows.  The text is selectable with the pointer and copies as **plain text** across runs — the styles are how it is drawn, not what it is.  runs := []baldr.Run{{end = 5, attrs = {.Bold}}, {end = 11}} baldr.styled_text(ui, "Hello world", runs)

### `Styled_Part`

```odin
Styled_Part :: enum u8 { Body, Muted, Link, Code, Selection }
```

Styled_Part names the colours a styled_text draws with, for `styled_text_options` to override one without naming the others.

### `Styled_Palette`

```odin
Styled_Palette :: struct {
	body, muted, link, code, selection: Color,
}
```

Styled_Palette is those colours. A zero member is the one derived from the ink.

### `styled_text_options`

```odin
styled_text_options :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node,
	body: Maybe(Color) = nil, muted: Maybe(Color) = nil, link: Maybe(Color) = nil,
	code: Maybe(Color) = nil, selection: Maybe(Color) = nil)
```

styled_text_options overrides what a styled_text draws one part in, leaving the rest derived from its `ink`. The settings stay until changed, as a modifier's do.  n := baldr.styled_text(ui, body, runs, ink = th.color.on_primary) baldr.styled_text_options(ui, n, link = th.color.on_primary_container)

## widget_terminal.odin

A terminal's screen: rows × columns of cells the app fills from its terminal state (libvterm, a scrollback it keeps), drawn the way a terminal draws them, and every key and typed character given back to it while it has the keyboard.

It is not a text area in a monospace font: the program has already laid the text out, cell by cell, and every glyph sits on its cell and nowhere else. The cell's width is a whole number of device pixels, each glyph is placed at its cell's origin rather than advanced by the font, backgrounds are runs of one colour with edges on device pixels (no seams), and box drawing, block elements, braille and powerline separators fill the cell exactly so lines join and graphs are solid. What the family lacks comes from the toolkit's fonts and the system's, and an emoji over two cells is fitted to them.

Nor is it rebuilt as a whole: the app says which rows it changed (term_touch); each row's drawing is kept in device pixels, only a changed row is made again, and only changed rows are damage for the compositor.

The keyboard: while the terminal has focus every key goes to the program — Tab, Shift+Tab, Escape, F10, Alt and Super with the arrows — after the app's own shortcut() calls have had their turn (so a terminal keeps Ctrl+Shift+C for itself). Ctrl+Tab and Ctrl+Shift+Tab, when the app has not bound them, move focus out, so the keyboard is never trapped. An input method composes at the cursor (Ime_Purpose.Terminal), and what it commits arrives as text.

Accessibility: the node is a Terminal; a reader gets the screen as text, one line per row, with the caret at the cursor, and hears what changes on it as the text it inserts. The cursor is the focus indicator, as in every terminal: solid while it has the keyboard, hollow when not.

### `TERM_CELL_RUNES`

```odin
TERM_CELL_RUNES :: 6
```

TERM_CELL_RUNES is how many code points one cell holds: a character and the marks combined with it (libvterm's VTERM_MAX_CHARS_PER_CELL).

### `Term_Style`

```odin
Term_Style :: enum u8 { Bold, Italic, Dim, Strike }
```

Term_Style is how a cell's text is set, beyond its colours.

### `Term_Styles`

```odin
Term_Styles :: bit_set[Term_Style; u8]
```

### `Term_Underline`

```odin
Term_Underline :: enum u8 { None, Single, Double, Curly, Dotted, Dashed }
```

Term_Underline is how a cell's text is underlined.

### `Term_Cell`

```odin
Term_Cell :: struct {
	text:            [TERM_CELL_RUNES]rune,
	fg, bg:          Color,
	underline_color: Color,
	style:           Term_Styles,
	underline:       Term_Underline,
	width:           u8,
}
```

Term_Cell is one cell of the screen. `text` is one grapheme cluster, its code points in order and the rest zero; all zero is an empty cell, and the right half of a wide character. `width` is 2 on a wide character's left half (the next cell is its right half), else 1. `bg` with alpha 0 is none: the screen's background shows. `underline_color` with alpha 0 is the text's own colour.

### `Term_Cursor_Shape`

```odin
Term_Cursor_Shape :: enum u8 { Block, Bar, Underline }
```

Term_Cursor_Shape is how the cursor is drawn at its cell.

### `Term_Cursor`

```odin
Term_Cursor :: struct {
	col, row: int,
	shape:    Term_Cursor_Shape,
	hidden:   bool,
	blink:    bool,
	color:    Color,
}
```

Term_Cursor is where the cursor is and how it looks. `color` with alpha 0 is the screen's foreground. It is drawn hollow while the terminal hasn't the keyboard; `blink` blinks it while it has (never with reduced motion), and a key press shows it again.

### `Term_Screen`

```odin
Term_Screen :: struct {
	cols, rows: int,
	cells:      []Term_Cell, // rows × cols, row after row
	row_rev:    []u64,       // when each row last changed (term_touch)
	rev:        u64,
	cursor:     Term_Cursor,
	foreground: Color,
	background: Color,
	allocator:  runtime.Allocator,
}
```

Term_Screen is what a terminal shows, owned by the app and edited in place, like a Document: fill its cells, then term_touch the rows you changed so the view draws them again. `foreground` is the default text colour (the cursor's, and an input method's composition); `background` is behind every cell whose own is none, and may be translucent (a window with `transparent` and a clear `clear` shows the desktop through it while the cells' own backgrounds stay opaque).

### `term_screen_resize`

```odin
term_screen_resize :: proc(s: ^Term_Screen, want_cols, want_rows: int, allocator := context.allocator)
```

term_screen_resize makes the screen `want_cols` × `want_rows`, keeping what fits of what it held (top left), with new cells empty. Every row counts as changed. The cells are allocated with `allocator` (the first call's, if later calls pass another); term_screen_destroy frees them.

### `term_screen_destroy`

```odin
term_screen_destroy :: proc(s: ^Term_Screen)
```

term_screen_destroy frees the screen's cells.

### `term_row`

```odin
term_row :: proc(s: ^Term_Screen, row: int) -> []Term_Cell
```

term_row is row `row`'s cells, an alias into the screen that the next term_screen_resize invalidates; nil for a row it hasn't.

### `term_cell_set`

```odin
term_cell_set :: proc(c: ^Term_Cell, text: string)
```

term_cell_set puts `text` (one grapheme cluster: at most TERM_CELL_RUNES code points are kept) into a cell's `text`.

### `term_touch`

```odin
term_touch :: proc(s: ^Term_Screen, row: int)
```

term_touch says row `row` changed: the view draws it again, and it is the compositor's damage.

### `term_touch_all`

```odin
term_touch_all :: proc(s: ^Term_Screen)
```

term_touch_all says every row changed (the screen scrolled, the app showed its scrollback).

### `Cell_Metrics`

```odin
Cell_Metrics :: struct {
	advance, line_height, ascent: f32,
}
```

Cell_Metrics is the size of a cell in logical px, already on device pixels: `advance` wide, `line_height` tall, the baseline `ascent` below its top. The grid's columns are multiples of `advance` from its left.

### `Term_Size`

```odin
Term_Size :: struct {
	cols, rows: int,
	cell:       Cell_Metrics,
	px:         [2]int,
}
```

Term_Size is the grid a terminal's node holds, sent to `on_resize` when it changes (the pty is resized to match): `cols` × `rows` whole cells, the cell's metrics, and `px`, the grid in device pixels (a pty's ws_xpixel, ws_ypixel).

### `Term_Key`

```odin
Term_Key :: struct {
	key:    Key,
	keysym: u32,
	char:   rune,
	mods:   Mods,
	repeat: bool,
	text:   string,
}
```

Term_Key is a key pressed (or repeating) while the terminal has the keyboard. `key` is the portable key (Unknown for one Baldr has no name for); `keysym` is the platform's own code, an xkb keysym on Linux, which tells the keypad's keys and dead keys apart; `char` is the character the key makes with the layout and Shift, Ctrl and Alt aside (Ctrl+C: 'c', Alt+Shift+A: 'A'; 0 for none); `text` is what it types — "" with Ctrl, Alt or Super held, for a key that types nothing (an arrow, Enter, a dead key), and never a control character. `text` lives while the message is handled: clone it to keep it.

### `terminal`

```odin
terminal :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), screen: ^Term_Screen, on_key: Handler(Msg, Term_Key) = nil, on_text: Handler(Msg, string) = nil,
	on_resize: Handler(Msg, Term_Size) = nil, family: string = "", size: f32 = 0, padding: f32 = 0, width: f32 = 0, height: f32 = 0,
	flex: f32 = 1, label: string = "", disabled := false, tag: u64 = 0, key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

terminal shows `screen` in `family` at `size` (logical px, scaled by the desktop's text size; 0: the theme's body size). The family is found by name among the machine's fonts ("JetBrains Mono"); without it, and for an empty name, the toolkit's own monospace face draws. Bold is the family's bold face.  While it has the keyboard, `on_key` hears every key the app's shortcuts don't take (see Term_Key) and `on_text` what an input method commits. `on_resize` hears the grid its node holds, when that changes — at first, on a resize, when the scale or the font changes; the app resizes its pty and its screen to it (a screen of another size is drawn from the top left, clipped). `padding` is a margin in logical px on every side, in the screen's background (one fill with the grid's, so a translucent one is even): the grid starts inside it and the cells counted are those that fit inside it. Pointer samples, the wheel and drops still measure from the node's top left, so subtract `padding` to find a cell. It takes `flex` of its parent's room (1: all of it) and is at least one cell and its margin. `label` names it for a reader (its title).  baldr.terminal(ui, &s.screen, on_key = key_to_pty, on_text = text_to_pty, on_resize = resize_pty, family = s.conf.font, size = s.conf.size, label = s.title)

### `cell_metrics`

```odin
cell_metrics :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), family: string = "", size: f32 = 0) -> Cell_Metrics
```

cell_metrics is the cell of `family` at `size` (as `terminal` takes them) on this surface: its scale puts the cell on device pixels. An app sizes a window in cells with it (80 × 24 before the first on_resize).

## widget_typeset_view.odin

typeset_view: a range of a laid-out paragraph's lines, drawn in the UI — a book reader's page.

A page is the end of one paragraph, some whole ones, and the start of another. The app lays each paragraph out once with `typeset` (lines with byte ranges, heights and baselines), decides where the page breaks, and builds one of these per paragraph on the page, naming which lines to draw.

Nothing is laid out here: a type-size change re-lays the chapter once, and turning pages costs only drawing. Each glyph is rasterised at its run's size in device pixels, so the page is crisp at any scale.

The Typeset belongs to the app and must outlive every build that names it: the node holds a pointer, not a copy, and so does the screen reader's text. Lay out in `update`, keep it in the app's state, and `typeset_destroy` it when the chapter or the type size changes.

```odin
// the lines of this paragraph that fall on this page
baldr.typeset_view(ui, &s.paras[i].ts, first = 4, count = 9)
```

### `typeset_view`

```odin
typeset_view :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), ts: ^Typeset, first: int = 0, count: int = -1,
	ink: Color = {}, label: string = "", width: f32 = 0, flex: f32 = 0,
	key: u64 = 0, loc := #caller_location) -> Node
```

typeset_view draws lines `first ..< first + count` of `ts` (a `count` of -1, the default, is the rest of them). The node is as wide as the Typeset's box and as tall as the lines it draws, so a column of them stacks into a page with the paragraph spacing the app puts between them.  `ink` is what text is drawn in — the theme's own text colour when it is left out. A run that carries its own `ink` keeps it, which is how a link, dimmed text or a sepia theme's colours reach the page; a run whose ink has no alpha takes this one.  `label` names the node for a screen reader; the words themselves are its accessible text, the paragraph's source over those lines, so a word broken across a line is still one word and the drawn hyphen is not in it.

### `typeset_view_line_at`

```odin
typeset_view_line_at :: proc(ts: ^Typeset, first, count: int, y: f32) -> int
```

typeset_view_line_at is the line of `ts` under `y`, measured from the top of the range a typeset_view drew, or -1 past its end. It is what turns a click on a page into a place in the text, together with `typeset_hit`.

## zoom.odin

Zoom: Ctrl + wheel over a node that asked for it. Tables and icon views take `on_zoom` in their builders (a file manager's icon size and row density); any other node gets it through `zoomable`. The handler's value is the wheel travel in logical px, positive to zoom in (one notch is 48); the app scales its tile, row height or image by it. Ctrl++ and Ctrl+- are the app's `shortcut`s.

### `zoomable`

```odin
zoomable :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), n: Node, on_zoom: Handler(Msg, f32))
```

zoomable makes `n` report Ctrl + wheel through `on_zoom`.  pic := baldr.image(ui, &s.photo, width = s.zoom * 400) baldr.zoomable(ui, pic, proc(e: baldr.Ev(f32)) -> Msg { return Zoom{e.value} })

### `zoom_event`

```odin
zoom_event :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), pos: [2]f32, delta: [2]f32) -> bool
```

zoom_event routes a Ctrl + wheel at `pos` to the nearest node with an on_zoom. Returns true when one took it.

## app_darwin.odin

### `default_backend`

```odin
default_backend :: proc() -> ^platform.Backend
```

default_backend is the Cocoa backend (platform/cocoa): an NSApplication, a window per surface, and the event pump translated into platform.Event. The renderer it feeds is Metal (gpu/metal.odin), not MoltenVK: the dependency rule allows `vendor:darwin/*` and no C compiled into the tree, and Metal is what the platform itself ships.

## app_linux.odin

### `default_backend`

```odin
default_backend :: proc() -> ^platform.Backend
```

default_backend picks the Linux backend.  Wayland when there is a Wayland display to connect to, X11 otherwise — the order every toolkit uses, because a Wayland session usually also runs Xwayland, and taking X there would give up per-surface scaling, the fractional-scale protocol and the shell protocols for nothing.  `BALDR_BACKEND` forces one: `wayland` or `x11`. An X11 session has no WAYLAND_DISPLAY, so it needs no variable; the override is for testing the X11 backend from inside a Wayland session.

## app_windows.odin

### `default_backend`

```odin
default_backend :: proc() -> ^platform.Backend
```

## drag_picture.odin

The picture a drag carries once the desktop has it.

Inside the window the ghost is a node in the overlay, drawn by the renderer. Outside, the compositor draws the drag's picture from a surface we hand over, so it needs pixels made before the drag leaves, and there is no GPU pass to make them with, nor a way to read one back. This file draws the chip on the CPU from the same pieces as the ghost's chip: the theme's surface colours, the source's icon, its name, and the count for a selection.

It is the chip, not the mirror: the in-window ghost's faded copy of the source (`paint_drag_mirror`) is an arbitrary subtree and cannot be drawn here. For a row that is an icon and a name the two look nearly the same.

Everything is in device pixels: a 2× window hands over a picture twice the size with `scale` 2, so it is sharp at its logical size.

### `DRAG_PICTURE_MAX_W`

```odin
DRAG_PICTURE_MAX_W :: f32(280)
```

DRAG_PICTURE_MAX_W is the widest the chip is drawn, in logical pixels — the same ceiling the ghost's chip has, so a long file name is cut in the same place inside the window and out of it.

### `DRAG_PICTURE_MAX_PX`

```odin
DRAG_PICTURE_MAX_PX :: i32(2048)
```

DRAG_PICTURE_MAX_PX is the most device pixels a side may have. A picture is small by nature; this is here so a silly scale cannot ask for a hundred megabytes of shared memory.

### `DRAG_PICTURE_PIC_MAX`

```odin
DRAG_PICTURE_PIC_MAX :: f32(48)
```

DRAG_PICTURE_PIC_MAX is the largest a source's own picture is drawn on the chip, in logical pixels: big enough to recognise a thumbnail, small enough that the chip stays a label beside the pointer.

## ime.odin

Input methods: typing Chinese, Japanese or Korean goes through an input method, which composes several keystrokes into text. The focused field tells it what it is, what is around the caret and where the caret is on screen; the method answers with a preedit — the composition in progress — text to commit, and text to delete around the caret.

A preedit is never in the Document and never in undo: it is shown at the caret, underlined, and replaced or cleared as the method sees fit. Only a commit edits the text, and it edits it exactly as typing does, so it joins the same undo group.

The platform installs `Tree.ime.hook` (app.odin, zwp_text_input_v3 on Wayland). Without one, everything here is inert and keys type directly.

### `Ime_Purpose`

```odin
Ime_Purpose :: enum u8 { Normal, Alpha, Digits, Number, Phone, Url, Email, Name, Password, Pin, Date, Time, Datetime, Terminal }
```

Ime_Purpose is what a field is for, so an input method can suit itself to it: digits for a PIN, no composing in a password.

### `Ime_Hint`

```odin
Ime_Hint :: enum u8 { Completion, Spellcheck, Auto_Capitalization, Lowercase, Uppercase, Titlecase, Hidden_Text, Sensitive_Data, Latin, Multiline }
```

Ime_Hint asks an input method for a behaviour where it has the choice.

### `Ime_Hints`

```odin
Ime_Hints :: bit_set[Ime_Hint; u16]
```

### `Ime_Cause`

```odin
Ime_Cause :: enum u8 { Input_Method, Other }
```

Ime_Cause says what moved the text last: the input method itself, or anything else (typing, a click, the app).

### `IME_SURROUNDING_MAX`

```odin
IME_SURROUNDING_MAX :: 2000
```

IME_SURROUNDING_MAX is how much text around the caret is sent.

### `Ime_Info`

```odin
Ime_Info :: struct {
	on:             bool,
	purpose:        Ime_Purpose,
	hints:          Ime_Hints,
	surrounding:    string,
	cursor, anchor: int,
	cause:          Ime_Cause,
	caret:          Rect,
}
```

Ime_Info is what the focused field tells the input method: whether it wants one, what it is for, the text around the caret (never the preedit) with the caret and the selection anchor as byte offsets into it, and the caret's rectangle in window coordinates, so the candidate window sits by it.

### `Input_Method`

```odin
Input_Method :: struct {
	user:    rawptr,
	surface: u32, // the window the field is on, as the platform knows it
	set:     proc(user: rawptr, surface: u32, info: Ime_Info),
}
```

Input_Method is the platform hook the runtime installs on the tree, the way it installs Clipboard. `set` is called when what the field tells the input method changes; the backend sends what moved.

### `Ime_Event`

```odin
Ime_Event :: struct {
	delete_before, delete_after: int,
	commit:                      string,
	preedit:                     string,
	cursor_begin, cursor_end:    int,
}
```

Ime_Event is one round of changes from the input method, applied in this order: the preedit shown now goes, `delete_before` bytes before the caret and `delete_after` after it are deleted, `commit` is inserted as typed, and `preedit` is shown at the caret with its cursor between `cursor_begin` and `cursor_end` (-1 and -1: no cursor of its own).

### `Ime`

```odin
Ime :: struct {
	hook:      Input_Method,
	node:      Node,   // the field showing a preedit (NODE_NONE: none)
	text:      string, // the preedit itself (tree allocator)
	lo, hi:    int,    // the input method's cursor inside `text`; both -1: hidden
	cause:     Ime_Cause,
	sent:      Ime_Info,
	sent_text: string, // sent.surrounding, ours
}
```

Ime is the tree's composition state: which field is composing, what it shows, and what was last told to the input method.

### `ime_apply`

```odin
ime_apply :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), ev: Ime_Event) -> bool
```

ime_apply performs one round from the input method on the focused field (see Ime_Event). It returns true when anything changed, so the runtime knows to draw. A field that takes no input methods ignores it.

```odin
inotify_reports_directory_entries_and_a_watched_file :: proc(t: ^testing.T)
```

## popup.odin

Popups from shell surfaces. On a .Layer surface, what the overlay root would draw beside an anchor (a popover, menu, submenu, select list, context menu or tooltip) gets a surface of its own, an xdg_popup on Wayland, so a 36 px bar can open a 570 px Control Centre. It stays in the same tree: the view builds it as always, layout sizes it free of the bar's bounds, and it sits where the compositor placed it; the bar's own drawing, hit tests and input region leave it out, and its surface draws only it. A press outside a menu or popover (the compositor's grab) sends its dismiss message; a tooltip takes no grab.

### `POPUP_BACKDROP_WAIT_MS`

```odin
POPUP_BACKDROP_WAIT_MS :: 100.0
```

POPUP_BACKDROP_WAIT_MS is the longest a glass popup's first paint waits for the compositor to measure what is behind it.

### `POPUP_SCREEN_MARGIN`

```odin
POPUP_SCREEN_MARGIN :: f32(8)
```

### `POPUP_PLACE_WAIT_MS`

```odin
POPUP_PLACE_WAIT_MS :: 50.0
```

POPUP_PLACE_WAIT_MS is the longest a popup's frame waits for the compositor to place it after a move.

## selection_paint.odin

What a text selection looks like, and the one way every widget draws it.

The selected words change colour with the band, as every desktop does: no wash over text that keeps its own colour can stand 3:1 off a dark surface and still leave light text 4.5:1 on top. The pair is the theme's own `primary` and `on_primary`; in all five built-in themes the band stands 4.22–11.18:1 off `surface` and `surface_high`, and the text reads 4.65–13.27:1 on the band. Where the accent cannot show (a fill that is the accent, like an outgoing bubble) the selection is reverse video: the band is the ink and the text the fill, which reads exactly as well as the unselected text did. `th.color.selection`, the wash, stays in the theme for an app that paints its own; nothing in the toolkit draws with it.

### `SELECTION_BAND_MIN`

```odin
SELECTION_BAND_MIN :: f32(3)
```

SELECTION_BAND_MIN is how far the band must stand off the surface it is on: WCAG's 3:1 for a change of state. SELECTION_TEXT_MIN is what the selected words must read at on the band: body text's 4.5:1.

### `SELECTION_TEXT_MIN`

```odin
SELECTION_TEXT_MIN :: f32(4.5)
```

### `selection_pair`

```odin
selection_pair :: proc(th: ^Theme, fill: Maybe(Color) = nil, ink: Maybe(Color) = nil) -> (band, text: Color)
```

selection_pair is the band a selection draws and the colour the words in it are drawn in, for text in `ink` on a surface `fill`.  Either may be nil: a widget on one of the theme's own surfaces knows neither, and gets the theme's pair — which stands off every neutral surface the theme has. One that knows its fill — a bubble — is checked against it. One that knows its ink and not its page — an editor on a coloured note — gets reverse video against the ink, because its ink is the one thing known to read on that page.

### `selection_overpaint`

```odin
selection_overpaint :: proc(t: ^Tree, l: ^Layer, from, to: int, rects: []Rect, band, text: Color, radius: f32 = 2)
```

selection_overpaint lays a selection over text a widget has already drawn: for each of `rects`, an opaque band and then, clipped to it, the text commands in `l.cmds[from:to]` again in `text`. Drawn over rather than under, so one routine serves every widget without any of them splitting a run at a selection; the band covers the old colour first, so none of it fringes the new, and whole runs are redrawn and cut by the clip, so their shaping is kept and a ligature half in the selection is half in each colour. Underlines and strikes (thin rects) come along recoloured; a code chip's wash or a highlighter stripe stays under the band. `paint_opacity` (a fading popover) applies to both colours.

## svg_draw.odin

SVG geometry: path data and basic shapes flattened to polylines in user space (curves within SVG_TOLERANCE device pixels), strokes built as outlines (joins, caps, dashes), and a scanline rasteriser giving each pixel its exact horizontal coverage over SVG_SUBROWS rows of samples, under the nonzero or evenodd rule. See svg_doc.odin.

### `SVG_TOLERANCE`

```odin
SVG_TOLERANCE :: 0.05 // how far a flattened curve may stray from the true one, in device pixels
```

### `SVG_SUBROWS`

```odin
SVG_SUBROWS :: 16 // coverage samples per pixel row
```

## terminal_draw.odin

Drawing a terminal's screen (widget_terminal.odin has the widget). Paint puts one Draw_Cells in the layer; render_draw turns it into GPU commands straight from the app's screen, row by row, keeping each row's commands in device pixels relative to the grid so an unchanged row costs a copy. Backgrounds of every row go first and text after, so a glyph that reaches into the next row is never covered by its background; the cursor and a composition are drawn fresh each frame.

### `Draw_Cells`

```odin
Draw_Cells :: struct { node: Node, pos: [2]f32, alpha: f32 }
```

Draw_Cells draws terminal `node`'s screen with its grid's top left at `pos`, at `alpha` (its fade).

## touch.odin

Touch: one finger drives the same controls the pointer does, with the gestures a phone user expects. A touch starts Pending; if it moves past TOUCH_SLOP it becomes a pan of the nearest container that scrolls along the dominant direction (with a fling on release), a slider drag when it started on a slider and moves sideways, or a plain press; a touch that lifts without moving is a tap (press + release through the pointer path, so buttons, tabs, options and text carets all work). Extra fingers are ignored. Hover never sticks after a touch.

### `TOUCH_SLOP`

```odin
TOUCH_SLOP :: f32(8)
```

### `Touch_Gesture`

```odin
Touch_Gesture :: enum u8 { None, Pending, Pan, Slider, Press, Drag }
```

### `TOUCH_LONG_PRESS_MS`

```odin
TOUCH_LONG_PRESS_MS :: 450.0 // a finger resting this long on a drag source lifts it (a plain move scrolls)
```

### `Touch_State`

struct

### `touch_down`

```odin
touch_down :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), id: i32, pos: [2]f32, now_ms: f64) -> (changed: bool)
```

### `touch_move`

```odin
touch_move :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), id: i32, pos: [2]f32, now_ms: f64) -> (changed: bool)
```

### `touch_up`

```odin
touch_up :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg), id: i32, now_ms: f64) -> (changed: bool)
```

### `touch_cancel`

```odin
touch_cancel :: proc(ui: ^Ui($Msg)) -> (changed: bool)
```

touch_cancel drops the sequence without activating anything.
